L32@P2-4: iiiAbstract
L33@P4: A physical machine for storage and manipulation of information, being physical, will always be subject
L34@P4: to noise and failure. For this reason, the design of fault-tolerant architectures is of prime importance
L35@P4: for building a working quantum computer. Quantum error correction codes offer a possible elegant
L36@P4: framework for fault-tolerance when provided with methods to operate qubits without corrupting the
L37@P4: information stored therein. This work specialises in hypergraph product (HGP) codes and seeks to lay
L38@P4: the groundwork for a quantum computer architecture based on them.
L39@P4: The leading approach to fault-tolerant quantum computation is, today, based on the planar code.
L40@P4: A planar-code-based quantum computer, however, would require dramatic qubit overhead and we
L41@P4: believe that good low-density parity-check (LDPC) codes are necessary to attain the full potential
L42@P4: of quantum computing. The HGP codes, of which the planar code is an instance, are not, strictly
L43@P4: speaking, good LDPC codes. Still, they are an efficient alternative. On the one hand, the best HGP
L44@P4: codes improve upon the planar code as they can store multiple logical qubits. On the other, they are
L45@P4: not considered good because their noise robustness is sub-optimal. Nonetheless, we see the design of
L46@P4: a HGP-based quantum computer as a bridge between the currently-favoured planar code design and
L47@P4: the gold standard of good LDPC codes. A HGP-based architecture would inform our knowledge on
L48@P4: how to design fault-tolerant protocols when a code stores multiple logical qubits, which is, to a large
L49@P4: extent, still an open question.
L50@P4: Our first original contribution is a decoding algorithm for all families of two-fold HGP codes. Second, we exhibit a constructive method to implement some logical encoded operations, given HGP codes
L51@P4: with particular symmetries. Last, we propose the concept of confinement as an essential characteristic
L52@P4: for a code family to be robust against syndrome measurement errors. Importantly, we show that both
L53@P4: expander and three-dimensional HGP codes have the desired confinement property.

L75@P8-10: viiviiiChapter 1
L76@P10: Context and notation - Introduction
L77@P10: Reliable exchange of information needs redundancy. Natural language is redundant and in fact, we
L78@P10: could write a sentence, remove all the vowels –‘th dg s n th grdn’ – or make a few typos – ‘teh dog is in
L79@P10: the graden’ – but still have a good chance that our message goes through. These are just two sides of the
L80@P10: same coin: we can encode information to compress it (source coding) or we can encode information to
L81@P10: protect it from errors (channel coding). Here we deal with the latter when the information is processed
L82@P10: by a quantum computer. Before turning to the quantum side, we briefly go over the key features of
L83@P10: channel coding for classical information processing in Section 1.1. We introduce the corresponding
L84@P10: concepts for quantum information processing in the remaining Sections 1.2 to 1.6.
L85@P10: 1.1 Bits
L86@P10: The basic unit of information is the bit, a two-state system, F2, whose possible values are {0, 1} [1, 2].
L87@P10: If a bit b has an equal probability of being in each state, the amount of information content in b is 1
L88@P10: shannon. Given a bit b in an unknown state, we can flip its value:
L89@P10: flip(b) =
L90@P10: 
L91@P10: 
L92@P10: 
L93@P10: 0 if b = 1
L94@P10: 1 if b = 0
L95@P10: ,
L96@P10: = b + 1 in F2.
L97@P10: The flip operation maps valid states of b into valid ones: it is a logical operation on the bit state. In
L98@P10: contrast, the operation vespa(b) = 🛵 is not a logical operation since the symbol 🛵 does not belong
L99@P10: to F2 and it is therefore not a valid state for the bit b.
L100@P10: Because a bit only has two possible valid states, the flip is the only reversible error a bit is subject
L101@P10: to. Nonetheless, if b undergoes a flip error with some probability p ∈ (0, 1), there is no way to infer
L102@P10: whether b has been erroneously flipped or not. Being a logical operation, the flip preserves valid states
L103@P10: and thus cannot be detected. With only one bit at our disposal, the knowledge that an error has
L104@P10: occurred with probability p is not enough: we need to add redundancy.
L105@P10: One way of adding redundancy is to use the classical repetition code. It encodes the logical bit b
L106@P10: into a register of n > 1 physical bits. For n = 3, the bit b is mapped into c = (c1 c2 c3) ∈ F
L107@P10: 3
L108@P10: 2 via the
L109@P10-11: 1linear map:
L110@P11: G : F2 =⇒ F
L111@P11: 3
L112@P11: 2
L113@P11: b 7−→
L114@P11: 
L115@P11: 
L116@P11: 1
L117@P11: 1
L118@P11: 1
L119@P11: 
L120@P11:  · b. (1.1)
L121@P11: The valid states for the register c are the ones in the image1 of G. The image of G is
L122@P11: im(G) = {(0 0 0),(1 1 1)}.
L123@P11: We call the vector space im(G) the codespace. The elements of the codespace are the codewords. The
L124@P11: logical flip operation on the codespace requires three single bit-flips:
L125@P11: flip(c) =
L126@P11: 
L127@P11: 
L128@P11: flip(c1)
L129@P11: flip(c2)
L130@P11: flip(c3)
L131@P11: 
L132@P11:  = c +
L133@P11: 
L134@P11: 
L135@P11: 1
L136@P11: 1
L137@P11: 1
L138@P11: 
L139@P11: 
L140@P11: ∈ F
L141@P11: 3
L142@P11: 2
L143@P11: .
L144@P11: One flip was previously enough to corrupt the information stored in b, but as many as three physical
L145@P11: flips are now necessary to corrupt an encoded state in im(G) and for the event to go undetected. If
L146@P11: the register c is found in a state with ci 6= cj for some i 6= j then at least an error has occurred: we
L147@P11: have detected that c belongs to the complement2 F
L148@P11: 3
L149@P11: 2 \im(G) of the codespace. Crucially, the codespace
L150@P11: im(G) is isomorphic to F2, the vector space describing the state space of a single bit. Despite that, the
L151@P11: logical flip in the codespace ‘costs’ three times the cost of a single bit-flip. We have added redundancy
L152@P11: and lowered the probability of undetectable errors – from p to p
L153@P11: 3
L154@P11: in this example.
L155@P11: The repetition code offers a working, albeit simple, example of classical code. However, its construction does not carry over to qubits as we illustrate in the following Sections. In Section 1.2, we review
L156@P11: some basic features of qubit systems. Our presentation, far from exhaustive, aims to highlight the
L157@P11: primary issues at the core of robust quantum information processing. The quantum repetition code is
L158@P11: presented in Section 1.2.1, and its shortcomings are pointed out. In Section 1.3 we briefly run through
L159@P11: classical linear codes as it is needed to understand their quantum counterpart, the stabiliser codes. We
L160@P11: outline the construction of the planar code – the quantum working equivalent of the repetition code –
L161@P11: in Section 1.4. We conclude this Chapter with Sections 1.5 and 1.6, where we discuss two concurrent
L162@P11: issues to quantum code construction for the design of a fault-tolerant computer architecture: logical
L163@P11: operations and noisy measurements.
L164@P11: 1.2 Qubits
L165@P11: A qubit is a two-dimensional Hilbert space, C
L166@P11: 2
L167@P11: , whose state is described by a unit vector [3, 4]. We
L168@P11: write |0i and |1i to indicate the standard basis of C
L169@P11: 2
L170@P11: , so that the state of a qubit q is written as
L171@P11: |φi = α |0i + β |1i, where the vector of coefficients (α, β)
L172@P11: T ∈ C2 has unit norm, |α|2 + |β|2 = 1.
L173@P11: 1The image of a function f : A −→ B, denoted im(f), is the set of all elements f(a) ∈ B, as a varies in the domain
L174@P11: A of f.
L175@P11: 2Given two sets A and B, the complement of B in A is the set of all elements of A that do not belong to B. The
L176@P11: complement is written A \ B.
L177@P11-12: 2The possible reversible operations on a qubit are unitary maps: U ∈ C
L178@P12: 2×2
L179@P12: such that UU† = 1. An
L180@P12: extensively used basis for the linear operators in C
L181@P12: 2×2
L182@P12: is the Pauli basis:
L183@P12: 1 =
L184@P12: (
L185@P12: 1 0
L186@P12: 0 1)
L187@P12: , X =
L188@P12: (
L189@P12: 0 1
L190@P12: 1 0)
L191@P12: , Y =
L192@P12: (
L193@P12: 0 −i
L194@P12: i 0
L195@P12: )
L196@P12: , Z =
L197@P12: (
L198@P12: 1 0
L199@P12: 0 −1
L200@P12: )
L201@P12: .
L202@P12: The Pauli operators X, Y, Z, together with ±i1, generate a multiplicative group which obeys the
L203@P12: product and commutativity relations:
L204@P12: X2 = Y
L205@P12: 2 = Z2 = 1, XY = iZ, Y Z = iX, ZX = iY,
L206@P12: and
L207@P12: XY = −Y X, Y Z = −Y Z, ZX = −XZ.
L208@P12: An n-qubit register has states that are vectors in (C
L209@P12: 2
L210@P12: )
L211@P12: ⊗n with standard basis {|vi s.t v ∈ Fn
L212@P12: 2 }.
L213@P12: The Pauli group on n qubits, Pn, is the group generated by n-fold tensor products of Pauli operators.
L214@P12: Given a Pauli operator P ∈ P1 ⊆ C
L215@P12: 2×2
L216@P12: , we write Pi for the operator in Pn ⊆ C
L217@P12: 2
L218@P12: n×2n
L219@P12: which is the
L220@P12: tensor product of P at position i and the identity elsewhere. We define the support of a Pauli operator
L221@P12: Pn as the set:
L222@P12: supp(P) = {i such that P = P1 ⊗ . . . ⊗ Pn and Pi 6= 1},
L223@P12: and its weight |P| as |P| = |supp(P)|. On (C
L224@P12: 2
L225@P12: )
L226@P12: ⊗n, the Pauli basis is the set:
L227@P12: {1n, Xi
L228@P12: , Zi, Yi s.t. 1 ≤ i ≤ n},
L229@P12: where 1n ∈ (C)
L230@P12: 2
L231@P12: n×2
L232@P12: n
L233@P12: is the identity matrix of size 2
L234@P12: n × 2n. Similarly to the one-qubit Pauli basis
L235@P12: operators in P1, Pauli basis operators in Pn square to the identity and any two of them either commute
L236@P12: or anti-commute.
L237@P12: Information on the qubits’ state is acquired via the measurement of observables, which are Hermitian operators A = A† ∈ C
L238@P12: 2
L239@P12: n×2n
L240@P12: . Measuring an observable collapses the state of the qubits onto
L241@P12: one of the eigenspaces of the measured observable. The possible outcomes are the eigenvalues of the
L242@P12: observable, observed with probability equal to the squared norm of the inner product3 between the
L243@P12: pre-measurement state of the qubits and the corresponding eigenvector. Note that outcomes and postmeasurement states for an observable are well defined. By the Spectral Theorem, every Hermitian
L244@P12: operator has an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors [5]. Furthermore, if A and B are both Hermitian
L245@P12: and commute, they have a common basis of eigenvectors and hence the measurement of one does not
L246@P12: alter the probability distribution of the outcome of the other. Commuting observables are compatible.
L247@P12: Importantly, if we knew that a qubit register is in one of two orthogonal states, we could measure
L248@P12: the corresponding observable – any Hermitian operator whose eigenvectors are the two orthogonal
L249@P12: states – and distinguish between the two orthogonal options with certainty. However, if we do not
L250@P12: have such a priori knowledge, measuring a qubit register would collapse its wave function and corrupt
L251@P12: the information stored therein. A possible solution to gather information on the state of a register
L252@P12: whilst preserving it is to ‘write’ that information on an auxiliary register of qubits, as we will now
L253@P12: 3The complex inner product is a map ⟨·, ·⟩ : C⊗n × C⊗n −→ C defined as ⟨v, w⟩ := v†w, where v†
L254@P12: is the conjugate
L255@P12: transpose of v.
L256@P12-13: 3explain. We refer to the qubit register we want to gather information about as the data register, and
L257@P13: to the auxiliary register as auxilia register4. Information on the data qubits can be transferred to the
L258@P13: auxilia register via the application of some entangling unitary operators on the composite data-auxilia
L259@P13: system. Via the appropriate choice of the entangling operation, we can assume that the desired information can be acquired from the destructive measurement results of the auxilia qubits. In the following
L260@P13: presentation, we will shortly refer to measurement outcomes of data qubits. By this, we always imply,
L261@P13: if not otherwise specified, that suitable auxilia qubits are freshly prepared for the task, appropriate
L262@P13: entangling operations between data and auxilia qubits are performed, the auxilia qubits are measured
L263@P13: and then reinitialized in a standard state, ready to be used again.
L264@P13: To model the manipulation of information stored in qubit registers, we use the quantum circuit model
L265@P13: of computation [3]. The quantum circuit model is based on the assumption that computation on a
L266@P13: qubit register can be reduced to:
L267@P13: (i) Register preparation in a initial state, conventionally |0i
L268@P13: ⊗n
L269@P13: .
L270@P13: (ii) Active computation via the application of unitary operators on the register for a finite amount
L271@P13: of time.
L272@P13: (iii) Results acquisition via the measurement of qubits in the Pauli Z basis.
L273@P13: Some observations are immediate. First, any realization of a quantum computer, a physical machine
L274@P13: that runs quantum circuits, will be able to perform only a finite set of operations in a finite number of
L275@P13: time steps. Second, if any unitary is a possible operation, then it is also a possible error on the qubit
L276@P13: register. Third, every component – state preparation, gates, measurements – is subject to noise.
L277@P13: The Solovay-Kitaev theorem [7, 3] resolves the first obstacle: even if unitaries on (C
L278@P13: 2
L279@P13: )
L280@P13: ⊗n are a continuous set, a finite number of gates is sufficient to efficiently implement any unitary to arbitrary precision.
L281@P13: More precisely Solovay-Kitaev states the following.
L282@P13: Consider a norm k·k over the unitaries on C
L283@P13: 2
L284@P13: . Let G be a finite set of unitaries, closed under
L285@P13: multiplicative inverse. Further assume that the multiplicative group hGi generated by G is
L286@P13: dense in the unitaries, so that for every unitary U and ε > 0 in R, there exists G ∈ hGi such
L287@P13: that kU − Gk < ε. Fix a precision ε > 0. Then every unitary can be approximated within
L288@P13: distance ε via a sequence of at most nϵ gates in G, where nε ∝ logc(
L289@P13: 1
L290@P13: ε
L291@P13: ) and 1 < c < 4.
L292@P13: We remark the importance of the poly-logarithmic scaling in the inverse-precision 1
L293@P13: ε
L294@P13: in Solovay-Kitaev’s
L295@P13: result. In fact, assume we want to implement m arbitrary unitaries, in m separate timesteps, on a
L296@P13: qubit and we only have access to the gates in G. We aim at precision ε > 0. Roughly, we will have
L297@P13: 4The most common term in the literature for auxilia qubits is ancilla qubits. However, the Latin term ancilla means
L298@P13: maidservant and is therefore intrinsically misogynist. For this reason, the community started using alternative terms
L299@P13: such as measurement or syndrome qubits. I believe that the term ‘measurement qubits’ is biased towards the circuit
L300@P13: model of computation as opposed to the measurement-based model [6]; whilst the term ‘syndrome qubits’ fails to cover
L301@P13: the different usages of ancilla qubits, which go beyond the syndrome extraction function. As such, I have looked for a
L302@P13: gender-neutral term. Auxilium in Latin means help, aid. I warmly invite the reader to adopt the phrasing auxilia qubits,
L303@P13: as I firmly believe that changing the words we use is the first step towards shaping our thought, raising awareness and
L304@P13: overcoming (more or less) implicit biases.
L305@P13-14: 4to implement each of the m unitaries to precision m/ε. By the Solovay-Kitaev’s theorem, the total
L306@P14: time cost scales as m · logc(
L307@P14: m
L308@P14: ε
L309@P14: ), with a total scale factor only poly-logarithmic. Keeping time overhead
L310@P14: under control is paramount for the realizations of faithful quantum computations and Solovay-Kitav’s
L311@P14: theorem ensures that this is possible for single-qubit unitary operations. Conveniently, it can be shown
L312@P14: that single-qubit unitaries and controlled-NOT, CNOT, gates

L328@P14: there exists finite sets that can efficiently approximate any unitary operation up to arbitrary precision.
L329@P14: We call any such finite set universal. Infinitely many universal gate sets exist, our preferred one is the
L330@P14: Clifford+T set. The Clifford group Cn on (C

L361@P14: The T gate grants universality to the set Clifford+T and therefore we have solved the first issue: a
L362@P14: quantum computer that implements Clifford+T gates is computationally universal.
L363@P14: Before turning to the second issue, namely that possible errors are a continuum, we remark some key
L364@P14: feature of the Clifford group. The Clifford group is not universal because the set generated by {S, H}
L365@P14: is not dense in the set of single-qubit unitaries – whilst {T, H} generates a dense set. Furthermore,
L366@P14: as per the Gottesman-Knill theorem, a circuit made of state preparation, Clifford operations only
L367@P14: and measurements, can be simulated in polynomial time on a probabilistic classical computer [8].
L368@P14: Conjugation via Clifford permutes the Pauli and, in fact, the Clifford group can be alternatively
L369@P14: defined as:
L370@P14: Cn = {U unitary s.t. UP U† ∈ Pn for all P ∈ Pn}.
L371@P14: Every unitary operator can be written as a linear combination of the Pauli and therefore any operator
L372@P14: can be characterized, up to global phase, by tracking its action on the Pauli. Hoping that the quantum
L373@P14: circuit model strictly contains the classical circuit model, Clifford and Pauli are not sufficient to fully
L374@P14: express the quantum circuit model of computation – still, we can use them to define a self-contained
L375@P14: theory of error correction, as the discussion below and Section 1.3 explain.
L376@P14: Since any unitary is a possible operation, it also constitutes a possible error. Conveniently though
L377@P14: we can write a generic unitary U on C
L378@P14: 2 as:
L379@P14: U = a11 + axX + azZ + ayY,
L380@P14-15: 5for some complex coefficients a1, ax, ay, az. Consider a n qubit register, |φ, αi where |φi ∈ C
L381@P15: 2
L382@P15: represent
L383@P15: the state of the data qubit and |αi ∈ (C
L384@P15: 2
L385@P15: )
L386@P15: ⊗n−1
L387@P15: represent the state of a n − 1 auxilia register. Let
L388@P15: P = 1, X1, Z1, Y1 be a Pauli operator with support on the data qubit and suppose there exists a unitary
L389@P15: operator O on (C
L390@P15: 2
L391@P15: )
L392@P15: ⊗n such that, for some initial state |α0i of the auxilia register,
L393@P15: O (P |φ, α0i) = |P φ, αP i, (1.2)
L394@P15: where |αP i = |α1i, |αX1
L395@P15: i, |αZ1i, |αY1i are orthogonal in (C
L396@P15: 2
L397@P15: )
L398@P15: ⊗n−1
L399@P15: . Since orthogonal states can be
L400@P15: distinguished via appropriate measurements, we can identify in which of the product states on the
L401@P15: right-hand side of Eq. (1.2) the data-auxilia qubit register is in. By linearity, if U acts on the data
L402@P15: qubit, Eq. (1.2) yields:
L403@P15: O(U |φ, α0i) = a1 |φ, α1i + ax |X1φ, αX1i + az |Z1φ, αZ1i + ay |Y1φ, αY1i.
L404@P15: Provided that such a unitary operator O does exist, and upon appropriate measurement of the auxilia
L405@P15: qubits, the post-measurement state of the n-qubit register is |P φ, αP i with probability |ap|
L406@P15: 2
L407@P15: . The
L408@P15: original state of the data qubit |φi can thus be restored by applying the correction operator P to the
L409@P15: register. Ergo, if we were able to detect and correct for Z and X errors we could correct for all errors
L410@P15: – Y errors corresponding to the concurrent event of an X and a Z error. We refer to this phenomenon
L411@P15: as error discretization [3].
L412@P15: To showcase error discretization, we have made a very special assumption: namely that, given a
L413@P15: n qubit register, errors could occur only on the first qubit of the register. Nonetheless, our analysis
L414@P15: serves well to demonstrate:
L415@P15: (i) Even if measurements destroy superposition, not every superposition stores quantum information:
L416@P15: the state of the data qubit |φi is preserved when measuring the auxilia register |αP i.
L417@P15: (ii) Even if the amount of information gained in a measurement is finite, measurements can be used
L418@P15: to correct a continuous set of possible errors.
L419@P15: Error discretization is a key idea in quantum error correction and it remains a faithful assumption
L420@P15: when errors are local and occur independently on each qubit of a register i.e. local stochastic noise
L421@P15: model, see Eq. (1.8) in Section 1.3. Under such a premise, we can model errors occurring at any stage
L422@P15: of the circuit model of computation and therefore also solve the third of our issues. Specifically, each
L423@P15: gate of a circuit could fail and propagate errors (circuit level noise) and measurements result could be
L424@P15: faulty too (measurement errors). We refer the reader to Sections 1.5 to 1.6 and [3] for a more detailed
L425@P15: discussion on these matters and conclude this Section by exhibiting a toy example of quantum code.
L426@P15: 1.2.1 The quantum repetition code
L427@P15: Mirroring the classical repetition code, we encode the logical qubit q in a 3-qubit register qr via the
L428@P15: unitary maps that on the standard basis act as:
L429@P15: Gr : C
L430@P15: 2 −→ (C2
L431@P15: )
L432@P15: ⊗3
L433@P15: |0i 7−→ |000i, (1.3)
L434@P15: |1i 7−→ |111i.
L435@P15-16: 6With such encoding, the generic state in the quantum repetition codespace im(Gr) ' C
L436@P16: 2
L437@P16: spanned by
L438@P16: {|000i, |111i} is:
L439@P16: |ψi
L440@P16: r = α |000i + β |111i. (1.4)
L441@P16: A valid choice5for the Pauli operators on the codespace is described by the group homomorphism γ
L442@P16: induced by:
L443@P16: Nr
L444@P16: γ
L445@P16: −→ P1 (1.5)
L446@P16: Z1Z2 7−→ 1,
L447@P16: Z2Z3 7−→ 1,
L448@P16: X1X2X3 7−→ X,
L449@P16: Z1 7−→ Z.
L450@P16: where Nr is the group generated by:
L451@P16: {i1, Z1Z2, Z2Z3, X1X2X3, Z1}.
L452@P16: The homomorphism γ is well-defined: its kernel6,
L453@P16: Sr := ker(γ) = hZ1Z2, Z2Z3 i,
L454@P16: is a normal subgroup of Nr and the pre-images7 of X and Z anti-commute.
L455@P16: We have added degeneracy: all the elements in Sr act as the identity on the generic encoded
L456@P16: state |ψi
L457@P16: r ∈ (C
L458@P16: 2
L459@P16: )
L460@P16: ⊗3
L461@P16: e.g. Z1Z2 |ψi
L462@P16: r = |ψir
L463@P16: . Accordingly, every Pauli in the pre-image γ
L464@P16: −1
L465@P16: (X) of X
L466@P16: has the same action on the encoded state |ψi
L467@P16: r
L468@P16: as the Pauli X has on the generic one-qubit state
L469@P16: |φi = α |0i + β |1i. Similarly for γ
L470@P16: −1
L471@P16: (Z). For example, the logical Pauli Z on |ψi
L472@P16: r
L473@P16: can equivalently
L474@P16: be implemented as Z1 |ψi
L475@P16: r
L476@P16: or Z3 |ψi
L477@P16: r
L478@P16: . So, if for some reason we do not have access to the first qubit
L479@P16: of the register qr, we could still perform a Z operation on the logical qubit of the quantum repetition
L480@P16: code applying Z3. Yet, degeneracy is not the same as redundancy. On the one hand, the Pauli X on
L481@P16: q is mapped to a weight three Pauli operator on qr. On the other, the minimum weight of an encoded
L482@P16: Z is not increased. Because of this imbalance, the quantum repetition code is resilient to X errors but
L483@P16: sensitive to Z errors, as we now explain.
L484@P16: The operators in Sr have trivial action on the codespace and any error-free state |ψi
L485@P16: r
L486@P16: is their common +1 eigenstate. If the data register q3 yields the result −1 upon measurement of Z1Z2 or Z2Z3,
L487@P16: then at least one error has occurred and the register q3 is in a state in C
L488@P16: 2 \ im(Gr). However, measuring Z1Z2 and Z2Z3 detects Pauli X errors of weight at most two, but cannot discern any difference
L489@P16: between Z errors and valid states in the codespace. Because of degeneracy, all Pauli Z operators in
L490@P16: P3 of weight one or three act as the logical Z operator on the encoded state |ψi
L491@P16: r
L492@P16: and all the weight
L493@P16: two have trivial action. In other words, the action of Z operators is either a valid logical operation or
L494@P16: trivial and therefore undetectable. If errors on each qubit are independent and one error occurs with
L495@P16: 5The more expert reader will recognise Z1Z2, Z2Z3 and X1X2X3, Z1 as the stabilisers and the logical Pauli operators
L496@P16: of the quantum repetition code. We decided to postpone the introduction of such terminology to Section 1.3 to illustrate
L497@P16: to the less expert reader the group-theoretic essence of code construction.
L498@P16: 6The kernel of a map f : A −→ B, denoted ker(f), is the set of all elements a ∈ A such that f(a) = 0 ∈ B.
L499@P16: 7The pre-image of a map f : A → B, denoted f−1
L500@P16: (b) for b ∈ B, is the set of all elements a ∈ A such that f(a) = b.
L501@P16-17: 7probability p, encoding via the quantum repetition code decreases the probability of undetectable X
L502@P17: errors from p to p
L503@P17: 3
L504@P17: , but increases the probability of undetectable Z error from p to 3p(1 − p)
L505@P17: 2 + p3 –
L506@P17: so we have worsened it by a leading factor of three in p.
L507@P17: Universal computation and discretization of errors are expressions of the dual nature of the Pauli
L508@P17: operators since, being both unitary and Hermitian, they serve as operations as well as observables to
L509@P17: be measured. In addition to this, Pauli operators can define a self-contained theory of error correction.
L510@P17: The need to protect from both X- and Z-type errors, and to aggregate equivalent (i.e. degenerate)
L511@P17: ones, shows that a simple translation of what has been done for bits is not enough to protect a logical
L512@P17: qubit from noise. Yet, degeneracy and redundancy are fundamental attributes of robust qubit encoding
L513@P17: – as Section 1.3 and Section 1.4 specify.
L514@P17: 1.3 Classical linear codes and their quantum counterpart: stabiliser
L515@P17: and CSS codes
L516@P17: A classical linear code is a vector subspace of F
L517@P17: n
L518@P17: 2 defined as the kernel of a linear map σ : F
L519@P17: n
L520@P17: 2 −→ F
L521@P17: m
L522@P17: 2
L523@P17: .
L524@P17: We call the subspace codespace and its elements codewords. With this terminology, the defining
L525@P17: features of the syndrome map is that it yields the all-zeroes vector only when it acts on a codeword.
L526@P17: Given a matrix representation H ∈ F
L527@P17: m×n
L528@P17: 2
L529@P17: of σ, we say that the associated code has length n, dimension
L530@P17: k and distance d if:
L531@P17: k = dim(ker H) = n − rank(H) and min
L532@P17: v∈ker H,
L533@P17: v̸=0
L534@P17: |v| = d,
L535@P17: where |v| is the Hamming weight of the binary vector v:
L536@P17: |v| = |{vi 6= 0, where v = (v1, . . . , vn) ∈ F
L537@P17: n
L538@P17: 2 }|.
L539@P17: We say that H is a parity-check matrix for the code and call its rows checks. A code is a (r, c)
L540@P17: low-density parity-check (LDPC) code if the rows and columns of H have weight at most r and c
L541@P17: respectively. We will shortly say that the code is (r, c)-LDPC and has parameters [n, k, d].
L542@P17: For instance, a parity check matrix for the classical repetition code in Section 1.1 is:
L543@P17: H3 =
L544@P17: (
L545@P17: 1 1 0
L546@P17: 0 1 1)
L547@P17: .
L548@P17: The parity-check matrix H3 defines a (2, 2)-LDPC code of parameters [3, 1, 3]. See Fig. 1.
L549@P17: Figure 1: The classical repetition code of parameters [7, 1, 7]. Bits are placed on edges and checks are
L550@P17: on circles. Each circle/check evaluates that the two edges incident to it have the same parity. Wiggly
L551@P17: lines represent flip errors and red circles highlight the checks whose incident edges have different parity
L552@P17: i.e. red circles represent the support of the syndrome. We note how flip errors on consecutive edges
L553@P17: yield a weight two syndrome, with checks located at the boundaries of the error.
L554@P17-18: 8Given a parity check matrix H ∈ F
L555@P18: m×n
L556@P18: 2
L557@P18: of a [n, k, d] code, any error e ∈ F
L558@P18: n
L559@P18: 2 of weight strictly less
L560@P18: than d can be detected. Namely, by construction, H(c + e) 6= 0 ∈ F
L561@P18: m
L562@P18: 2
L563@P18: for any codeword c ∈ ker H. In
L564@P18: particular, the value of the syndrome map is determined by the error vector e and it is independent
L565@P18: of the codeword c. The optimal decoder for a code of distance d corrects all errors of weight at most
L566@P18: t = b
L567@P18: d−1
L568@P18: 2
L569@P18: c because for every c ∈ ker H the ball of radius t centred in it does not contain any other
L570@P18: vectors in ker H.
L571@P18: In the following, we consider a local stochastic noise model of parameter p ∈ (0, 1), where the
L572@P18: probability of an error e to occur is:
L573@P18: P(e) = p
L574@P18: |e|
L575@P18: (1 − p)
L576@P18: n−|e|
L577@P18: . (1.6)
L578@P18: Eq. (1.6) signifies that single bit-flips are independent and the probability of an error occurring depends
L579@P18: only on its weight. Since P(e) is monotonically decreasing for noise rates p < 1
L580@P18: 2
L581@P18: , minimum weight errors
L582@P18: are the most likely in this regime. The maximum-likelihood decoder (ML decoder ) for p < 1/2 and
L583@P18: P(e) as in Eq. (1.6) is D:
L584@P18: D : im(σ) −→ F
L585@P18: n
L586@P18: 2
L587@P18: ,
L588@P18: s 7−→ arg max
L589@P18: e
L590@P18: P (e | σ(e) = s). (1.7)
L591@P18: The decoder D outputs the most likely error so that, for a bit register in the state m ∈ F
L592@P18: n
L593@P18: 2
L594@P18: , the recovered state is m + D(σ(m)). For example, the maximum likelihood error recovering scheme for the
L595@P18: repetition code under local stochastic noise is based on a majority vote strategy: if for some m ∈ F
L596@P18: 3
L597@P18: 2
L598@P18: (or F
L599@P18: n
L600@P18: 2
L601@P18: in the general case) a non-zero syndrome is detected, flipping the minority bit (or bits) restores
L602@P18: the parity of the register. In general, assuming local stochastic noise, the ML decoder is optimal.
L603@P18: The quantum counterpart of linear codes are stabiliser codes, first introduced in [9]. A stabiliser
L604@P18: code is a subspace of (C
L605@P18: 2
L606@P18: )
L607@P18: ⊗n defined as the +1 common eigenspace of the stabiliser group, an Abelian
L608@P18: subgroup S of the Pauli operators in Pn such that −1 6∈ S. As for classical codes, we call this subspace
L609@P18: codespace and its elements the codewords. We denote by C(S) the codespace defined by the stabiliser
L610@P18: group S,
L611@P18: C(S) = {|ψi s.t. S |ψi = |ψi ∀S ∈ S}.
L612@P18: Concretely, the group Sr introduced in Section 1.2.1 is a stabiliser group and C(Sr) is the subspace
L613@P18: generated by |000i and |111i in (C
L614@P18: 2
L615@P18: )
L616@P18: ⊗3
L617@P18: . Because Tr(X) = Tr(Y ) = Tr(Z) = 0, for P = P1 ⊗ . . . Pn ∈
L618@P18: Pn, it holds:
L619@P18: Tr(P) = ∏
L620@P18: i
L621@P18: Tr(Pi) = 0.
L622@P18: Combining P
L623@P18: 2 = 1, Tr(P) = 0 and the Spectral Theorem, we find that each Pauli P ∈ Pn has exactly
L624@P18: 2
L625@P18: n−1
L626@P18: independent +1 eigenvectors, 2
L627@P18: n−1
L628@P18: independent −1 eigenvectors, and that the dimension of the
L629@P18: common +1 eigenspace of m independent Pauli operators in Pn is k = n − m. For the quantum
L630@P18: repetition code, {Z1Z2, Z2Z3} is a minimal set of generators for the stabiliser group and k = 1.
L631@P18: Given a set S1, . . . , Sm ∈ S of generators for the stabiliser group, they uniquely define a syndrome
L632@P18: map:
L633@P18: σ : Pn −→ F
L634@P18: m
L635@P18: 2
L636@P18: E 7−→ (s1, . . . , sm),
L637@P18-19: 9where si = 0 if and only if ESi = SiE. The kernel of the syndrome map is the normaliser of S in Pn,
L638@P19: denoted N (S). The normaliser is the set of Pauli operators that commutes with all the stabilisers.
L639@P19: Because S is normal in N (S), the quotient8:
L640@P19: L := N(S)/S,
L641@P19: is well-defined. The Pauli subgroup L is referred to as the logical Pauli group. If the stabiliser group
L642@P19: has m generators, L is isomorphic to Pk, where k = n − m and the codespace C(S) is isomorphic to
L643@P19: (C
L644@P19: 2
L645@P19: )
L646@P19: ⊗k
L647@P19: . The normaliser for the quantum repetition code is the group Nr introduced in Eq. (1.5) and
L648@P19: generated by {1, Z1Z2, Z2Z3, X1X2X3, Z1}. With a little abuse of notation, we write L ∈ L to indicate
L649@P19: any representative L in its equivalence class [L]. Explicitly:
L650@P19: L ∈ [L] = {LS such that S ∈ S}.
L651@P19: For each [L] 6= [1] in L, there exists by construction at least one state |ψi ∈ C(S) such that L|ψi 6= |ψi.
L652@P19: Most importantly, elements in the same equivalence class in the logical Pauli group yield the same
L653@P19: action on the codespace:
L654@P19: L|ψi = L
L655@P19: ′
L656@P19: |ψi, for each L, L′ ∈ [L] and |ψi ∈ C(S).
L657@P19: The logical Pauli group of the classical repetition code is generated by {Z1, X1X2X3}.
L658@P19: The distance d of C(S) is the minimum weight of any non-trivial logical operator in L. Briefly we
L659@P19: say that the code C(S) is [[n, k, d]]. If the stabiliser generators have all weight at most r and every qubit
L660@P19: in (C
L661@P19: 2
L662@P19: )
L663@P19: ⊗n is involved in at most c stabiliser measurements we say that the code is a (r, c) quantum
L664@P19: LDPC code (qLDPC). The quantum repetition code is (2, 2)-qLDPC code of parameters [[3, 1, 1]].
L665@P19: The measurement of the stabiliser operators detects all errors in Pn \ N (S). In particular all errors
L666@P19: of weight less than d but the one that have trivial action on the codespace, namely Pauli operators in
L667@P19: the stabiliser group. The optimal decoder for a distance d code can correct non-trivial Pauli errors up
L668@P19: to weight t = b
L669@P19: d−1
L670@P19: 2
L671@P19: c.
L672@P19: The definition of a quantum analogue of a maximum-likelihood decoder is subtle, because of the
L673@P19: degeneracy of the syndrome map. Let |ψiS ∈ C(S) be an encoded state for some non-trivial stabiliser
L674@P19: code with stabiliser group S and let E ∈ Pn \ N (S) be a correctable error. Suppose that the encoded
L675@P19: state |ψiSundergoes the error E. Consider a decoder that, on input σ(E), outputs F with σ(F) =
L676@P19: σ(E). Upon recovery, the state F E |ψiS ∈ C(S) is restored. The following holds:
L677@P19: ker(σ) = N (S) =⇒ F = EL, for some L ∈ N (S).
L678@P19: If L ∈ S, then L is the identity on the codespace and the restored state is equivalent to the original
L679@P19: one. If instead L ∈ N (S) \ S, then L is a non-trivial Pauli operator on the encoded state |ψiSand in
L680@P19: particular |ψiS6= L|ψiSin C(S). Ultimately, the original state is restored if the decoder outputs any
L681@P19: of the Pauli operators equivalent to the original error E, namely operators in the set ΣE:
L682@P19: ΣE = {SE such that S ∈ S}.
L683@P19: 8A quotient group A/B is well defined if B ⊆ A is normal in A, meaning that aba−1 ∈ B for every a in A and b in
L684@P19: B. Given a quotient group (A/B, ·), we indicate by [a] its elements. The elements of a quotient group are equivalence
L685@P19: classes: [a] = {ab such that b ∈ B}.
L686@P19-20: 10Shortly:
L687@P20: σ(F) = σ(EL) = σ(E)
L688@P20: but

L707@P20: In other words, when Pauli errors on each qubit are independent and identically distributed so that
L708@P20: low-weight errors are more likely, the code’s degeneracy gives rise to a tension between the most
L709@P20: probable error – namely the minimum weight one that agrees with the syndrome – and the number of
L710@P20: different error configurations equivalent to it. An equivalence class that happens to contain multiple
L711@P20: low-weight errors could end up having a greater overall probability than the equivalence class of the
L712@P20: minimum weight error.
L713@P20: The qML decoder is optimal but the summation in Eq. (1.9) contains exponentially many terms:
L714@P20: 2
L715@P20: n−k
L716@P20: for a [[n, k, d]] code. A computationally easier algorithm is the quantum minimum-weight decoder
L717@P20: (qMW decoder):
L718@P20: DMW : im(σ) −→ Pn
L719@P20: s 7−→ arg max

L722@P20: In general, qML and qMW decoders could yield different answers but for sufficiently low physical error
L723@P20: probability p, it can be shown that they are equivalent [10]. The classical ML decoder instead is a
L724@P20: minimum-weight decoder: degeneracy is exquisitely quantum.

L793@P21: A stabiliser code is a Calderbank-Shor-Steane code (CSS code) whenever the stabiliser group has
L794@P21: a set of generators that can be partitioned in a set Sx of X operators and a set Sz of Z operators [11,
L795@P21: 12]. In the following we describe CSS codes borrowing from the language of algebraic topology and
L796@P21: using chain complexes, see [13]. We believe that the chain complex perspective can not only enlighten
L797@P21: our understanding of CSS codes, but also inspires new code constructions, as it has been demonstrated
L798@P21: several times already [14].
L799@P21: We can associate a length-2 chain complex over F2 to any CSS code and, vice-versa, to any chain
L800@P21: complex of length at least two we can associate a CSS code. A length-ℓ chain complex is an object
L801@P21: described by a sequence of ℓ+ 1 vector spaces {Ci}i over F2 and ℓ linear operators δi: Ci → Ci+1 such
L802@P21: that, for each i, δi+1δi = 0. Given a chain complex:
L803@P21: C0
L804@P21: δ0 −→ C1δ1 −→ C2

L883@P22: For CSS codes, we define the X and Z distances, dx and dz, as the minimum weight of a representative
L884@P22: of a non-trivial cycle and co-cycle. The distance of the code is the minimum between the two. For
L885@P22: instance, the quantum repetition code is a CSS codes with dx = 3 and dz = 1 and hence it has distance
L886@P22: 1. We show in Section 1.4 how to build a CSS code with both dx > 1 and dz > 1.
L887@P22: 1.4 The planar code
L888@P22: In Section 1.3 we introduced the quantum repetition code and pointed out how it fails to protect
L889@P22: the encoded logical qubit from arbitrary errors. In this Section, we briefly review the planar code,
L890@P22: a quantum code which represents the proper quantum version of the classical repetition code. The
L891@P22: planar code was first introduced in [15, 16] as an adaption to the two-dimensional planar geometry
L892@P22: of Kitaev’s seminal work on the toric code [7, 17]. Not only is the planar code the currently leading
L893@P22: candidate in quantum error correction [18] but it is also an hypergraph product code – the main object
L894@P22: of our studies in Chapters 3 to 5.
L895@P22: We imagine qubits placed on the edges of a ℓ × (ℓ − 1) square lattice L and refer to this n-qubit
L896@P22: register, where n = ℓ
L897@P22: 2 + (ℓ−1)2 = 2ℓ2 −2ℓ+ 1, as qℓ. On qℓ, we define the operator Zvert as any of the
L898@P22: Z Pauli operators whose support spans an entire column of vertical edges. Similarly, the operator Xhor
L899@P22: is defined as any X Pauli operator whose support spans an entire row of vertical edges. See Fig. 2a.
L900@P22: The operators Zvert and Xhor have both weight ℓ and they always anti-commute since their supports
L901@P22-23: 13(a) (b)
L902@P23: Figure 2: Planar code of distance d = 10. Qubits are placed on the edges. Pauli Z operators have
L903@P23: support on orange edges, while Pauli X operators have support on blue edges. In (2a), we can see
L904@P23: one face operator, the orange square, and one vertex operator, the blue cross. The horizontal blue
L905@P23: line represents the support of Xhor and the vertical orange one represents the support of Zvert. As
L906@P23: expected, Xhor and Zvert have odd overlap – namely the overlap on the one qubit located on the
L907@P23: bottom-right-corner edge. In (2b) colored edges represent the support of Pauli errors: blue for X
L908@P23: errors and orange for Z errors. Orange squares and blue circles correspond to the locations of the
L909@P23: stabilisers which yield non-trivial syndrome. Orange squares at the boundary of blue errors are face
L910@P23: stabilisers that anti-commute with the blue errors while blue circles are vertex stabilisers that anticommute with the orange errors. The error locations and shapes have been chosen to highlight the
L911@P23: similarity between the classical repetition code and the planar code, see also Fig. 1.
L912@P23: overlap on exactly one physical qubit. Since they respect the commutation relations of Pauli X and
L913@P23: Pauli Z in P1, they suitably define logical Pauli X and Z operators on a one-dimensional subspace of
L914@P23: (C
L915@P23: 2

L920@P23: i∼f
L921@P23: Zi,
L922@P23: +v =
L923@P23: ∏
L924@P23: i∼v

L926@P23: where i represents a qubit location. We write i ∼ f if the edge i belongs to the face f, and i ∼ v if the
L927@P23: edge i is incident to the vertex v. We define the groups S□ and S+ as the subgroups of Pn generated
L928@P23: by the faces and vertices operators respectively:
L929@P23: S□ = h □f s.t. f is a face of L i,
L930@P23: S+ = h +v s.t. v is a vertex of L i.
L931@P23: The group S = hS□, S+i is Abelian: on a square lattice, neighbouring faces and vertices shares two
L932@P23: edges, so the Z operator □f and the X operator +v, either have disjoint support or they overlap on
L933@P23-24: 14exactly two edges. Hence,
L934@P24: Nℓ

L952@P24: The chain complex associated with the planar code is:
L953@P24: F
L954@P24: n−1

L956@P24: σ
L957@P24: T
L958@P24: + −−→ F

L962@P24: n−1

L966@P24: 2

L968@P24: ⊗n, the vth coordinate of σ+(|ψi) ∈ F
L969@P24: n−1
L970@P24: 2

L972@P24: measurement of the observable +v on the state |ψi ∈ (C
L973@P24: 2
L974@P24: )
L975@P24: ⊗n yields outcome −1, and likewise for
L976@P24: σ□(·). By construction, σ+(P) = σ□(P) = 0, for every P ∈ Nℓ. Shortly, the planar code has parameters n = ℓ
L977@P24: 2 + (ℓ − 1)2
L978@P24: , k = 1, dx = dz = ℓ.
L979@P24: The planar code is the quantum twin of the classical repetition code, as we now outline. Suppose
L980@P24: that a Z error Zi occurs on a qubit placed on the vertical edge i. Upon measurement of face operators,
L981@P24: the state Zi|ψi
L982@P24: ℓ
L983@P24: yield outcomes +1 because □f and Zi commute for every face f ∈ L. The situation is
L984@P24: different when we measure vertex X-operators. Operators +v defined by vertices v that are not on the
L985@P24: same column as the edge i, commute with Zi. However, if we restrict to vertices on the same column
L986@P24: as i, we find an instance of the repetition code of length ℓ: only the vertices at the north and the south
L987@P24: of the edge i anti-commute with Zi. Furthermore, if we expand the error pattern Zi and consider a Z
L988@P24: error Z(w) with support on w < ℓ consecutive vertical edges, then, again as for the repetition code, the
L989@P24: only vertex operators that possibly anti-commute with it are the ones that reside at its north and south
L990@P24: boundary. A similar argument works for horizontal edges, east and west boundaries and X errors,
L991@P24: Fig. 2b. The observation that the encoding induced on columns by vertex operators is a repetition
L992@P24: code is key in understanding that operators of the type Zvert are the minimum weight Z-operators that
L993@P24: can not be detected – and similarly for Xhor type operators. As in the classical repetition code, where
L994@P24: the parallel flip operation on all the bits of the register was a valid logical operation on the encoded
L995@P24: bit, here Zvert and Xhor are non-trivial logical operations on the codespace, therefore undetectable.
L996@P24: Hence, for ℓ ≥ 2, all single-qubit errors can be detected and, using n = 2ℓ
L997@P24: 2 − 2ℓ + 1 physical qubits to
L998@P24: protect one logical qubit, we can decrease the probability of undetectable Pauli errors from p to p
L999@P24: ℓ
L1000@P24: .
L1001@P24: The planar code is a ‘working’ quantum error correcting code, not only it is resilient to both X
L1002@P24: and Z errors (and therefore all errors) but, at the cost of considering codes of increasing size, it can
L1003@P24-25: 15grant protection against an arbitrary number of errors. Most importantly for our scope, it is a hypergraph product code, see Chapter 3.
L1004@P25: In Sections 1.5 and 1.6, we formulate two central problems in the theory of quantum information
L1005@P25: processing, namely logical operations and syndrome errors. Our brief overview aims to familiarise the
L1006@P25: reader to our works in Chapters 4 and 5.
L1007@P25: 1.5 On logical operations
L1008@P25: A code, per se, is a static object. Nonetheless, we want to process and manipulate the information
L1009@P25: stored and protected in its codespace. To this end it is necessary to develop a theory of fault-tolerant
L1010@P25: computation and seek ways to implement logical operations on the protected logical qubits. Suppose
L1011@P25: that C(S) is a [[n, k, d]] code, with encoding map Gˆ : (C
L1012@P25: 2

L1014@P25: ⊗k −→ (C2
L1015@P25: )
L1016@P25: ⊗n. The obvious strategy is:
L1017@P25: (i) Un-encode the logical qubit state |ψi
L1018@P25: log ∈ C(S) ⊆ (C
L1019@P25: 2
L1020@P25: )
L1021@P25: ⊗n. Namely apply a left-inverse of G˜ to
L1022@P25: the n-qubit register |ψi

L1024@P25: 2

L1029@P25: 2

L1037@P25: .

L1046@P25: Clearly though, during step (ii) the physical qubits are not protected from noise.
L1047@P25: A possible solution is to avoid the un-encode step and perform the operation directly on the
L1048@P25: codespace via transversal operators. A unitary operator U on a n-qubit register is said to be transversal
L1049@P25: if it can be written as the tensor product of single-qubit unitaries:
L1050@P25: U =
L1051@P25: ⊗n
L1052@P25: i=1
L1053@P25: Ui,
L1054@P25: where Uiis unitary operator on C
L1055@P25: 2
L1056@P25: . A transversal operator is naturally fault-tolerant. A Pauli error
L1057@P25: on one of the qubits on the register is only propagated to one single qubit, and failure of one of its
L1058@P25: product components similarly only affects one qubit. Unfortunately, by the Eastin-Knill theorem, no
L1059@P25: code allows a universal set of transversal gates [19, 20]. The Eastin–Knill theorem, however, does
L1060@P25: not rule out the existence of other fault-tolerant protocols for universal quantum computation and
L1061@P25: in fact, there exist several proposals for universal fault-tolerant computation such as the addition of
L1062@P25: measurements and classical feed-forward [21], magic state distillation [22], code concatenation [23] and
L1063@P25: pieceable fault tolerance [24].
L1064@P25: 1.6 On syndrome measurement errors
L1065@P25: We have so far illustrated a framework where information is protected by a code, errors are detected
L1066@P25: via perfect syndrome measurements and the appropriate correction is found by a decoder. However,
L1067@P25: the syndrome can be noisy too and conventionally syndrome errors are dealt with by repeating multiple
L1068@P25: rounds of measurements to increase the confidence in the result. In this Section instead we discuss the
L1069@P25: single-shot scenario, where only one round of syndrome measurements is performed.
L1070@P25-26: 16Our presentation starts with the construction of a two-dimensional classical repetition code. Not
L1071@P26: only does the two-dimensional repetition code protect one single bit from flip errors in single-shot mode
L1072@P26: but, more importantly, it constitutes a simple but sound illustration of the confinement property and
L1073@P26: related single-shot error correction features [25, 26] that are the main object of Chapter 5. Single-shot
L1074@P26: error correction has historically been understood as a by-product of self-correction phenomena [27,
L1075@P26: 28], and in this same spirit we then introduce the two-dimensional Ising model as a self-correcting
L1076@P26: classical memory. Macroscopic confinement for the two-dimensional repetition code corresponds to
L1077@P26: the existence of a macroscopic energy barrier in the two-dimensional Ising model, which ensures its
L1078@P26: self-correcting properties as a classical memory. We review these concepts and their relations in Section 1.6.1.
L1079@P26: Given a syndrome map σ : F
L1080@P26: n
L1081@P26: 2 −→ F
L1082@P26: m
L1083@P26: 2
L1084@P26: , we assume that the observed syndrome s˜ is noisy:
L1085@P26: s˜ = σ(e) + η ∈ F
L1086@P26: m
L1087@P26: 2
L1088@P26: ,
L1089@P26: where e ∈ F
L1090@P26: n
L1091@P26: 2
L1092@P26: is the bit error vector and η ∈ F
L1093@P26: m
L1094@P26: 2
L1095@P26: is the syndrome measurement error. Loosely, given a
L1096@P26: [n, k, d] code with syndrome map σ, we say that a pair syndrome map-decoder (σ, Dss) is single-shot if
L1097@P26: The decoder Dss, on input a noisy syndrome σ(e) + η, for |e| < t and |η| < dss for some
L1098@P26: integer dss > 1 outputs a recovery operator er such that, the residual error on the system
L1099@P26: after correction is correctable:
L1100@P26: |e + er| < t,
L1101@P26: where t = bd−1/2c and d is the distance of the code defined by the syndrome map σ.
L1102@P26: In what follows, we refer to the classical repetition code as defined in Section 1.3 as the one-dimensional
L1103@P26: repetition code. We claim that the one-dimensional repetition code and a ML decoder are not singleshot. We illustrate our claim with an example. Consider the [7, 1, 7] repetition code with syndrome
L1104@P26: map H ∈ F
L1105@P26: 6×7
L1106@P26: 2
L1107@P26: such that Hi,i = 1, Hi,i+1 = 1, for i = 1, . . . , 6 and Hi,j = 0 elsewhere, see Fig. 1. Let
L1108@P26: e be the bit error and η the syndrome error defined as:
L1109@P26: e =
L1110@P26: (
L1111@P26: 0 0 1 1 0 0 0)T
L1112@P26: , η =
L1113@P26: (
L1114@P26: 0 1 0 0 0 0)T
L1115@P26: .
L1116@P26: The observed syndrome s˜ is:
L1117@P26: s˜ =
L1118@P26: (
L1119@P26: 0 0 0 1 0 0)T
L1120@P26: .
L1121@P26: On input s˜, the ML decoder D defined in Eq. (1.7), outputs the recovery operator er:
L1122@P26: er =

L1125@P26: yielding the non-correctable weight-5 residual error e + er:
L1126@P26: e + er =

L1129@P26: .
L1130@P26: Although an example does not constitute a proof, it is enough to reveal why the one-dimensional
L1131@P26: repetition code does not have single-shot properties. The reason is that the one-dimensional repetition
L1132@P26: code does not have confinement. We can think of the syndrome weight as a cost function. In the
L1133@P26-27: 17(a) (b) (c)
L1134@P27: Figure 3: Two-dimensional [90, 1, 90] repetition code. Bits are placed on faces and checks on edges.
L1135@P27: Grey faces represent the support of flip errors on bits. The red edges represent the support of the
L1136@P27: corresponding syndrome. In (3a) the support of the error e is highlighted in grey. The syndrome σ(e)
L1137@P27: has support on the red edges which form the perimeter of the error area. In (3b) the error e and
L1138@P27: the observed syndrome σ(e) + η. The support of the syndrome error η is represented by the shaded
L1139@P27: red edges. The observed syndrome σ(e) + η has support on the red edges. In (3c) the support of
L1140@P27: the residual error e + er is highlighted in grey. Its syndrome σ(e + er) has support on the red edges
L1141@P27: at its boundary. The residual error is produced after the recovery er is found by a ML decoder that
L1142@P27: minimises the weight of the residual syndrome σ(e + er).
L1143@P27: one-dimensional repetition code, errors on adjacent edges always trigger at most two checks, the ones
L1144@P27: at the boundaries of the error chain. Hence, starting from an error configuration where only one edge
L1145@P27: is flipped, we could lengthen the error chain at one of the boundaries without increasing the weight of
L1146@P27: its syndrome. Crucially, we could do so till we have flipped more than half of the edges, and the error
L1147@P27: chain is not correctable anymore. The key point is that the syndrome weight is not always proportional
L1148@P27: to the error weight and therefore small syndrome errors could produce high-weight residual errors: the
L1149@P27: syndrome map is not confined.
L1150@P27: Alternatively, we can build a two-dimensional repetition code and ensure that the syndrome’s
L1151@P27: weight grows with the weight of the error. In the two-dimensional repetition code we consider a square
L1152@P27: lattice where bits are placed on faces and checks on edges. Each edge assesses the parity of the faces
L1153@P27: it belongs to: the faces at its north and south for horizontal edges, the faces at its east and west for
L1154@P27: vertical edges. In particular the syndrome is local with respect to the error support. Moreover, the
L1155@P27: syndrome weight cost function grows as the perimeter of the error area: the syndrome is confined.
L1156@P27: Because the syndrome weight is proportional to the error weight, small syndrome errors will produce
L1157@P27: small residual error, see Fig. 3. This intuition is justified when the syndrome weight is mapped into
L1158@P27: an energy cost of a suitably defined statistical mechanical model. As we explain in Section 1.6.1, for
L1159@P27: our purposes the repetition code corresponds to the Ising model.
L1160@P27: 1.6.1 Statistical mechanics models for error correction
L1161@P27: The bit (and qubit) encoding proposed rely on a common strategy that we can summarise as follows.
L1162@P27: (i) Find an abstraction of the elementary system considered, the vector space F2.
L1163@P27-28: 18(ii) Identify its basic dynamics, the flip operator.
L1164@P28: (iii) Find a description for composite systems, the direct sum of vector spaces over F2.
L1165@P28: (iv) Find an embedding of the elementary system into a composite one such that the elementary
L1166@P28: system is there described by global features, the syndrome map.
L1167@P28: (v) Determine a suitable description of the dynamics of the embedded elementary system, logical
L1168@P28: flip.
L1169@P28: (vi) Verify that the chosen embedding mitigates the impact of errors, lower probability of undetected
L1170@P28: errors.
L1171@P28: Essential to successful error protection in this paradigm is the embedding of the dynamics of the
L1172@P28: elementary system, the bit, into macroscopic properties of a large system of interacting elementary
L1173@P28: units, a multi-bit register. The prototypical example of such embedding in statistical mechanics is
L1174@P28: the Ising model of ferromagnetism and its physical realization is the basis of magnetic classical data
L1175@P28: storage [29]. The fact that the two-dimensional (2D) Ising model has a phase transition ensures the
L1176@P28: existence of a self-correcting bit memory – a macroscopic physical system that enables robust storage
L1177@P28: and manipulation of classical information without the need for active error correction [27, 30].
L1178@P28: In this Section, we think of a bit as a spin- 1
L1179@P28: 2
L1180@P28: particle whose possible states are the ↑ and the ↓
L1181@P28: state, corresponding to 0 and 1 respectively. In the D-dimensional Ising model, a spin- 1
L1182@P28: 2
L1183@P28: is placed at
L1184@P28: each site of a D-dimensional lattice and each spin is coupled to its neighbouring spins [29]. We here
L1185@P28: consider only square lattices and indicate by Ω the configuration space for n spins on a D-dimensional
L1186@P28: square lattice, |Ω| = 2n. For ω ∈ Ω with the assignment ↑ to +1 and ↓ to −1, the total energy of the
L1187@P28: system reads:
L1188@P28: H(ω) = −J
L1189@P28: ∑
L1190@P28: i∼j
L1191@P28: ωiωj , (1.13)
L1192@P28: where i ∼ j if the lattice site i and j are neighbours. On a square lattice in D-dimension each spin has
L1193@P28: 2D neighbours e.g. in 1 dimension each spin in the bulk is coupled to its left and right neighbours and
L1194@P28: in 2 dimensions it is coupled to its north, east, west and south ones. We consider the ferromagnetic
L1195@P28: Ising model, obtained for J > 0 in Eq. (1.13). A ferromagnetic system has two degenerate ground
L1196@P28: states: the one with all spins aligned in the ↑ state, ω

L1199@P28: state, ω
L1200@P28: ↓

L1230@P29: For all finite positive temperatures, the average magnetization of the system is 0. In fact, if −ω
L1231@P29: denotes the configuration where all spins are flipped with respect to ω, we have H(ω) = H(−ω) and
L1232@P29: hence P(ω) = P(−ω). Because m(ω) = −m(−ω), the non-zero terms in Eq. (1.14) cancel pairwise
L1233@P29: and hmi = 0. The system however has a temperature dependency. In the limit β → 0 the Boltzmann

L1256@P29: (1.15)
L1257@P29: Equation (1.15) says that, in the limit of small temperatures, a system initialised in one of the two
L1258@P29: ground states is stable under sufficiently small thermal fluctuations. The two limit cases of infinite
L1259@P29: and zero temperature reveal the existence of very different possible scenarios. At high temperatures,
L1260@P29: we expect the typical configuration to have an equal proportion of spins in the ↑ and ↓ states. At zero
L1261@P29: temperature, we expect most of the spins to be in the same state. Nonetheless, these two limit cases do
L1262@P29: not inform us on the typical behaviour of the system for intermediate values of the inverse temperature
L1263@P29: 0 < β < ∞. For our purposes, we want to determine if it is possible to preserve the system in one of
L1264@P29: the two ground states at finite values of β < ∞, at the price of considering systems of growing size. In
L1265@P29: other words, if above we have fixed the size n of the configuration space and studied the behaviour as
L1266@P29: β → ∞, now we want to study the behaviour of the system when β is fixed and n → ∞.
L1267@P29: The problem is to assess whether or not there exist arbitrarily low but positive values of the temperature for which the magnetization of the system is observed as a global order parameter. More
L1268@P29: precisely, we want to understand whether D-dimensional lattice spin systems of increasingly large size
L1269@P29: n exhibits global magnetization, for some arbitrary β < ∞. A counting argument shows that the
L1270@P29: probability for the system to be in one of the two ground states decreases, even at low temperatures,
L1271@P29: as the number of spins increases. In fact, the number of excited states that differs from one of the two
L1272@P29: ground states on w sites is proportional to (n
L1273@P29: w
L1274@P29: )
L1275@P29: ∼
L1276@P29: n
L1277@P29: w
L1278@P29: w!
L1279@P29: . Hence, even if the ground states have larger
L1280@P29: individual probability at low temperatures, the number of different possible configurations of excited
L1281@P29: states grows with the size of the system n. The tension between these two phenomena, the energy and
L1282@P29: the entropy cost of excitations, determines the existence of a phase transition, a discontinuous change
L1283@P29: in the global magnetization of the system.
L1284@P29: In the Ising model, the existence of a phase transition is dimensionally-dependent: the 1D Ising
L1285@P29-30: 20model has no phase transition whilst the 2D Ising model, or actually the Ising model in any dimension
L1286@P30: D≥ 2, does have a phase transition. To understand how this is the case we consider the energy cost, or
L1287@P30: energy barrier, of a spin flip. Let us consider a 1D and a 2D lattice where most of the spins are aligned
L1288@P30: in the ↑ state but there are some ‘droplets’ of flipped neighbouring spins in the ↓ state. Eq. (1.13)
L1289@P30: imposes an energy cost of 2J for each pair of anti-aligned neighbouring spins, so that the energy cost
L1290@P30: for flipping a droplet of spins is proportional to its boundary. Because in 1D boundaries of consecutive
L1291@P30: segments of spins are zero-dimensional, flipping one spin has an energy penalty of 4J, but flipping any
L1292@P30: of its neighbours has no additional energy cost. Thus, starting from the ground state configuration
L1293@P30: ω
L1294@P30: ↑
L1295@P30: , we could reverse the magnetization of the system at constant energy cost – 4J. In 2D instead,
L1296@P30: the boundary of a droplet grows proportionally to its area and therefore the energy cost for reversing
L1297@P30: the magnetization of the system is proportional to the system size. As such, we expect systems of
L1298@P30: increasing size to be increasingly more robust to thermal fluctuations. Peierls argument [31] that the
L1299@P30: 2D Ising model has a phase transition at some critical temperature Tc > 0 is based on this same intuition and in particular entails that the macroscopic behaviour of the 2D Ising model depends on the
L1300@P30: temperature. At high temperature the typical configuration has magnetization close to 0, whilst below Tc the absolute value of the magnetization gets closer to 1 as the temperature decreases, see Fig. 4.
L1301@P30: Figure 4: Figure from [29]. Monte Carlo simulations of the 2D Ising model for n = 100 as p =
L1302@P30: 1 − e
L1303@P30: −2β ∈ [0, 1) varies. White dots represent ↑ spins and black dots represent ↓ spins, pc = 1 − e
L1304@P30: −2
L1305@P30: 1
L1306@P30: Tc
L1307@P30: corresponds to the value at the critical temperature Tc. For high temperatures, p is close to 0 and the
L1308@P30: ↑ and ↓ states roughly appear in the same proportions. At low temperatures and above pc, a typical
L1309@P30: configuration is a ground state with a small number of excitations and hence would give magnetization
L1310@P30: close to either +1 or −1.
L1311@P30: The correspondence of the Ising model to a self-correcting memory is understood via the identification of thermal fluctuations with random flip errors . The states ω
L1312@P30: ↑ and ω↓
L1313@P30: can be naturally thought
L1314@P30: of as the length n repetition code encoding of a bit or a qubit, as in Eq. (1.1) and Eq. (1.3). The
L1315@P30: magnetization is a global property of the system and it is also a measure of the information there
L1316@P30: stored: m(ω
L1317@P30: ↑
L1318@P30: ) = 1, m(ω
L1319@P30: ↓
L1320@P30: ) = −1 and m(ω) 6= ±1 otherwise. If we find m > 0, a majority of the spins
L1321@P30: is in the ↑ state and a ML decoder would interpret the encoded bit as being in the 0 state; vice-versa,
L1322@P30: m < 0 indicates the 1 state. The existence of a phase transition therefore determines the ability of the
L1323@P30-31: 21spin system to robustly encode a bit: if the magnetization of the system is preserved at some non-zero
L1324@P31: temperature, the logical bit can undergo thermal excitations but still preserve its information content.
L1325@P31: In conclusion, a bit encoded in the magnetization of the 2D Ising model where flip errors correspond
L1326@P31: to thermal fluctuations exhibits self-correction below a critical temperature Tc. Below Tc, the thermal
L1327@P31: fluctuations will correct errors at a higher pace than they are introduced, by favouring lower energy
L1328@P31: configurations in which the error droplets narrow.
L1329@P31: Self-correction and single-shot error correction are complementary problems [25]. In both settings,
L1330@P31: the goal is not to remove all entropy – all errors – in the system but only to keep it under control.
L1331@P31: By ensuring that the residual error left on the system is small enough, we can be confident that the
L1332@P31: information there stored is not irreversibly damaged. Self-correction is intrinsically syndrome-agnostic
L1333@P31: and therefore could explain single-shot error correction, where syndrome information has to be treated
L1334@P31: as maybe inaccurate. We refer to Chapter 5 for an in-depth analysis of single-shot error correction.

L1388@P32-34: 2324Chapter 2
L1389@P34: The need for good codes - Overview
L1390@P34: The two main challenges to overcome in order to build a usable and useful quantum computer are
L1391@P34: noise and scalability. Careful quantum code design could provide the solution to both. The planar
L1392@P34: code is today the leading approach for fault-tolerant quantum computing. Crucially, it is an LDPC
L1393@P34: code for which the syndrome extraction circuit is not only low-depth but also local1. Locality is what
L1394@P34: makes planar code experimental realizations practical [1, 2, 3, 4]. However, the planar code family
L1395@P34: parameters are not good.
L1396@P34: The term good is borrowed from classical coding theory: a [n, k, d] code is said good if both k and d
L1397@P34: scale linearly with n – the same definition applies to a [[n, k, d]] quantum code. A code family has rate
L1398@P34: r ∈ R if the ratio k/n tend to r, as we consider codes of increasing size n. In particular, codes with
L1399@P34: dimension linear in their length will have positive rate r > 0. A family of codes has a threshold pth > 0
L1400@P34: if, for error rates below pth in the examined noise model, there exists a decoder whose probability of
L1401@P34: failure decays exponentially in the system size [5]. Loosely, having a threshold means that the code is
L1402@P34: robust to typical errors and hence, by considering codes of increasing size and distance, we can increase
L1403@P34: the system’s resilience to faults.
L1404@P34: The planar code has distance scaling as √
L1405@P34: n and zero rate. Even if the distance scaling is sublinear, the planar code family has a threshold [6, 7]. Having a threshold, the planar code solves the
L1406@P34: problem of noise but fails in addressing scalability. Thirteen physical qubits2 are needed to protect
L1407@P34: one logical qubit from one error, and a reasonably protected logical qubit would need between 103
L1408@P34: and 104 physical qubits, depending on the application and the noise in the device [9, 10, 11, 12]. This
L1409@P34: contrasts with the best achievable parameters for classical LDPC codes: as first shown by Gallager in
L1410@P34: 1963 [13], good classical LDPC codes do exist. Furthermore, these codes can be efficiently decoded
L1411@P34: [14, 15]. In classical coding theory the LDPC property is necessary for the existence of efficient
L1412@P34: decoders, but, for quantum codes, it is even more relevant. At a high level, a scalable fault-tolerant
L1413@P34: quantum computation needs, of course, codes with a good rate, good distance and an efficient decoding
L1414@P34: algorithm. At a lower level, it needs to be resilient to errors occurring at any step in the circuit model.
L1415@P34: We need fault-tolerant state preparation, fault-tolerant measurements for syndrome extraction and
L1416@P34: fault-tolerant gate constructions. The LDPC assumption is here essential in the design of quantum
L1417@P34: 1As seen in Section 1.4, the planar code is a (4, 4)-qLDPC code i.e. each stabiliser only involves at most 4 qubits,
L1418@P34: and at most 4 stabilisers act on each qubit.
L1419@P34: 2More precisely: 13 data qubits and 12 auxilia qubits, for a total of 25 physical qubits. This figure is reduced to 17
L1420@P34: physical qubits – 9 data and 8 auxilia qubits – for the rotated version of the planar code, see [8].
L1421@P34-35: 25codes because the number of gates, and hence potentially faulty locations, required to perform error
L1422@P35: correction scales with the stabilizer weight [16, 17, 18].
L1423@P35: Gallager’s construction is random: with high probability, a random low-density parity check matrix
L1424@P35: will define a classical code with constant rate and linear distance. However, to build a quantum LDPC
L1425@P35: code, some structure is needed. On the one hand, a [[n, k, d]] stabilizer code can be mapped onto a
L1426@P35: [[4n, 2k, 2d]] CSS code, so that parameters are the same up to constant factors [19]. On the other, we
L1427@P35: cannot employ random binary code constructions to define CSS codes via their symplectic representation. A random matrix for the X stabilizers would define a high-distance classical code, as such the
L1428@P35: resulting Z stabilizer matrix will not be sparse. In the literature, structure to ensure the commutativity constraints whilst preserving the sparsity of the check matrices has either been given by geometry
L1429@P35: or via randomness aided by product constructions. The earliest examples of the former, obtained via
L1430@P35: hyperbolic surfaces, can be found in [20, 21, 22] or the review [23]. Our work focuses on the first
L1431@P35: product construction, the hypergraph product, originally proposed by Tillich and Zémor in [24]. The
L1432@P35: planar code is a special instance of hypergraph product codes which, in general, use classical random
L1433@P35: matrices as seeds [24, 25, 26, 27]. When good LDPC code families are used as seeds, the corresponding
L1434@P35: hypergraph product codes have constant rate and distance scaling as √n. Even if hypergraph product
L1435@P35: codes do not have great distance scaling, as happens for the planar code, they tolerate typical errors
L1436@P35: [5]. Furthermore, they can be efficiently decoded [28, 29, 30, 31, 32, 33, 34, 35] and some work has also
L1437@P35: been done in terms of fault-tolerant computation [36, 37, 38], and syndrome extraction circuits [39, 40].
L1438@P35: When we started working on hypergraph product codes they were the best LDPC code family known,
L1439@P35: with k ∼ n and d ∼
L1440@P35: √
L1441@P35: n. This is no longer the case and good qLDPC codes are now known to exist.
L1442@P35: We here report a brief history of the good qLDPC discovery and refer the reader to [23] for a –already
L1443@P35: outdated– review. In 1997 Kitaev introduces the toric code – a hypergraph product code with parameters [[2n
L1444@P35: 2
L1445@P35: , 2, n]]. In 2002, Freedman, Meyer and Luo used hyperbolic geometry to build a family of
L1446@P35: codes with distance scaling as √
L1447@P35: n
L1448@P35: √4
L1449@P35: log n but again with zero rate [20]. No progress was made until
L1450@P35: 2020, when the first code constructions with d ∼
L1451@P35: √
L1452@P35: n polylog n were proposed [41, 42] and then finally
L1453@P35: d > √
L1454@P35: n polylog n [43, 44, 45]. Some of these constructions yield a constant rate in the asymptotic
L1455@P35: limit but still do not obey the definition of good codes. Panteleev and Kalachev eventually exhibited
L1456@P35: the first construction of asymptotically good quantum LDPC codes in 2021 [46], closely followed by
L1457@P35: Leverrier and Zémor [47], Dinur et al. [48], Lin and Hsieh [49]. The existence of (asymptotically) good
L1458@P35: quantum LDPC codes is definitely a breakthrough for our field but it is far from being the end of the
L1459@P35: story.
L1460@P35: Good LDPC codes could solve scalability in theory, but much more goes into the architecture of
L1461@P35: a fault-tolerant quantum computer – at a minimum: experimentally feasible circuit design, practical
L1462@P35: efficient decoders and fault-tolerant encoded operations are needed. These issues have been studied
L1463@P35: at depth for the surface code (see, for instance, the review [9]) and less so for codes with k > 1 [23].
L1464@P35: Gottesman’s foundational Threshold Theorem [50] ensures that fault-tolerant quantum computation
L1465@P35: is possible with constant overhead if positive rate codes are available. The overhead is there defined
L1466@P35: as the ratio of physical qubits over logical qubits used in a fault-tolerant algorithm, and Gottesman
L1467@P35: shows that it scales as the inverse rate of the code family, in the limit of very large computations.
L1468@P35: Nonetheless, the Threshold Theorem establishes that fault-tolerant quantum computing architectures
L1469@P35: are viable as long as we use positive rate codes.
L1470@P35-36: 26Hypergraph product codes satisfy all necessary conditions of Gottesman’s result – roughly, they
L1471@P36: are LDPC, have constant rate, efficient decoding algorithms and a threshold [5, 29, 30]. Hypergraph
L1472@P36: product codes could thus serve as a bridge between a surface-code architecture and a constant-overhead
L1473@P36: one. Their study could inform the exploration and design of new techniques for syndrome extraction,
L1474@P36: decoding and fault-tolerant gates implementation that could be later generalised to other code constructions. As per the Threshold Theorem, they could also serve as the cardinal building block of a
L1475@P36: scalable quantum computer.
L1476@P36: The three pieces of original work we present in Chapter 3, Chapter 4 and Chapter 5 aim to aid
L1477@P36: and foster the design of a full fault-tolerant architecture with hypergraph product codes. In the effort
L1478@P36: of giving practical answers to the Threshold Theorem requirements, we address the issues of decoding,
L1479@P36: fault-tolerant gates and measurement errors during syndrome extraction. In Chapter 3 we detail the
L1480@P36: code construction and present a novel qMW decoder for hypergraph product codes. We propose a
L1481@P36: fault-tolerant method to implement logical gates in Chapter 4. In Chapter 5 we exhibit a foundational
L1482@P36: result on code design for faulty syndromes. We conclude in Chapter 6, discussing some open questions.

L1588@P39-40: 30Chapter 3
L1589@P40: Code construction and decoding -
L1590@P40: ReShape
L1591@P40: Context and Results
L1592@P40: In [1] we propose the ReShape decoder for hypergraph product codes: we show how to lift a classical
L1593@P40: maximum-likelihood decoder (ML decoder, see page 9) to a quantum minimum-weight decoder (qMW
L1594@P40: decoder, see page 11). The adaptation of classical decoders to the quantum setting is, in general, not
L1595@P40: a trivial task. For instance, belief-propagation [2] can efficiently decode all classical LDPC codes but,
L1596@P40: because of the degeneracy of quantum codes, it cannot be used out-of-the-box on stabilizer codes.
L1597@P40: Similarly, the bit-flip decoding algorithm for classical expander codes fails in the quantum setting [3,
L1598@P40: 4]. Both these algorithms however can be modified to work on quantum codes [5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11].
L1599@P40: Differently, our ReShape decoder uses the classical decoding algorithm as a black box. In a nutshell,
L1600@P40: hypergraph product codes are built via the tensor product of two classical linear codes; we ‘invert’ the
L1601@P40: product structure to reduce the quantum decoding problem to a classical one. This action, an ad hoc
L1602@P40: version of the Cleaning Lemma [12], informs us of some invariant properties of hypergraph product
L1603@P40: codes which further clarify the composition of their logical Pauli group.
L1604@P40: Limitations
L1605@P40: The ReShape decoder is not suitable for use in realistic noise settings as it only works for adversarial
L1606@P40: noise. An adversarial error model is such that an adversary chooses error locations to damage the
L1607@P40: encoded information. Under this noise model, and by definition of [[n, k, d]] code, an adversary is
L1608@P40: always able to find t = b
L1609@P40: d−1
L1610@P40: 2
L1611@P40: c locations for which any decoder would fail. On the contrary, under
L1612@P40: local stochastic noise of parameter p, we expect to have about pn errors on n physical qubits, but we
L1613@P40: also expect the probability of t malicious locations to undergo errors to be low. Kovalev and Pryadko
L1614@P40: showed that typical errors can be corrected by any qLDPC code provided that p is below a certain
L1615@P40: threshold pth [13]. At low p, typical errors form isolated clusters in a low-degree graph associated
L1616@P40: with the code. As such, a decoder able to correct isolated and not malicious clusters separately would
L1617@P40: succeed with high probability. ReShape however is intrinsically non-local on the relevant graph and
L1618@P40: hence it fails to satisfy Kovalev and Pryadko’s assumptions.

L1620@P41: AOQ derived the proofs, the numerical simulations and wrote the manuscript.
