L1647@P41-42: 32IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON INFORMATION THEORY, VOL. 68, NO. 10, OCTOBER 2022 6569
L1648@P42: ReShape: A Decoder for Hypergraph Product Codes
L1649@P42: Armanda O. Quintavalle and Earl T. Campbell
L1650@P42: Abstract— The design of decoding algorithms is a significant
L1651@P42: technological component in the development of fault-tolerant
L1652@P42: quantum computers. Often design of quantum decoders is
L1653@P42: inspired by classical decoding algorithms, but there are no
L1654@P42: general principles for building quantum decoders from classical
L1655@P42: decoders. Given any pair of classical codes, we can build
L1656@P42: a quantum code using the hypergraph product, yielding a
L1657@P42: hypergraph product code. Here we show we can also lift the
L1658@P42: decoders for these classical codes. That is, given oracle access
L1659@P42: to a minimum weight decoder for the relevant classical codes,
L1660@P42: the corresponding [[n, k, d]] quantum code can be efficiently
L1661@P42: decoded for any error of weight smaller than (d − 1)/2.
L1662@P42: The quantum decoder requires only O(k) oracle calls to the
L1663@P42: classical decoder and O(n2) classical resources. The lift and the
L1664@P42: correctness proof of the decoder have a purely algebraic nature
L1665@P42: that draws on the discovery of some novel homological invariants
L1666@P42: of the hypergraph product codespace. While the decoder works
L1667@P42: perfectly for adversarial errors, that is errors of weight up to half
L1668@P42: the code distance, it is not suitable for more realistic stochastic
L1669@P42: noise models and therefore can not be used to establish an error
L1670@P42: correcting threshold.
L1671@P42: Index Terms— Quantum error correction, product codes,
L1672@P42: decoding.
L1673@P42: I. INTRODUCTION
L1674@P42: The construction of quantum codes often takes classical
L1675@P42: codes as a starting point. The CSS construction is one method
L1676@P42: for combining a pair of classical codes into a quantum code.
L1677@P42: However, the CSS recipe only works when the pair of classical
L1678@P42: codes are dual to each other. Unfortunately, some of the
L1679@P42: best known classical code families, such as those based on
L1680@P42: expander graphs, do not come in convenient dual pairs. The
L1681@P42: hypergraph product is a different recipe that allows a pair
L1682@P42: of arbitrary classical codes to form the basis of a quantum
L1683@P42: code [1]. Crucially, when the hypergraph product uses families
L1684@P42: of classical low-density parity check (LDPC) codes, it leads
L1685@P42: to families of quantum-LDPC codes. The quantum-LDPC
L1686@P42: property eases the experimental difficulty of implementation
L1687@P42: and, combined with suitably growing distance, ensures the
L1688@P42: existence of an error correction threshold [2].
L1689@P42: Manuscript received 10 August 2021; revised 29 March 2022; accepted
L1690@P42: 6 June 2022. Date of publication 17 June 2022; date of current version
L1691@P42: 15 September 2022. This work was supported by the Engineering and
L1692@P42: Physical Sciences Research Council under Grant EP/M024261/1 (E.T.C).
L1693@P42: (Corresponding author: Armanda O. Quintavalle.)
L1694@P42: Armanda O. Quintavalle is with the Department of Physics and
L1695@P42: Astronomy, The University of Sheffield, Sheffield S3 7RH, U.K. (e-mail:
L1696@P42: armandaoq@gmail.com).
L1697@P42: Earl T. Campbell is with the Department of Physics and
L1698@P42: Astronomy, The University of Sheffield, Sheffield S3 7RH, U.K., and
L1699@P42: also with Riverlane, Cambridge CB2 3BZ, U.K.
L1700@P42: Communicated by P. Kiran Sarvepalli, Associate Editor for Quantum
L1701@P42: Information Theory.
L1702@P42: Color versions of one or more figures in this article are available at
L1703@P42: https://doi.org/10.1109/TIT.2022.3184108.
L1704@P42: Digital Object Identifier 10.1109/TIT.2022.3184108
L1705@P42: Two of the most widely known quantum codes, the toric
L1706@P42: and planar surface codes, are hypergraph product codes that
L1707@P42: use the classical repetition code as their seed classical code.
L1708@P42: The decoding problem for the surface code can be recast
L1709@P42: as a minimum-weight perfect-matching problem, which is
L1710@P42: efficiently solved by the blossom algorithm [3], [4] and the
L1711@P42: union-find algorithm [5]. Another interesting class of hypergraph product codes uses classical expander codes as their
L1712@P42: seed, with the resulting offspring called quantum expander
L1713@P42: codes [6], which are quantum-LDPC codes achieving both
L1714@P42: constant rate and Ω(√n) distance. The classical expander
L1715@P42: codes can be decoded by a very simple bit-flip algorithm discovered by Spiser and Spielman [7]. This inspired the small-set
L1716@P42: flip decoder for quantum expander codes, which follows a similar idea but is slightly modified, and has been shown to correct
L1717@P42: adversarial errors [6], stochastic errors [8] and also to operate
L1718@P42: as a single-shot decoder [9]. However, any binary linear code
L1719@P42: can be used as a seed to build hypergraph product codes. Using
L1720@P42: classical codes other than repetition and expander codes, for
L1721@P42: instance the semi-topological codes proposed in [10], yield
L1722@P42: a broad range of hypergraph product codes for which there
L1723@P42: is no general propose decoder that is proven to work across
L1724@P42: the whole code family. For classical LDPC codes, using a
L1725@P42: belief propagation decoder (BP) works well in practice but it
L1726@P42: cannot be used out of the box on quantum-LDPC codes. In fact
L1727@P42: whenever a decoding instance has more than one minimum
L1728@P42: weight solution, it is degenerate, BP does not converge and
L1729@P42: yields a decoding failure. Degeneracy is the quintessential
L1730@P42: feature of quantum codes and therefore some workarounds
L1731@P42: are needed to use BP on quantum-LDPC codes [11], [12].
L1732@P42: The literature offers many examples of BP inspired decoders
L1733@P42: for quantum-LDPC codes which show an error correcting
L1734@P42: threshold [10], [13]–[17], however none of them come with
L1735@P42: a correctness proof. Recently, a union-find like decoder has
L1736@P42: been proposed to decode quantum-LDPC codes [18]. The
L1737@P42: authors in [18] prove that their union-find decoder corrects
L1738@P42: for all errors of weight up to a polynomial in the distance
L1739@P42: for three classes of quantum-LDPC codes: codes with linear
L1740@P42: confinement (see [19], [20]), D-dimensional hyperbolic codes
L1741@P42: and D-dimensional toric codes for D ≥ 3. The decoder
L1742@P42: in [18] is therefore provably correct for adversarial noise,
L1743@P42: nonetheless a comprehensive investigation of its performance
L1744@P42: under stochastic noise is still missing.
L1745@P42: Here we introduce the ReShape decoder for generic hypergraph product codes. Given a [[n, k, d]] hypergraph product
L1746@P42: code built using classical codes with parity matrices δA
L1747@P42: and δB, we assume access to a minimum weight decoder for
L1748@P42: parity matrices δA, δB, δT
L1749@P42: A and δTB. The ReShape decoder
L1750@P42: calls these classical decoders as blackbox oracles without
L1751@P42: any modification or knowledge of their internal working,
L1752@P42: 0018-9448 © 2022 IEEE. Personal use is permitted, but republication/redistribution requires IEEE permission.
L1753@P42: See https://www.ieee.org/publications/rights/index.html for more information.
L1754@P42-43: Authorized licensed use limited to: Sheffield University. Downloaded on September 21,2022 at 17:03:24 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Restrictions apply.6570 IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON INFORMATION THEORY, VOL. 68, NO. 10, OCTOBER 2022
L1755@P43: and furthermore only requires O(k) oracle calls, and only
L1756@P43: a polynomial amount of additional classical computation.
L1757@P43: Under these conditions we prove that ReShape works in
L1758@P43: the adversarial setting, correcting errors (up to stabilisers) of
L1759@P43: weight less than half the code distance. Therefore, ReShape
L1760@P43: lifts the classical decoders to the status of a quantum decoder,
L1761@P43: providing the first general purpose hypergraph product codes
L1762@P43: decoder proven to correct adversarial errors. Formally we
L1763@P43: prove:
L1764@P43: Theorem 1: Any [[n, k, d]] hypergraph product code constructed from the classical parity check matrices δA and δB
L1765@P43: can be successfully decoded from error of weight up to
L1766@P43: (d − 1)/2 using O(k) oracle calls to classical decoders for
L1767@P43: the seed matrices and their transpose plus O(n2) classical
L1768@P43: operations.
L1769@P43: Theorem 1 though, does not state anything about stochastic noise or error correcting thresholds. Families of n-qubit
L1770@P43: hypergraph product codes have distance of at most O(
L1771@P43: √n)
L1772@P43: and so they are bad codes in the sense that the distance is
L1773@P43: sub-linear. However, given a stochastic noise model with each
L1774@P43: qubit affected independently with probability p, the typical
L1775@P43: error size will be pn. Thus, for n> (d/2p), the most
L1776@P43: likely errors will not necessarily be corrected by ReShape
L1777@P43: and there is no guarantee that a threshold will be observed.
L1778@P43: Indeed, we implemented ReShape for several code families
L1779@P43: and found evidence that ReShape fails to provide a threshold
L1780@P43: (see Figure 4). A clear open problem is whether there exists a
L1781@P43: similar general lifting procedure, or modification of ReShape,
L1782@P43: for which one can prove good performance in the stochastic
L1783@P43: settings. Hence, if on one hand Theorem 1 provides a solution
L1784@P43: to the adversarial decoding problem for hypergraph product
L1785@P43: codes, on the other, a stronger, difficult and much longed-for
L1786@P43: result is desirable. Namely, the solution of the stochastic
L1787@P43: decoding problem for hypergraph product codes both on a
L1788@P43: theoretical level (proof of a threshold) and on a practical one
L1789@P43: (numerical observation of a high correcting threshold). Even
L1790@P43: so, ReShape still provides some improvement over state-of theart BP and union-find like decoders for stochastic noise. First,
L1791@P43: ReShape comes with a proof of correctness, that BP lacks;
L1792@P43: second, the proof works for all errors up to the optimal value
L1793@P43: of (d − 1)/2, whilst the modification of union-find proposed
L1794@P43: in [18] is provably correct only for errors of weight up to Adα,
L1795@P43: for some A, α > 0 and α< 1.
L1796@P43: II. PRELIMINARIES AND NOTATION
L1797@P43: A classical [n, k, d] linear code is compactly described by
L1798@P43: its parity check matrix H. The matrix H is a binary matrix of
L1799@P43: size m × n such that the codespace C(H) ⊆ n
L1800@P43: 2 is described
L1801@P43: by:
L1802@P43: C(H)= {v ∈ n
L1803@P43: 2 : Hv =0}. (1)
L1804@P43: The codespace C(H) has dimension k = n − rank(H) and
L1805@P43: distance d defined as:
L1806@P43: d = min{|v| : v ∈C(H),v =0},
L1807@P43: where |v| is the Hamming weight of the binary vector v.
L1808@P43: Whenever the parity check matrix has columns and rows
L1809@P43: of small weight we say that it is a low density parity
L1810@P43: check (LDPC) matrix; when H has constant column and row
L1811@P43: weight wc,wr we shortly say that it is a (wc,wr)-matrix.
L1812@P43: The classical decoding problem can be stated as: given a
L1813@P43: syndrome vector s ∈ m
L1814@P43: 2 , find the minimum weight solution
L1815@P43: e ∈ n
L1816@P43: 2 to the equation
L1817@P43: He = s. (2)
L1818@P43: It is easy to show that the optimal decoder for any classical
L1819@P43: linear code can correct errors of weight up to half the code
L1820@P43: distance (see, for instance, [21]).
L1821@P43: A quantum [[n, k, d]] stabiliser code [22] is a subspace of
L1822@P43: dimension 2k of the Hilbert space (2)⊗n. It is described
L1823@P43: as the common +1 eigenspace of its stabiliser group S,
L1824@P43: an Abelian subgroup of the Pauli group Pn such that − ∈S.
L1825@P43: The Pauli group on n qubits is the group generated by the
L1826@P43: n-fold tensor product of single qubit Pauli operators. The
L1827@P43: weight |P| of a Pauli operator P ∈Pn is the number of
L1828@P43: its non-identity factors. We indicate by N (S) the normaliser
L1829@P43: of S i.e. the group of Paulis which commute with the stabiliser
L1830@P43: group S. Because S⊆N (S), the quotient group
L1831@P43: L = N(S)
L1832@P43: /S
L1833@P43: is well defined and referred to as homology group, see
L1834@P43: Appendix B. Elements [P] of L are homology classes: equivalence classes with respect to the congruence modulo multiplication by stabiliser operators. Explicitly:
L1835@P43: [P]= {PS : S ∈ S}, (3)
L1836@P43: and for any Pauli P, its homology class [P] is uniquely defined
L1837@P43: via Eq. (3). Importantly, each Pauli P such that [P] =[]
L1838@P43: in L is an operator that preserves the codespace and has
L1839@P43: non-trivial action on it. We refer to such code operators
L1840@P43: modulo S as logical Pauli operators; with slight abuse of
L1841@P43: notation we write P ∈L, meaning [P] ∈L. Two logical
L1842@P43: operators P, Q are said to be homologically equivalent, or just
L1843@P43: equivalent, if and only if they belong to the same homology
L1844@P43: class i.e. by Eq. (3), if and only if [P]=[Q]. Importantly,
L1845@P43: for a code of dimension k, LPk. The distance d of the
L1846@P43: code is the minimum weight of any non-trivial logical operator
L1847@P43: in L. Any generating set of the stabiliser group S induces a
L1848@P43: syndrome map σ. Namely, if S = S1,...,Sm	, the associated
L1849@P43: syndrome function σ maps any Pauli P ∈Pn in a binary
L1850@P43: vector s =(s1,...,sm)T ∈ m
L1851@P43: 2 such that si =0 if and
L1852@P43: only if P commutes with Si and 1 otherwise. We refer to the
L1853@P43: vector s as the syndrome. Conventionally, when considering
L1854@P43: a stabiliser code, it is always intended that a generating set
L1855@P43: {S1,...,Sm} for the stabiliser group is chosen and with it a
L1856@P43: syndrome map. We say that a stabiliser code is LDPC if each
L1857@P43: Si has low weight and each qubit is in the support of only a
L1858@P43: few generators.
L1859@P43: The decoding problem for stabiliser codes can be stated
L1860@P43: as: given a syndrome vector s ∈ m
L1861@P43: 2 , find an operator
L1862@P43: Er ∈Pn such that (i) σ(Er)= s and (ii) [Er]=[Emin],
L1863@P43: where Emin is a minimum weight operator with syndrome s.
L1864@P43: We call any operator that satisfies (i) a valid solution of the
L1865@P43: syndrome equation and operators for which both (i) and (ii)
L1866@P43: are true, correct solutions.
L1867@P43-44: Authorized licensed use limited to: Sheffield University. Downloaded on September 21,2022 at 17:03:24 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Restrictions apply.QUINTAVALLE AND CAMPBELL: ReSHAPE: A DECODER FOR HYPERGRAPH PRODUCT CODES 6571
L1868@P44: Pauli operators can be put into a one-to-one correspondence
L1869@P44: with binary vectors, if we discard the phase factor ±i. In fact,
L1870@P44: any Pauli P can be written as:
L1871@P44: P ∝ X[v] · Z[w],
L1872@P44: = Xv1 ⊗ ... ⊗ Xvn · Zw1 ⊗ ... ⊗ Zwn , v, w ∈ n
L1873@P44: 2
L1874@P44: from which it follows:
L1875@P44: (X[v]Z[w])(X[v
L1876@P44: ]Z[w]) = ±X[v + v]Z[w + w], (4)
L1877@P44: and two operators commute if and only if
L1878@P44: v, w
L1879@P44: 	 + v,w	 = 0 mod 2 (5)
L1880@P44: and anti-commute otherwise. This correspondence between
L1881@P44: binary vectors and Pauli operators is particularly handy when
L1882@P44: dealing with CSS codes [23], [24]. CSS codes are stabiliser
L1883@P44: codes for which the stabiliser group can be generated by two
L1884@P44: disjoint sets Sx and Sz of X and Z type operators respectively.
L1885@P44: If Sx = {X[v1],...X[vmx ]}, Sz = {Z[w1],...,Z[wmz ]} and
L1886@P44: we define HX and HZ as the matrices whose rows are the vis
L1887@P44: and the wis respectively, then the commutation relation on
L1888@P44: the stabilisers generators translate in to the binary constraint
L1889@P44: HXHT
L1890@P44: Z =0. Using Eq. (5), it is easy to show that the
L1891@P44: syndrome for a Pauli error E = X[ex]Z[ez] is described by
L1892@P44: the two binary vectors sz = HZex and sx = HXez. Since
L1893@P44: these two linear equations are independent, we can treat the
L1894@P44: X-part and Z-part of the error separately. For CSS codes,
L1895@P44: we define the X-distance dx as the minimum weight of an
L1896@P44: operator X[v] which commutes with all the stabilisers in Sz
L1897@P44: but does not belong to the group generated by Sx. Note that
L1898@P44: the weight of an operator X[v] equates the Hamming weight
L1899@P44: |v| of the vector v. Therefore, combining Eq. (4), Eq. (5) and
L1900@P44: the definition of dx, we shortly say that dx is the minimum
L1901@P44: weight of a vector v in ker HZ which does not belong to the
L1902@P44: row span of HX, i.e.
L1903@P44: dx := min{|v| : HZv =0,v ∈ Im HT
L1904@P44: X} (6)
L1905@P44: Similarly, dz is the minimum weight of a vector in ker HX
L1906@P44: not in Im HT
L1907@P44: Z .
L1908@P44: The Z-error decoding problem for CSS code can be stated
L1909@P44: as: given a syndrome vector s ∈ mx
L1910@P44: 2 , find a valid and correct
L1911@P44: solution e ∈ n
L1912@P44: 2 to the equation:
L1913@P44: HXe = s, (7)
L1914@P44: where er is valid if and only if HXer = s and it is correct if
L1915@P44: and only if it belongs to the homology class of the minimum
L1916@P44: weight operator with syndrome s. Because for an operator
L1917@P44: Z[e] its weight equates the Hamming weight of the vector e,
L1918@P44: the Z-decoding problem for CSS codes can be reformulated
L1919@P44: exactly as done for the classical decoding problem in Eq. (2).
L1920@P44: Explicitly, given s, find the minimum weight solution to the
L1921@P44: linear equation HXe = s. The X-decoding problem is derived
L1922@P44: from Eq. (7) by duality, exchanging the role of X and Z.
L1923@P44: It goes without saying that, if any CSS code defines two
L1924@P44: classical parity check matrices, the converse is also true.
L1925@P44: Namely, starting from any two binary matrices H1,H2 such
L1926@P44: that H1HT
L1927@P44: 2 =0, this defines a CSS code with HX = H1,
L1928@P44: HZ = H2. If the classical linear code with parity check Hi
L1929@P44: has parameters [n, ki,di], the associated quantum code has
L1930@P44: parameters [[n, k1 + k2 − n, dx,dz]] where dx ≥ d2 and
L1931@P44: dz ≥ d1. A review on quantum codes can be found, for
L1932@P44: instance, in [25], [26].
L1933@P44: In this article we focus on a sub-class of CSS codes,
L1934@P44: the hypergraph product codes [27]–[30]. We give a minimal description of these codes in Section III and we refer
L1935@P44: the reader to Appendix B for a more detailed presentation.
L1936@P44: We study some homology invariants for the logical operators
L1937@P44: of the hypergraph product codes in Section IV-A. These
L1938@P44: invariants are the algebraic core upon which we design a
L1939@P44: decoder for these codes, the ReShape decoder. We prove that
L1940@P44: ReShape is an efficient and correct decoder for adversarial
L1941@P44: noise in Section IV-B. We conclude with some consideration on the performance of ReShape under stochastic noise
L1942@P44: in Section IV-C.
L1943@P44: III. HYPERGRAPH PRODUCT CODES
L1944@P44: We here present a bottom-up overview on hypergraph product codes. The purpose of this Section is dual: we both want to
L1945@P44: describe the hypergraph product codes with the least possible
L1946@P44: technical overhead and introduce the notation necessary to
L1947@P44: motivate and give an intuition for the results presented in
L1948@P44: Section IV. We refer the reader interested in the homology
L1949@P44: theory approach to Appendix B.
L1950@P44: The most well-known example of hypergraph product code
L1951@P44: is the toric code and its variations [31], [32]. The toric code is
L1952@P44: conventionally represented by a square lattice where qubits
L1953@P44: sit on edges, X-stabilisers are identified with vertices and
L1954@P44: Z-stabilisers with faces. Since a square lattice has two kind of
L1955@P44: edges, vertical and horizontal edges, the first evident feature
L1956@P44: of this identification is that, accordingly, there are two type of
L1957@P44: qubits. The second is that each vertex/X-stabiliser uniquely
L1958@P44: identifies a row of horizontal edges and a column of vertical
L1959@P44: one, starting from the four ones that are incident to it. The
L1960@P44: third is that faces/Z-stabilisers, similarly to vertices, uniquely
L1961@P44: identify a column of horizontal edges and a row of vertical
L1962@P44: ones, starting from the four which lie on its boundary. Very
L1963@P44: similar attributes can be found in all the hypergraph product
L1964@P44: codes, as we now explain.
L1965@P44: Consider two classical parity check matrices δA,δB of size
L1966@P44: ma × na and mb × nb; we indicate with C(δA,δB) their
L1967@P44: hypergraph product code and refer to the matrices δA and
L1968@P44: δB as seed matrices. The qubits of the code C(δA,δB) can
L1969@P44: be labelled as left and right qubits. Left qubits can be placed
L1970@P44: in a na × nb grid and right qubits in a ma × mb grid, see
L1971@P44: Figure 1. Under this labelling, left and right qubits are uniquely
L1972@P44: identified by pair of indices (ja,jb) and (ia,ib) respectively,
L1973@P44: where ja,jb vary among the column indices of δA,δB while
L1974@P44: ia,ib vary among their row indices. Given a pair (L, R) of
L1975@P44: binary matrices, of size na × nb and ma × mb respectively,
L1976@P44: we define the Z-operator:
L1977@P44: Z(L, R)=

L1991@P44: of the operator and to R as its right part.

L2125@P46: to be ∞. In particular, whenever one or both seed matrices

L2127@P46: expression for k cancel out e.g. if δA or δB have full rank,
L2128@P46: then k =(na − rka)(nb − rkb), dx = db and dz = da.
L2129@P46: The similarities in structure between general hypergraph

L2132@P46: product code C(δL,δL) where δL is the full-rank L − 1 × L

L2148@P46: edges; vertices and faces on the square lattice can be indexed
L2149@P46: in the natural way yielding the same stabiliser indexing of
L2150@P46: the general hypergraph product codes; string like (resp. loop
L2151@P46: like) logical operators correspond precisely to the left and
L2152@P46: right logical operators described above which have single
L2153@P46: column/single row support.
L2154@P46: In what follows, we focus on Z-errors and their correction.
L2155@P46: With slight abuse of notation, we will refer to pair of matrices
L2156@P46: (L, R) as operators (and vice versa sometimes) where the
L2157@P46: identification is clear via Eq. (8). The corresponding results
L2158@P46: for X-errors are easily obtained by duality as per any CSS
L2159@P46: code. More precisely, by swapping the role of X and Z but
L2160@P46: also the role of rows and columns; alternatively, considering
L2161@P46: the code C(δT
L2162@P46: A,δTB), see Appendix B.
L2163@P46: IV. RESULTS
L2164@P46: Here we present the ReShape decoder. The intuition behind
L2165@P46: ReShape is that we can look at hypergaph product codes as
L2166@P46: codes built combining (product) multiple copies of the same
L2167@P46: classical codes. As such, with due care, we can ‘decouple’
L2168@P46: these copies and retrieve the original classical seed codes.
L2169@P46: On a [[n, k, d]] hypergraph product code C(δA,δB),
L2170@P46: ReShape works by splitting the decoding problem into
L2171@P46: k smaller classical decoding problems which can be solved
L2172@P46: using classical decoding algorithms for the seed matrices.
L2173@P46: In order to identify the k classical decoding problems,
L2174@P46: it applies a linear transformation, a change of basis, on the
L2175@P46: n dimensional codespace of C(δA,δB), yielding a canonical
L2176@P46: form for error operators. This canonical form exposes two
L2177@P46: important features of the codespace: the first one is that logical
L2178@P46: operators of C(δA,δB) are naturally partitioned into two sets,
L2179@P46: of left and right operators; the second is that the weight of
L2180@P46: each logical operator directly depends on the weight of the
L2181@P46: classical codewords of the seed codes. By writing an operator
L2182@P46: in its canonical form, we can immediately assess to which of
L2183@P46: the two classes it belongs and, via classical decoding, to which
L2184@P46: logical operator it is closest. Hence, we successfully detect and
L2185@P46: correct errors.
L2186@P46: In this Section, we first proceed to study the algebraic
L2187@P46: invariants of the logical operators upon which the canonical
L2188@P46: form is defined. The correctness of ReShape, and so the
L2189@P46: proof of Theorem 1, strongly relies on the existence of these
L2190@P46: invariants. We detail the Reshape algorithm in Section IV-B
L2191@P46: and discuss its limitations in Section IV-C. All the proof of
L2192@P46: this Section are deferred to Appendix C.
L2193@P46: A. Invariants
L2194@P46: The characteristic shape of operators on the codespace of
L2195@P46: C(δA,δB) and the structure of its stabilisers and logical operators, induces a canonical form for Z-operators in C(δA,δB).
L2196@P46-47: Authorized licensed use limited to: Sheffield University. Downloaded on September 21,2022 at 17:03:24 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Restrictions apply.6574 IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON INFORMATION THEORY, VOL. 68, NO. 10, OCTOBER 2022
L2197@P47: More precisely, by combining the construction outlined in
L2198@P47: Section III and the definition of complement of a vector
L2199@P47: subspace (see Appendix A) we have proven the following:
L2200@P47: Proposition 1 (Canonical Form): Let (L, R) be a
L2201@P47: Z-operator on the codespace of C(δA,δB). For a vector space
L2202@P47: V ⊆ n
L2203@P47: 2 , we denote by V • any space such that V ⊕V •  n2 ,
L2204@P47: (see Appendix A). Then, for the operator (L, R), the left part L
L2205@P47: can be expressed as a sum of a free part ML and a logical
L2206@P47: part OL such that every row of ML belongs to Im δT
L2207@P47: B and
L2208@P47: every row of OL belongs to (Im δT
L2209@P47: B)•. Similarly, the right
L2210@P47: part R can be expressed as a sum of a free part MR and a
L2211@P47: logical part OR such that every column of MR belongs to
L2212@P47: Im δA and every column of OR belongs to (Im δA)•. Hence,
L2213@P47: for (L, R) holds:
L2214@P47: (L, R)=(ML + OL,MR + OR). (CF)
L2215@P47: We refer to the writing given by Eq. (CF) as canonical form
L2216@P47: of the operator (L, R).
L2217@P47: Crucially, as we detail in Appendix C, it is always possible
L2218@P47: to ‘move’ the support of the free part of an operator from
L2219@P47: the left qubits to the right qubits and vice versa, by adding
L2220@P47: stabilisers. Opposite is the situation for the logical part: the
L2221@P47: support of the logical part of an operator cannot be moved
L2222@P47: from the left to the right qubits without changing its homology
L2223@P47: class. These two observations justify the name free and logical
L2224@P47: part in the canonical form of a Z-operator on C(δA,δB).
L2225@P47: We refer to Figure 1 and 2 for a visual representation of the
L2226@P47: canonical form of a Z-operator on C(δA,δB). In Figures 1b
L2227@P47: and 1e we see stabiliser operators in their canonical form:
L2228@P47: their free part has support pictured, their logical part is 0.
L2229@P47: In Figures 1c, 1d, 1f, 1g we see logical operators in their
L2230@P47: canonical form: their free part is 0, whilst their logical part,
L2231@P47: pictured, has support contained in either a line or a column of
L2232@P47: one of the two grids of qubits. In Figure 2 we see a Z-operator
L2233@P47: whose free and logical part are both non trivial.
L2234@P47: Given a Z-operator (L, R) we define its row-column weight
L2235@P47: as
L2236@P47: Definition 1: Let (L, R) be any Z-operator on the physical
L2237@P47: qubits of the code C(δA,δB). Its row-column weight is the
L2238@P47: integer pair:
L2239@P47: wtrc(L, R) := (#row(L), #col(R))
L2240@P47: where
L2241@P47: row(L) := {Li row of L : Li =0},
L2242@P47: col(R) := {Rj column of R : Rj =0},
L2243@P47: and the hash symbol # denotes the cardinality of a set.
L2244@P47: The primary significance of this novel notion of weight is
L2245@P47: explained by Proposition 2, which also represents a key result
L2246@P47: towards the construction of the ReShape decoder.
L2247@P47: Proposition 2: If (L, R) is a non-trivial logical Z-operator
L2248@P47: of C(δA,δB) then either #row(L) ≥ da or #col(R) ≥ dT
L2249@P47: b
L2250@P47: (or both).
L2251@P47: Corollary 1 below further specifies the structure of logical Z-operators and it is easily derived from the proof of
L2252@P47: Proposition 2, which is deferred to Appendix C.
L2253@P47: Fig. 2. A graphical representation of a Z operator on the physical qubits
L2254@P47: of the code C(δA,δB) also represented in Figure 1. The filled circles in (2a)
L2255@P47: represent the support of the operator: its Hamming weight is 23 while its
L2256@P47: row-column weight is (4, 4), see Definition 1. The operator in (2a) can be
L2257@P47: written as a sum of its free and its logical parts represented in (2b) and (2c),
L2258@P47: see Proposition (1). As per Definition 2 and figure (2c), the logical row-column
L2259@P47: weight of the operator in (2a) is (1, 0).
L2260@P47: Corollary 1: If (L, R) is a non-trivial logical Z-operator on
L2261@P47: C(δA,δB), at least one of the following hold:
L2262@P47: (i) L has at least da rows which are not in Im δT
L2263@P47: B when seen
L2264@P47: as vectors in nb
L2265@P47: 2 .
L2266@P47: (ii) R has at least dT
L2267@P47: b columns which are not in Im δA when
L2268@P47: seen as vectors of ma
L2269@P47: 2 .
L2270@P47: Proposition 2 and Corollary 1 naturally yield
L2271@P47: Definition 2: Let (L, R) be a Z-operator on C(δA,δB).
L2272@P47: Its logical row-column weight is the integer pair
L2273@P47: wtlog
L2274@P47: rc (L, R) := (#rowlog(L), #collog(R))
L2275@P47: where
L2276@P47: rowlog(L) := {Li row of L : Li ∈ Im δT
L2277@P47: B},
L2278@P47: collog(R) := {Rj column of R : Rj ∈ Im δA}.
L2279@P47: Equivalently, if (L, R) has canonical form given by
L2280@P47: (L, R)=(OL + ML,OR + MR),
L2281@P47: for its logical row-column weight holds:
L2282@P47: wtlog
L2283@P47: rc (L, R) = wtrc(OL,OR).
L2284@P47: The pivotal property of the logical row-column weight is
L2285@P47: expressed by Proposition 3.
L2286@P47: Proposition 3: The logical row-column weight of a
L2287@P47: Z-operator on C(δA,δB) is an invariant of its homology class.
L2288@P47: Proposition 3 not only justifies the introduction of the notion
L2289@P47: of logical row-column weight but also constitutes the core
L2290@P47: resource upon which we prove the correctness of the ReShape
L2291@P47: decoder, which we now introduce.
L2292@P47-48: Authorized licensed use limited to: Sheffield University. Downloaded on September 21,2022 at 17:03:24 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Restrictions apply.QUINTAVALLE AND CAMPBELL: ReSHAPE: A DECODER FOR HYPERGRAPH PRODUCT CODES 6575
L2293@P48: B. The ReShape Decoder
L2294@P48: An hypergraph product code C(δA,δB) is a CSS code and as
L2295@P48: such the decoding for X and Z error can be treated separately
L2296@P48: but in a symmetric way. Here we focus on Z-errors and
L2297@P48: therefore we measure a generating set of X-stabilisers. The
L2298@P48: Z-error decoding problem for C(δA,δB) can be stated as:
L2299@P48: given a ma × nb syndrome matrix S, find a valid and correct
L2300@P48: solution (L, ˜ R˜) to the equation:
L2301@P48: S = σ(L, R) := δAL + RδB, (SE)
L2302@P48: where (L, ˜ R˜) is valid if σ(L, ˜ R˜)= S and it is correct if it
L2303@P48: belongs to the homology class of the minimum weight operator
L2304@P48: with syndrome S. Crucially, finding a valid solution (L, R)
L2305@P48: to Eq. (SE) is always possible by solving the linear system of
L2306@P48: equation where the parity check matrix of X stabilisers is the
L2307@P48: matrix of coefficients and the syndrome S is the constant term.
L2308@P48: The difficulties arise if we are interest in finding a correct
L2309@P48: solution to Eq. (SE).
L2310@P48: The ReShape decoder for Z-errors is build upon two classical minimum weight decoding algorithms: DδA and DδT
L2311@P48: B .
L2312@P48: By this we mean that (i) the algorithms DδA and DδT
L2313@P48: B are
L2314@P48: optimal decoders for the classical linear code with parity
L2315@P48: check matrix δA and δT
L2316@P48: B respectively, and (ii) they solve the
L2317@P48: classical decoding problem of Eq. (2) for errors of weight up to
L2318@P48: (da−1)/2 and (dT
L2319@P48: b −1)/2 respectively. Reshape takes as input
L2320@P48: DδA , DδT
L2321@P48: B , a syndrome matrix S and a valid solution (L, R)
L2322@P48: of the Syndrome Equation (SE): σ(L, R)= S. Recall that a
L2323@P48: valid solution (L, R) can always be efficiently found either
L2324@P48: solving the associated linear system or querying a lookup
L2325@P48: table. It outputs a correct solution of (SE): an operator (L, ˜ R˜)
L2326@P48: homologically equivalent to the minimum weight operator
L2327@P48: (Lmin,Rmin) with syndrome S.
L2328@P48: ReShape (Algorithm 1) works separately on the left part L
L2329@P48: and on the right part R of the operator (L, R) and in fact
L2330@P48: it could be run in parallel (lines 1-10 and lines 11-20).
L2331@P48: Starting from a valid solution (L, R), it minimises its logical
L2332@P48: row-column weight by minimizing #rowlog(L) (lines 1-10)
L2333@P48: first and #collog(R) after (lines 11-20). Because the logical
L2334@P48: row-column weight is an homology invariant for Z-operators
L2335@P48: (Proposition 3) and ReShape minimises it, this suffices to
L2336@P48: assure that ReShape is correct, as stated in Proposition 4.
L2337@P48: ReShape works on the left part L of the inputted valid solution
L2338@P48: (L, R) (lines 1-10) into two steps: Decode and Split. Each
L2339@P48: of these two steps exploits a characteristic feature of the
L2340@P48: Z-operators on the codespace of C(δA,δB):
L2341@P48: (i) Split step: a Z-stabilizer (GL,GR) has left part GL such
L2342@P48: that every row is in the image of δT
L2343@P48: B;
L2344@P48: (ii) Decode step: a logical Z-operator which acts non-trivially
L2345@P48: on the left qubits has a representative (Lz,Rz) such that
L2346@P48: at least one column of Lz is in ker δA \{0}.
L2347@P48: The Split and Decode steps are similarly performed on the
L2348@P48: right part R, as specified in lines 11 - 20 of the pseudocode
L2349@P48: in Algorithm 1. Again with reference to the left part as guide
L2350@P48: case, we now describe the Split and Decode steps in details and
L2351@P48: specify their computational cost. By extending this analysis
L2352@P48: to the right part, and thanks to Proposition 4, Theorem 1 is
L2353@P48: proved.
L2354@P48: Algorithm 1 ReShape Decoder for Z-Errors
L2355@P48: Input: Classical decoder DδA and DδT
L2356@P48: B . Syndrome matrix S
L2357@P48: and operator (L, R) on C(δA,δB) s.t. σ(L, R)= S.
L2358@P48: Output: Operator (L, ˜ R˜) on C(δA,δB) s.t. σ(L, ˜ R˜)= S and
L2359@P48: [L, ˜ R˜]=[Lmin,Rmin], where (Lmin,Rmin) is a minimum
L2360@P48: weight operator with syndrome S.
L2361@P48: 1: for all Li rows of L do
L2362@P48: 2: Split: Li = mi + μi ∈ Im δT
L2363@P48: B ⊕ (Im δTB)•, as in (16)
L2364@P48: 3: end for
L2365@P48: 4: ML ← matrix whose rows are mi
L2366@P48: 5: OL ← matrix whose rows are μi
L2367@P48: 6: for all Oj
L2368@P48: L columns of OL do
L2369@P48: 7: Decode: ρj = DδA (Oj
L2370@P48: L)
L2371@P48: 8: end for
L2372@P48: 9: L˜ ← matrix whose columns are ρj
L2373@P48: 10: L˜ ← L˜ + OL + ML
L2374@P48: 11: for all Rj columns of R do
L2375@P48: 12: Split: Rj = mj + μj ∈ Im δA ⊕ (Im δA)•, as in (16)
L2376@P48: 13: end for
L2377@P48: 14: MR ← matrix whose columns are mj
L2378@P48: 15: OR ← matrix whose columns are μj
L2379@P48: 16: for all (OR)i rows of OR do
L2380@P48: 17: Decode: ρi = DδT
L2381@P48: B ((OR)i)
L2382@P48: 18: end for
L2383@P48: 19: R˜ ← matrix whose rows are ρi
L2384@P48: 20: R˜ ← R˜ + RL + MR
L2385@P48: 21: return (L, ˜ R˜)
L2386@P48: Let (L, R) be any valid solution of (SE) given in input to
L2387@P48: ReShape.
L2388@P48: (i) Split. First, in lines 1-3, L is written in its canonical
L2389@P48: form with respect to the basis described by Eq. (16):
L2390@P48: L = ML + OL.
L2391@P48: This operation has the cost of a change of basis over the
L2392@P48: vector space nb
L2393@P48: 2 , namely from the canonical basis to the basis
L2394@P48: described by Eq. (16). A change of basis over a vector space
L2395@P48: is a linear operation that correspond to a multiplication by
L2396@P48: an invertible square matrix. Since we are interested in computing the image of this linear transformation for each of the
L2397@P48: na column vectors of L, this amount to the multiplication of
L2398@P48: an na × nb and a nb × nb matrix. To sum up, the Split step
L2399@P48: of ReShape has cost O(nan2
L2400@P48: b ).
L2401@P48: (ii) Decode. The second step performed by ReShape
L2402@P48: (lines 6-10) aims to minimise the logical row-column
L2403@P48: weight of (L, R) by looking at non-homologically equivalent
L2404@P48: operators:
L2405@P48: (L, R)+(Lz, 0),
L2406@P48: as Lz varies in Lleft
L2407@P48: z . More precisely, ReShape exploits the
L2408@P48: canonical form of L computed at the previous step and scans
L2409@P48: through all the columns of its logical part OL. By construction,
L2410@P48: any row (OL)i of OL belongs to the complement of Im δT
L2411@P48: B.
L2412@P48: For this reason, (OL)i can be written as the linear combination
L2413@P48-49: Authorized licensed use limited to: Sheffield University. Downloaded on September 21,2022 at 17:03:24 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Restrictions apply.6576 IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON INFORMATION THEORY, VOL. 68, NO. 10, OCTOBER 2022
L2414@P49: of kb = nb − rank(δB) unit vectors in nb
L2415@P49: 2 which does not
L2416@P49: belong to Im δT
L2417@P49: B i.e. kb unit vectors which span (Im δTB)•, see
L2418@P49: Appendix A. Importantly, when we stack these row vectors
L2419@P49: (OL)i all together and consider the columns of the matrix OL,
L2420@P49: we observe that OL cannot have more than kb non-zero
L2421@P49: columns. Each non-zero column of OL is then treated as if it
L2422@P49: corresponded to a code-word of the classical code with parity
L2423@P49: check matrix δA (plus eventually a noise vector) and decoded
L2424@P49: individually using the classical minimum weight decoder DδA
L2425@P49: (line 7):
L2426@P49: DδA (Oj
L2427@P49: L)= ρj ⇐⇒ ρj ∈ arg min k∈ker δA
L2428@P49: (|k + Oj
L2429@P49: L|). (12)
L2430@P49: If the computational cost of the classical decoder DδA is
L2431@P49: O(ca), the computational cost of the second step of ReShape
L2432@P49: is O(kbca).
L2433@P49: The Split and Decode steps described for the left part are
L2434@P49: replicated, with opportune modifications, for the right part.
L2435@P49: To be exact, if one or both δA and δB are full rank, then the
L2436@P49: right part does not encode any logical operator so the algorithm
L2437@P49: terminates.2
L2438@P49: Proposition 4 below ensures that the recovery operator
L2439@P49: (L, ˜ R˜) found by ReShape is a correct solution of (SE), as long
L2440@P49: as the classical decoders DδA and DδT
L2441@P49: B succeed.
L2442@P49: Proposition 4: Let S be an X-syndrome matrix for
L2443@P49: C(δA,δB) and (L, R) any valid solution to the Syndrome
L2444@P49: Equation:
L2445@P49: σ(L, R)= S. (SE)
L2446@P49: Suppose that the minimum weight operator (Lmin,Rmin) with
L2447@P49: syndrome S has (da/2,dT
L2448@P49: b /2)-bounded logical row-column
L2449@P49: weight i.e.
L2450@P49: wtlog
L2451@P49: rc (Lmin,Rmin) = (#rowlog(Lmin), #collog(Rmin)),
L2452@P49: is such that
L2453@P49: #rowlog(Lmin) <
L2454@P49: da
L2455@P49: 2
L2456@P49: and #collog(Rmin) <
L2457@P49: dT
L2458@P49: b
L2459@P49: 2 . (13)
L2460@P49: Then, on input DδA , DδT
L2461@P49: B , S and (L, R), ReShape outputs a
L2462@P49: correct solution (L, ˜ R˜) of (SE), provided that the classical
L2463@P49: decoders DδA , DδT
L2464@P49: B succeed. In other words, the solution
L2465@P49: (L, ˜ R˜) found by ReShape is in the same homology class as
L2466@P49: the minimum weight operator with syndrome S:
L2467@P49: [Lmin,Rmin]=[L, ˜ R˜].
L2468@P49: It is important to note that the condition (13) on the weight
L2469@P49: of the original error is on its row-column weight, while usually
L2470@P49: decoding success is assessed depending on the weight of
L2471@P49: an operator, meaning the number of its non-identity factors.
L2472@P49: Obviously, for any operator (L, R) it holds:
L2473@P49: #row(L) ≤|L| and #col(R) ≤|R|.
L2474@P49: As a consequence, Proposition 4 entails that ReShape succeeds
L2475@P49: in correcting any Z-error of weight up to half the code distance
L2476@P49: dz = min{dA,dT
L2477@P49: B}. Combining this with the cost analysis of
L2478@P49: 2In fact, as per Eq. (9) and Eq. (10), if rank(δA)= ma or rank(δB )=
L2479@P49: mb, then ker δT
L2480@P49: A = (Im δA)• = {0} or ker δTB = (Im δB)• = {0} and so
L2481@P49: Lright
L2482@P49: x = Lrightz .
L2483@P49: the Split and Decode steps detailed above, gives a proof of
L2484@P49: Theorem 1.
L2485@P49: It is worth to observe that actually ReShape can correct
L2486@P49: errors of weight strictly bigger than half the code distance,
L2487@P49: as long as they are not too ‘spread’. In fact, whenever an error
L2488@P49: is homologically equivalent to an operator (L, R) such that
L2489@P49: L has ‘few’ non-zero rows and R has ‘few’ non-zero columns,
L2490@P49: ReShape succeeds. Formally, because by definition:
L2491@P49: #row(L) ≥ #rowlog(L) and #col(R) ≥ #collog(R).
L2492@P49: Proposition 4 yields
L2493@P49: Corollary 2: Provided that the classical decoders succeed, ReShape successfully corrects any Z-error (L, R) with
L2494@P49: bounded row-column weight:
L2495@P49: #row(L) <
L2496@P49: da
L2497@P49: 2 and #col(R) <
L2498@P49: dT
L2499@P49: b
L2500@P49: 2 .
L2501@P49: To sum up, ReShape successfully solves the decoding problem for any hypergraph product code requiring only k oracle
L2502@P49: calls to a classical decoder for the seed matrices, where k is the
L2503@P49: logical dimension of the code. Furthermore, it is able to correct
L2504@P49: for a vast class of errors of weight strictly bigger than half the
L2505@P49: code distance, provided that they have a ‘good’ shape. Here
L2506@P49: by ‘good’ we mean errors of low logical column-row weight
L2507@P49: but arbitrary Hamming weight as for instance the Z-operator
L2508@P49: pictured in Figure 2, that has Hamming weight 23 but logical
L2509@P49: row-column weight (1, 0) and would therefore be successfully
L2510@P49: corrected by the ReShape decoder.
L2511@P49: The next Section focuses on what happens when we cannot
L2512@P49: control the shape of the errors but we assume that the
L2513@P49: probability of a given error to occur decays exponentially in
L2514@P49: its weight.
L2515@P49: C. ReShape for Stochastic Noise
L2516@P49: Up till now, we have focused on the adversarial noise
L2517@P49: model: errors on qubits are always correctable because we
L2518@P49: assume they have weight less than half the code distance.
L2519@P49: In real systems though, this is rarely the case and it is
L2520@P49: more faithful to assume that errors are sampled accordingly
L2521@P49: to a local stochastic noise model, where qubits errors have
L2522@P49: arbitrary location but the probability of a given error decays
L2523@P49: exponentially in its weight [3]. More precisely the probability
L2524@P49: of a Pauli error E ∈Pn to occur is given by: (E)= p|E|
L2525@P49: (1 − p)
L2526@P49: n−|E|
L2527@P49: , (14)
L2528@P49: meaning that Pauli errors on each of the n qubits are
L2529@P49: independent and identically distributed. Under the binomial
L2530@P49: distribution associated to Eq. (14), the expected error weight
L2531@P49: on the encoded state is pn. Because the best possible distance
L2532@P49: scaling for the hypergraph product codes is ∼ √n (when
L2533@P49: the classical seed codes have linear distance), as n increases,
L2534@P49: we eventually find pn > √n/2 ∼ d/2. Nonetheless, it is
L2535@P49: well known that LDPC hypergraph product codes do have
L2536@P49: a positive error correcting threshold [2]. A family of codes
L2537@P49: has threshold pth > 0 if, for noise rate below pth, noncorrectable errors that destroy the logical information occur
L2538@P49-50: Authorized licensed use limited to: Sheffield University. Downloaded on September 21,2022 at 17:03:24 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Restrictions apply.QUINTAVALLE AND CAMPBELL: ReSHAPE: A DECODER FOR HYPERGRAPH PRODUCT CODES 6577
L2539@P50: Fig. 3. Graphical representation of one instance of ReShape for Z-errors. The code considered is the planar code of distance 3 (toric code with boundaries)
L2540@P50: or, equivalently, the [[13, 1, 3]] hypergraph product code C(δ, δ) for δ full-rank parity check matrix of the distance-3 repetition code i.e. leftmost matrix in (11).
L2541@P50: We use the same graphical representation used in Figure 1. A minimum weight logical Z operator for C(δ, δ) can be chosen to have support on all the
L2542@P50: qubits of a column of left qubits (Decode, bottom grid of qubits, support in red). The row span of δ consists of all vectors in 3
L2543@P50: 2 of even weight, hence for
L2544@P50: a generic Z-operator on C(δ, δ), all the rows of left qubits that have an even number of filled qubits belongs to its free part and do not contribute to its
L2545@P50: logical row-column weight. Since δ is full-rank, its column span is the whole space 2
L2546@P50: 2, and therefore a column displaying any choice of filled qubits is in
L2547@P50: the image of δT . As such, there is no contribution to the logical-row column weight from the right part of the operator. In particular, there is no need to run
L2548@P50: the ReShape decoder on the right part: Algorithm 1 will not execute lines 11 - 20.
L2549@P50: The figure is divided into to four sectors, one for each stage of the decoding cycle: Input, Split, Decode and Output.
L2550@P50: Input: the valid solution in input (L, R) has support represented by the black qubits. The operator (L, R) has Hamming weight 8.
L2551@P50: Split - Algorithm 1, lines 1 - 3: (L, R) is written as sum of its free part (ML,MR) (top); and its logical part (OL,OR) (bottom). For what said on the
L2552@P50: image of δ, the free part ML of L is a matrix whose rows have all even Hamming weight. Since OL has 2 non-zero rows, (L, R) has logical row-column
L2553@P50: weight wtlog
L2554@P50: rc (L, R)=2.
L2555@P50: Decode - Algorithm 1, lines 1 - 9: the non-zero column (0, 1, 1)T of the logical part OL of L is given in input to a decoder Dδ for the classical distance-3
L2556@P50: repetition code. The solution found is (1, 1, 1)T , represented by the single column of black bits on the top. This solution is plugged in the hypergraph product
L2557@P50: code and yields a logical operator correction represented by the operator at the bottom with support on the red qubits.
L2558@P50: Output - Algorithm 1, line 10: the output solution (L, ˜ R˜) is obtained by adding the input operator (L, R) and the operator found in the Decode step with
L2559@P50: support on the red qubits. The support of (L, ˜ R˜) is represented by the yellow qubits.
L2560@P50: We note that the solution found (L, ˜ R˜) has Hamming weight 7 and, by observing that only the first rows has odd weight, we deduce that its logical row-column
L2561@P50: weight is 1. It is easy to verify that (L, ˜ R˜) is indeed homologically equivalent to the minimum weight solution (Lˆ1,3, 0) where Lˆ1,3 ∈ 3×3
L2562@P50: 2 is the matrix
L2563@P50: with all zeros but for the (1, 3)-th entry which is 1. In fact, (L, ˜ R˜)=(Lˆ1,3, 0) + Sz(1, 1) + Sz(2, 1) + Sz(3, 1) thus, by (3), [L, ˜ R˜]=[Lˆ1,3, 0].
L2564@P50: with probability pnon−correctable which decays exponentially
L2565@P50: in the system size [2], [8], [33]:
L2566@P50: pnon−correctable ∝
L2567@P50: 	 p
L2568@P50: pth
L2569@P50: αdβ
L2570@P50: (15)
L2571@P50: for some α, β > 0. It is important to stress that Eq. (15)
L2572@P50: does not contrast with the fact that the typical error under the
L2573@P50: stochastic noise model will have weight pn. Instead, Eq. (15)
L2574@P50: entails that, among all the errors sampled, the non-correctable
L2575@P50: ones are only a small fraction. Beyond the theoretical threshold
L2576@P50: that Kovalev and Pryadko proved in [2], the literature offers
L2577@P50: several numerical evidence of decoders for hypergraph product or related families of codes which exhibit a threshold.
L2578@P50: Nonetheless these decoders either lack a correctness proof,
L2579@P50: e.g. BP in [10], [15], or need some additional constraints
L2580@P50: on the seed matrices, e.g. expander codes with small-set flip
L2581@P50: decoder [8], or augmented surface codes with the union-find
L2582@P50: decoder in [34].
L2583@P50: On the contrary, for any choice of the seed matrices in
L2584@P50: the hypergraph product, ReShape is provably correct for
L2585@P50: adversarial errors. Not surprisingly though, ReShape does not
L2586@P50: show a threshold and a possible intuition for its anti-threshold
L2587@P50: behaviour is the following.
L2588@P50: If we contrast Reshape with pairs of LDPC codes families
L2589@P50: and decoders which exhibit a threshold, such as expander
L2590@P50: codes with the small-set flip decoder [8] or hypergraph product
L2591@P50: codes with BP [10], [15], a feature of difference is the
L2592@P50: ‘locality’ of the decoding algorithms. Loosely, we say that a
L2593@P50: decoding algorithm is local if errors affecting distant regions
L2594@P50: on the qubit graph are dealt with separately and independently.
L2595@P50: We stress that a decoder’s locality is a feature of the algorithm
L2596@P50: and it is not related to the locality of the code’s stabiliser
L2597@P50: generators. A code can have local stabilisers, meaning that
L2598@P50: for a given layout of qubits in the space, stabiliser generators
L2599@P50: only involve qubits in a limited area, and yet be equipped with
L2600@P50: a non-local decoding algorithm. Indeed, ReShape is such a
L2601@P50: decoder. It is a non-local decoder that can be used on the very
L2602@P50: much local planar code. Locality of the decoding algorithm
L2603@P50: is relevant because local stochastic errors tend to form small
L2604@P50: disjoint clusters on the qubit graph which do not destroy
L2605@P50: the logical information as long as they are (1) small enough
L2606@P50: (2) sufficiently far apart. Therefore, if a decoder manages
L2607@P50: to mimic the error cluster distribution on the qubit graph
L2608@P50: and finds recovery operators accordingly, then it is likely
L2609@P50: to preserve the logical information and show a threshold.
L2610@P50: ReShape, on the other hand, has a deeply global nature.
L2611@P50-51: Authorized licensed use limited to: Sheffield University. Downloaded on September 21,2022 at 17:03:24 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Restrictions apply.6578 IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON INFORMATION THEORY, VOL. 68, NO. 10, OCTOBER 2022
L2612@P51: Fig. 4. Anti-threshold behaviour of ReShape on two families of hypergraph product codes. For both families, we plot the failure probability pfail as a
L2613@P51: function of the phase-flip noise rate p for codes with Z-distance d. All data points are generated with at least 104 Monte Carlo trials. (a) and (c): The toric
L2614@P51: code without boundaries. (b) and (d): A family of hypergraph product codes C(δ, δT ) where δ is a full-rank (3, 4)-matrix randomly generated, see [10].
L2615@P51: The Split step groups all the clusters of flipped qubits
L2616@P51: scattered across the qubit graph in a small pre-assigned
L2617@P51: region; a recovery is then chosen (Decode step) based on
L2618@P51: the syndrome information in this pre-assigned region. If we
L2619@P51: take the planar code as an example (see Figure 3), the
L2620@P51: Split step groups the error (and the syndrome) weight on
L2621@P51: one column of the left qubits. The subsequent Decode step
L2622@P51: decodes that column and finds a recovery operator with
L2623@P51: supported on the column. Because for the planar code a logical
L2624@P51: Z-operator can be chosen to have support on only one column
L2625@P51: of the left qubits, this procedure can easily destroy the logical
L2626@P51: information.
L2627@P51: Our intuition on the performance of ReShape under stochastic noise finds confirmation in the plots reported in Figure 4.
L2628@P51: Even if at first sight the plots in Figure 4a and 4b could
L2629@P51: indicate the presence of a very low threshold (below 1%),
L2630@P51: a closer analysis suggests that this is not the case. In fact,
L2631@P51: as d increases, the crossing point between the dashed curve
L2632@P51: labelled d =0 and the d-curves slips leftwards. Since
L2633@P51: the dashed curve is the locus of points where the failure
L2634@P51: probability pfail equates the noise rate p, it corresponds to
L2635@P51: the case of no encoding i.e. d =0. The common crossing
L2636@P51: point, in other words, represents the pseudo-threshold of the
L2637@P51: code [35]. Importantly, if a code family has a threshold pth
L2638@P51: in the sense of Eq. (15), then all the codes of the family
L2639@P51: crosses the curve d =0 at the same point of coordinates
L2640@P51: (pth,pth). Figure 4c clearly illustrates this left slipping phenomenon for the toric codes without boundaries. For close
L2641@P51: distances d =6, 8, 10, there it seems to be a common crossing
L2642@P51: point with the d =0 curve. However, the crossing point
L2643@P51: lowers if we increase d more substantially, e.g. d = 20.
L2644@P51: The situation appears less clear in Figure 4d because the
L2645@P51: pseudo-threshold seems to increase with the distance of
L2646@P51: the code. Still though, there is no common crossing point of the
L2647@P51: three curves; besides, we would expect the same trend as
L2648@P51: the one observed for the toric codes if codes of bigger distance
L2649@P51: were considered.
L2650@P51: In conclusion, ReShape is not suited to tackle stochastic
L2651@P51: errors on [[n, k, d]] code in the regime where typical errors
L2652@P51: have weight exceeding d/2.
L2653@P51-52: Authorized licensed use limited to: Sheffield University. Downloaded on September 21,2022 at 17:03:24 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Restrictions apply.QUINTAVALLE AND CAMPBELL: ReSHAPE: A DECODER FOR HYPERGRAPH PRODUCT CODES 6579
L2654@P52: V. CONCLUSION AND OUTLOOK
L2655@P52: In this paper we determined some important homology
L2656@P52: invariants of hypergraph product codes. Exploiting these
L2657@P52: invariants, we designed the ReShape decoder. ReShape is the
L2658@P52: first decoder to efficiently decode for all errors up to half
L2659@P52: the code distance, across the whole spectrum of hypergraph
L2660@P52: product codes.
L2661@P52: We foresee two natural extensions of this work. The first is
L2662@P52: to adapt ReShape for it to work in the stochastic noise model
L2663@P52: settings. Because ReShape actually succeeds in correcting
L2664@P52: errors of weight substantially bigger than (d − 1)/2 (namely
L2665@P52: it corrects error of weight as big as ∼ d2, when they have the
L2666@P52: right shape!), this gives us some hope that ReShape would
L2667@P52: work under stochastic noise if paired with the right clustering
L2668@P52: technique. For instance, something on the line of the clustering
L2669@P52: methods used in the renormalisation group or the union-find
L2670@P52: decoders [5], [18], [36], [37].
L2671@P52: The second is to find the corresponding invariants for
L2672@P52: other families of homological product codes. Specifically, for
L2673@P52: the codes in [38], which have ‘rectangular’ shaped logical
L2674@P52: operators instead of ‘string’ like as the standard hypergraph
L2675@P52: product codes here studied; or the balanced product codes
L2676@P52: proposed in [39]. Once found, the right invariants could be
L2677@P52: plugged-in an appropriately modified version of ReShape and
L2678@P52: yield a provable correct decoder for these class of codes too.

L2747@P52: APPENDIX B
L2748@P52: HYPERGRAPH PRODUCT CODES
L2749@P52: CSS codes can be easily described in terms of homology
L2750@P52: theory [31], [42], [43] via the identification of the objects
L2751@P52: of the code with a chain complex [44]. For our purposes,
L2752@P52: a length  chain complex is an object described by a sequence
L2753@P52: of  +1 vector spaces {Ci}i over 2 and  binary matrices
L2754@P52: {∂i : Ci −→ Ci+1}i such that, for each i, ∂i∂i−1 =0. In the
L2755@P52: following, we use the symbol ∂ to indicate the maps of a chain
L2756@P52: complex of length > 1 and the symbol δ to indicate the map
L2757@P52: of a chain complex of length 1. Given a chain complex C:
L2758@P52: C−1
L2759@P52: ∂−1
L2760@P52: −−→C0
L2761@P52: ∂0 −→C1, (C)
L2762@P52: we can define a CSS code C by equating:
L2763@P52: HZ = ∂T
L2764@P52: −1,HX = ∂0.
L2765@P52: Since ∂0∂−1 =0 by construction, X-type and Z-type operators do commute i.e. HX · HT
L2766@P52: Z =0 and the code C associated
L2767@P52: to the chain complex (C) is well defined. The code C has
L2768@P52: length n = dim(C0) and its dimension k equates to the dimension of the 0th homology group H0 = ker ∂0/ Im ∂−1 or,
L2769@P52: equivalently, to the dimension of the 0th co-homology group
L2770@P52: H∗
L2771@P52: 0 = ker ∂−1/ Im ∂0. Its Z-distance and X-distance are given
L2772@P52: by the minimum Hamming weight of any representative of a
L2773@P52: non-zero element in H0 and H∗
L2774@P52: 0 respectively:
L2775@P52: dz = min v∈n
L2776@P52: 2
L2777@P52: {|v| :[v] ∈H0, [v] =0},
L2778@P52: dx = min v∈n
L2779@P52: 2
L2780@P52: {|v| :[v] ∈H∗
L2781@P52: 0, [v] =0}.
L2782@P52: An hypergraph product code C(δA,δB), which is a CSS code,
L2783@P52: can be easily defined in terms of product of chain complexes.

L2785@P52: matrices δA and δB:

L2797@P52: and the direct sum space C0 = C0
L2798@P52: A ⊗ C0B ⊕ C1A ⊗ C1B.

L2824@P53: A. Reshaping of Vectors
L2825@P53: One tool we make extensive use of, and from which
L2826@P53: our decoder takes its name, is the reshaping of vectors
L2827@P53: of a two-fold tensor product space into matrices (see, for
L2828@P53: instance, [43], [45]). Consider a basis B of the vector space
L2829@P53: n1
L2830@P53: 2 ⊗ n22 :
L2831@P53: B = {ai ⊗ bj | i =1,...,n1 and j =1,...,n2}.
L2832@P53: Then any v ∈ n1
L2833@P53: 2 ⊗ n22 can be written as:
L2834@P53: v = 
L2835@P53: ai⊗bj∈B
L2836@P53: vij (ai ⊗ bj ),
L2837@P53: for some vij ∈ 2. We call the n1 × n2 matrix V with entries
L2838@P53: vij the reshaping of the vector v. By this identification, if ϕ,
L2839@P53: θ are respectively m1 × n1 and m2 × n2 matrices, then (ϕ ⊗
L2840@P53: θ)(V )= ϕV θT . The inner product between u ⊗ w and v in
L2841@P53: n1
L2842@P53: 2 ⊗ n22 can be computed as
L2843@P53: u ⊗ w, v	 = uT V w. (18)
L2844@P53: As we here detail, the identification of operators on the code
L2845@P53: space C(δA,δB) with pairs of binary matrices that we used in
L2846@P53: the main text is rigorously justified by the reshaping of vectors
L2847@P53: into matrices. With slight abuse of notation, we refer to binary
L2848@P53: vectors and binary matrices as operators and vice versa, where
L2849@P53: the identification is clear via Eq. (8).
L2850@P53: B. Graphical Representation
L2851@P53: Physical qubits of the code C(δA,δB) are in one-toone correspondence with basis elements of the space C0.
L2852@P53: If {eja }ja , {ejb }jb , {eia }ia , {eib }ib are bases of the spaces
L2853@P53: C0
L2854@P53: A,C0B,C1A,C1B of dimension na,nb,ma,mb respectively,
L2855@P53: then the union of the two sets
L2856@P53: BL := {(eja ⊗ ejb , 0)}
L2857@P53: BR := {(0,eia ⊗ eib )}
L2858@P53: is a basis of C0. We refer to qubits associated to elements
L2859@P53: in BL, or its span, as left qubits and to those associated
L2860@P53: to BR, or its span, as right qubits. Since qubit operators are
L2861@P53: vectors in C0, by reshaping, they can be identified with pairs
L2862@P53: of matrices (L, R) where L has size na × nb and R has size
L2863@P53: ma × mb; in particular, L acts on the left qubits while R acts
L2864@P53: on the right qubits.
L2865@P53: A Z-stabilizer for the code associated to the complex CA,B
L2866@P53: is any vector in Im ∂−1. A generating set for Z-stabilizers is:
L2867@P53: Sz := {∂−1(eja ⊗ eib )}ja,ib
L2868@P53: where eja and eib are unit vectors of C0
L2869@P53: A and C1B respectively,
L2870@P53: i.e. they are a basis of the two spaces. Let Ejaib ∈ C0
L2871@P53: A ⊗C1B be
L2872@P53: the reshaping of (eja ⊗ eib ), i.e. it is the matrix with all zeros
L2873@P53: entries but for the (ja,ib)-th entry which is 1. The reshape of
L2874@P53: ∂−1(eja ⊗ eib ) is then given by the pair of matrices:
L2875@P53: (L, R)=(Ejaib δB,δAEjaib ).
L2876@P53: Logical Z-operators are vectors in ker ∂0 which are not
L2877@P53: in Im ∂−1. Specifically, a minimal generating set of logical

L2906@P53: APPENDIX C
L2907@P53: PROOFS
L2908@P53: This Section contains all the proofs of the statements made
L2909@P53: in the main text.
L2910@P53: Broadly speaking, in this work we wanted to characterize
L2911@P53: Z-errors operators on the codespace of C(δA,δB) associated
L2912@P53: to the chain complex CA,B. In order to do so, we first
L2913@P53: studied the logical Z-operators of C(δA,δB) and introduced
L2914@P53: a canonical form for them. From homology theory, we know
L2915@P53: that non-trivial logical Z-operators are associated to vectors
L2916@P53: in ker ∂0 which do not belong to Im ∂−1. Lemma 1 below
L2917@P53: describes all the vectors in ker ∂0.
L2918@P53: Lemma 1: Let (L, R) ∈C0 be in ker ∂0, then:
L2919@P53: L ∈ ker δA ⊗ C0
L2920@P53: B + C0A ⊗ Im δTB,
L2921@P53: R ∈ C1
L2922@P53: A ⊗ ker δTB + Im δA ⊗ C1B.
L2923@P53-54: Authorized licensed use limited to: Sheffield University. Downloaded on September 21,2022 at 17:03:24 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Restrictions apply.QUINTAVALLE AND CAMPBELL: ReSHAPE: A DECODER FOR HYPERGRAPH PRODUCT CODES 6581
L2924@P54: Proof: Let (L, R) ∈ ker ∂0. Then:
L2925@P54: ∂0(L, R)=0 ⇐⇒ (δA ⊗ )L +( ⊗ δT
L2926@P54: B)R =0,
L2927@P54: ⇐⇒ δAL + RδB =0. (20)
L2928@P54: Eq. (20) yields:
L2929@P54: δAL = RδB = V, (21)
L2930@P54: for some V ∈ C1
L2931@P54: A ⊗ C0B. Eq. (21) entails that all columns
L2932@P54: of V belong to Im δA while its rows belong to Im δT
L2933@P54: B. As a
L2934@P54: consequence, it must exists U ∈ C0
L2935@P54: A ⊗ C1B such that:
L2936@P54: V = δAUδB.
L2937@P54: Therefore Eq. (21) can be re-written as:
L2938@P54: δAL = δAUδB
L2939@P54: which yields:
L2940@P54: (δA ⊗ )(L + UδB)= δAL + δAUδB
L2941@P54: = V + V
L2942@P54: =0 (22)
L2943@P54: Equivalently, Eq. (22) states that L + UδB has columns in
L2944@P54: ker δA:
L2945@P54: L + UδB ∈ ker δA ⊗ C0
L2946@P54: B
L2947@P54: and therefore:
L2948@P54: L ∈ ker δA ⊗ C0
L2949@P54: B + C0A ⊗ Im δTB,
L2950@P54: as in the thesis. Similarly, we find
L2951@P54: R ∈ C1
L2952@P54: A ⊗ ker δTB + Im δA ⊗ C1B.
L2953@P54: A proof of Proposition 1, reported below for clarity, follows
L2954@P54: directly combining what said in Appendix B-A and Lemma 1.
L2955@P54: Proposition 1 (Canonical Form): Let (L, R) be a
L2956@P54: Z-operator on the codespace of C(δA,δB). For a vector space
L2957@P54: V ⊆ n
L2958@P54: 2 , we denote by V • any space such that V ⊕V •  n2 ,
L2959@P54: (see Appendix A). Then, for the operator (L, R), the left part L
L2960@P54: can be expressed as a sum of a free part ML and a logical
L2961@P54: part OL such that every row of ML belongs to Im δT
L2962@P54: B and
L2963@P54: every row of OL belongs to (Im δT
L2964@P54: B)•. Similarly, the right
L2965@P54: part R can be expressed as a sum of a free part MR and a
L2966@P54: logical part OR such that every column of MR belongs to
L2967@P54: Im δA and every column of OR belongs to (Im δA)•. Hence,
L2968@P54: for (L, R) holds:
L2969@P54: (L, R)=(ML + OL,MR + OR). (CF)
L2970@P54: We refer to the writing given by Eq. (CF) as canonical form
L2971@P54: of the operator (L, R).
L2972@P54: In the main text, we have introduced the notions of
L2973@P54: row-column weight and logical row-column weight for a
L2974@P54: Z-operator on C(δA,δB). The definition of these two quantities finds its explanation in Proposition 2, whose proof builds
L2975@P54: on the results of Lemma 1.
L2976@P54: Proposition 2: If (L, R) is a non-trivial logical Z-operator
L2977@P54: of C(δA,δB) then either #row(L) ≥ da or #col(R) ≥ dT
L2978@P54: b
L2979@P54: (or both).
L2980@P54: Proof: If (L, R) is a non-trivial logical Z-operator, it must
L2981@P54: anti-commute with at least one logical X-operator (Lx,Rx).
L2982@P54: Because a Z-operator and a X-operator anti-commute if and
L2983@P54: only if their supports overlap on an odd number of positions,
L2984@P54: either L and Lx or R and Rx have odd overlap. Without loss
L2985@P54: of generality, we can assume that the former is verified and
L2986@P54: we can choose (Lx,Rx) as a left operator of the form
L2987@P54: (Lx,Rx)=(f ⊗ k, 0),
L2988@P54: where f is a unit vector in (Im δT
L2989@P54: A)• and k ∈ ker δB. In other
L2990@P54: words, we choose logical X-operator (f ⊗ k, 0) from the set
L2991@P54: of generators of X-logical operators Lˆleft
L2992@P54: x , as in the X-version
L2993@P54: of Eq. (19). The inner product equation for reshaped vectors
L2994@P54: Eq. (18) then yields:
L2995@P54: 1= (Lx, 0),(L, R)
L2996@P54: 
L2997@P54: = Lx,L	 + 0,R
L2998@P54: = fTLk. (23)
L2999@P54: Now, observe that (L, R) ∈C0 is a non-trivial logical
L3000@P54: Z-operator of C(δA,δB) if and only if [L, R] ∈H0 =
L3001@P54: ker ∂0/ Im ∂−1 and [L, R] =0 or, equivalently, if and only
L3002@P54: if:
L3003@P54: (L, R) ∈ ker ∂0 \ Im ∂−1.
L3004@P54: In particular, (L, R) belongs to ker ∂0 and thanks to Lemma 1,
L3005@P54: we can re-write it as:
L3006@P54: (L, R)=(KA + ULδB, K¯B + δAUR),
L3007@P54: where columns of KA belong to ker δA and rows of K¯B
L3008@P54: belong to ker δT
L3009@P54: B. Using Lemma 1’s decomposition for
L3010@P54: (L, R) ∈ ker ∂0, we can expand the matrix-vector product Lk
L3011@P54: as:
L3012@P54: Lk =(KA + ULδB)k
L3013@P54: = KAk + ULδBk
L3014@P54: = KAk since k ∈ ker δB. (24)
L3015@P54: Eq. (24) entails Lk = KAk and therefore that Lk, being a
L3016@P54: linear combination of column-vectors in ker δA, belongs to
L3017@P54: ker δA itself. Furthermore, by Eq. (23), Lk =0. To sum up,
L3018@P54: Lk is a non-zero vector in ker δA and therefore it must have
L3019@P54: Hamming weight at least da. As a consequence, L is a matrix
L3020@P54: with at least da rows:
L3021@P54: #row(L) ≥ da.
L3022@P54: Similarly, we would have found:
L3023@P54: #col(R) ≥ dT
L3024@P54: b ,
L3025@P54: if we had assumed that (L, R) anti-commuted with a logical
L3026@P54: X-operator (0,Rx) in Lˆright
L3027@P54: x .
L3028@P54: Corollary 1 follows easily.
L3029@P54: Corollary 1: If (L, R) is a non-trivial logical Z-operator on
L3030@P54: C(δA,δB), at least one of the following hold:
L3031@P54: (i) L has at least da rows which are not in Im δT
L3032@P54: B when seen
L3033@P54: as vectors in C0
L3034@P54: B.
L3035@P54: (ii) R has at least dT
L3036@P54: b columns which are not in Im δA when
L3037@P54: seen as vectors of C1
L3038@P54: A.
L3039@P54-55: Authorized licensed use limited to: Sheffield University. Downloaded on September 21,2022 at 17:03:24 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Restrictions apply.6582 IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON INFORMATION THEORY, VOL. 68, NO. 10, OCTOBER 2022
L3040@P55: Proof: Write (L, R) in its canonical form:
L3041@P55: (L, R)=(ML + OL,MR + OR),
L3042@P55: and let
L3043@P55: ML = NLδB,
L3044@P55: MR = δANR,
L3045@P55: for some binary matrices NL,NR of size na × mb. As done
L3046@P55: in the proof of Proposition 2, consider a logical X-operator
L3047@P55: (f ⊗ k, 0) such that it anti-commutes with (L, R). Combining
L3048@P55: the canonical form of L and Eq. (24), yields:
L3049@P55: Lk =(OL + NLδB)k
L3050@P55: = OLk since k ∈ ker δB
L3051@P55: = KAk by Eq. (24)
L3052@P55: for some na × nb matrix KA with columns in ker δA. By the
L3053@P55: same argument used in the proof of Proposition 2, we find:
L3054@P55: |KAk|≥ dA ⇒|OLk|≥ dA,
L3055@P55: and in particular that OL has at least da non-zero rows. Since
L3056@P55: by definition of canonical form the non-zero rows of OL are
L3057@P55: precisely those rows of L which do not belong to Im δT
L3058@P55: B,
L3059@P55: we have proven point (i). Point (ii) follows similarly in the case
L3060@P55: (L, R) anti-commutes with at least one logical X-operator of
L3061@P55: the form (0,Rx).
L3062@P55: Corollary 1, together with Proposition 3 below, justifies the
L3063@P55: definition of the logical row-column weight for Z-operators
L3064@P55: on C(δA,δB) (Definition 2). The logical row-column weight
L3065@P55: of (L, R) is denoted by the symbol wtlog
L3066@P55: rc (L, R) and stands for
L3067@P55: the integer pair (#rowlog(L), #collog(R)) where #rowlog(L)
L3068@P55: is the number of rows of L that are not in Im δT
L3069@P55: B and
L3070@P55: #collog(R) is the number of columns of R which are not
L3071@P55: in Im δA. Proposition 3, that we now prove, states that the
L3072@P55: logical row-column weight of a Z-operator on C(δA,δB) is an
L3073@P55: homology invariant of the chain complex CA,B and therefore
L3074@P55: it legitimates the name choice for this quantity.
L3075@P55: Proposition 3: The logical row-column weight of a
L3076@P55: Z-operator on C(δA,δB) is an invariant of its homology class.
L3077@P55: Proof: Let [L, R] be the homology class of (L, R):
L3078@P55: [L, R]= (L + GL,R + GR):(GL,GR)
L3079@P55: is a Z-stabiliser.
L3080@P55: The operator (GL,GR) ∈C0 is a Z-stabilizer for C(δA,δB)
L3081@P55: if and only if
L3082@P55: (GL,GR)= ∂−1(U)
L3083@P55: =(UδB,δAU) (25)
L3084@P55: For some na × mb binary matrix U. Eq. (25) entails that any
L3085@P55: row of GL belongs to Im δT
L3086@P55: B and any column of GR belongs
L3087@P55: to Im δA. Therefore, if we write (L, R) in its canonical form:
L3088@P55: (L, R)=(ML + OL,MR + OR),
L3089@P55: we see that we can ‘delete’ all the rows of ML by adding a
L3090@P55: stabiliser and hence ‘move’ part of the support of the operator
L3091@P55: (L, R) from the left qubits to the right qubits. Specifically,
L3092@P55: if ML = NLδB for some na × mb binary matrix NL,
L3093@P55: we consider the stabiliser G =(NLδB,δANL) and we obtain:
L3094@P55: (L, R)+ G =(OL,MR + OR + δANL).
L3095@P55: Similarly, we could move the MR part of the operator (L, R)
L3096@P55: from the right qubits to the left qubits, by adding the stabilizer
L3097@P55: G =(NRδB,δANR), for a na × mb matrix NR such that
L3098@P55: MR = δANR.
L3099@P55: On the other hand though, it is not possible to delete
L3100@P55: non-zero rows of OL via stabiliser addition. In other words,
L3101@P55: it is not possible to remove, via stabiliser addition, any of
L3102@P55: the rows of L that are not in Im δT
L3103@P55: B. Hence, the number
L3104@P55: #rowlog(L) of non-zero rows of OL is an homology invariant.
L3105@P55: Likewise, we find that it is not possible to delete any column in
L3106@P55: OR by adding stabilisers and therefore #collog(R) is a logical
L3107@P55: invariant too.
L3108@P55: The proof of Proposition 3 actually entails a stronger result
L3109@P55: than the invariance of the row-column weight of Z-operators
L3110@P55: on C(δA,δB). Namely, we have proven that the indices of the
L3111@P55: rows and the columns in the sets rowlog and collog respectively,
L3112@P55: are homology invariants of the reshaped Z-operators (L, R)
L3113@P55: on C(δA,δB). However, because to prove the correctness of
L3114@P55: ReShape it is sufficient to look at the cardinality of the two
L3115@P55: sets rowlog and collog, we decided to state Proposition 3 in
L3116@P55: this more compact and elegant form.
L3117@P55: We can now prove Proposition 4.
L3118@P55: Proposition 4: Let S be a X-syndrome matrix for C(δA,δB)
L3119@P55: and (L, R) any valid solution to the Syndrome Equation
L3120@P55: σ(L, R)= S. (SE)
L3121@P55: Suppose that the minimum weight operator (Lmin,Rmin) with
L3122@P55: syndrome S has (da/2,dT
L3123@P55: b /2)-bounded logical row-column
L3124@P55: weight i.e.
L3125@P55: wtlog
L3126@P55: rc (Lmin,Rmin) = (#rowlog(Lmin), #collog(Rmin)),
L3127@P55: is such that
L3128@P55: #rowlog(Lmin) <
L3129@P55: da
L3130@P55: 2 and #collog(Rmin) <
L3131@P55: dT
L3132@P55: b
L3133@P55: 2 . (13)
L3134@P55: Then, on input DδA , DδT
L3135@P55: B , S and (L, R), ReShape outputs a
L3136@P55: correct solution (L, ˜ R˜) of (SE), provided that the classical
L3137@P55: decoders DδA , DδT
L3138@P55: B succeed. In other words, the solution
L3139@P55: (L, ˜ R˜) found by ReShape is in the same homology class as
L3140@P55: the minimum weight operator with syndrome S:
L3141@P55: [Lmin,Rmin]=[L, ˜ R˜].
L3142@P55: Proof: This is a proof by contradiction: we suppose that
L3143@P55: the minimum weight solution and the solution found by
L3144@P55: ReShape (Algorithm 1) are not homologically equivalent and
L3145@P55: we find as a consequence that the minimum weight solution
L3146@P55: need to have high logical row-column weight.
L3147@P55: Let (L, R) be the valid solution of (SE) in input to ReShape
L3148@P55: and (L, ˜ R˜) be the recovery operator found.
L3149@P55: First note that σ(L, ˜ R˜)= σ(L, R). In fact, the Split step
L3150@P55: only finds the canonical form of (L, R) and therefore changes
L3151@P55: neither the operator (L, R) nor its syndrome. The Decode step,
L3152@P55: possibly adds to (L, R) logical Z-operators (Lz,Rz) such
L3153@P55-56: Authorized licensed use limited to: Sheffield University. Downloaded on September 21,2022 at 17:03:24 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Restrictions apply.QUINTAVALLE AND CAMPBELL: ReSHAPE: A DECODER FOR HYPERGRAPH PRODUCT CODES 6583
L3154@P56: that σ(Lz,Rz)=0 and therefore, even when it changes the
L3155@P56: operator, it preserves its syndrome.
L3156@P56: Suppose now that the solution found by ReShape and the
L3157@P56: minimum weight solution (Lmin,Rmin) of (SE) belong to two
L3158@P56: different homology classes:
L3159@P56: [L, ˜ R˜] =[Lmin,Rmin],
L3160@P56: where:
L3161@P56: #rowlog(L) <
L3162@P56: da
L3163@P56: 2
L3164@P56: and #collog(R) <
L3165@P56: dT
L3166@P56: b
L3167@P56: 2 .
L3168@P56: Since both (Lmin,Rmin) and (L, ˜ R˜) are valid solution of
L3169@P56: (SE), they must differ for an operator with zero X-syndrome.
L3170@P56: Because (Lmin,Rmin) and (L, ˜ R˜) are not homologically
L3171@P56: equivalent, they must differ for a non-trivial Z-operator in the
L3172@P56: normaliser N (S) of the stabiliser group. As such, they must
L3173@P56: differ for an operator which is the sum of a Z-stabiliser and

L3175@P56: (Lmin,Rmin)=(L, ˜ R˜)+(GL,GR)+(Lz,Rz), (26)

L3177@P56: logical Z-operator.
L3178@P56: Without loss of generality we assume that (Lz,Rz) is
L3179@P56: non-trivial on the left qubits, meaning that Lz has at least
L3180@P56: one non-zero column in ker δA. The proof is substantially the
L3181@P56: same in case it is non-trivial on the right qubits.

L3183@P56: form with respect to the same unit-vector basis used to write

L3198@P56: Oj

L3222@P56: lower bound on the number of its non-zero rows, we have:
L3223@P56: #row(Omin) ≥

L3369@P57-58: Authorized licensed use limited to: Sheffield University. Downloaded on September 21,2022 at 17:03:24 UTC from IEEE Xplore. Restrictions apply.Chapter 4
L3370@P58: Logical gates - Qubit partitions
L3371@P58: Context and Results
L3372@P58: In [1] we propose a method to implement logical encoded gates on hypergraph product codes via a
L3373@P58: generalization of the concept of transversal gates [2] that relies on partitions of the code’s data qubits.
L3374@P58: By the Eastin-Knill Theorem, no stabilizer code has a universal set of purely transversal gates [3] and
L3375@P58: therefore a fault-tolerant universal set will necessarily come with some, maybe substantial, overhead
L3376@P58: [4, 5, 6]. Bravyi and Konig showed that locality constraints in two dimensions limit the set of faulttolerant gates of a code to the Clifford group [7]. The planar code obeys Bravyi-Konig constraints and,
L3377@P58: even if more general hypergraph product codes do not, it has been shown [8, 9, 10] that they still suffer
L3378@P58: from the same limitations. Krishna and Poulin illustrated how, in principle, the idea of braiding on
L3379@P58: the planar code can be generalized to hypergraph product codes but their result is not constructive.
L3380@P58: We take a generalization approach too and adapt Moussa’s folding [11, 12, 13] of the planar code to
L3381@P58: other hypergraph product code families. Firstly, we show that a clever choice of the qubits partition
L3382@P58: naturally yield fault-tolerant operations. Secondly, we exhibit a few practical fault-tolerant examples to implement Clifford gates on families of hypergraph product codes that obey some symmetry
L3383@P58: constraints. Key in our construction is the discovery of a standard basis for the logical space of all
L3384@P58: hypergraph product codes that we believe is of independent interest. Crucially, transversal gates and
L3385@P58: the generalization we consider, do not require any qubit overhead. Albeit limited, our work gives a
L3386@P58: constructive approach to building logical gates on hypergraph product codes.
L3387@P58: Limitations
L3388@P58: The qubits partition method we proposed falls short in two ways. First, it does not yield the full
L3389@P58: Clifford group at zero overhead. State injection [4] and pieceable fault tolerance [14] can promote our
L3390@P58: set to computationally universal, however at the price of substantial physical qubit and time cost. As
L3391@P58: in [13], we could explore what happens if we relegate some of the logical qubits to the role of gauge
L3392@P58: qubits. Second, we conjecture that our partition strategy could produce non-Clifford gates on higher
L3393@P58: dimensional hypergraph product codes [15, 16], but we have not found the right partition – yet.
L3394@P58-59: 49Authorship declaration
L3395@P59: AOQ derived the proofs and wrote the majority of the manuscript.
