L3426@P59-60: 50Partitioning qubits in hypergraph product codes to
L3427@P60: implement logical gates
L3428@P60: Armanda O. Quintavalle1, Paul Webster2, and Michael Vasmer3,4,
L3429@P60: 1Department of Physics & Astronomy, University of Sheffield, Sheffield, S3 7RH, United Kingdom
L3430@P60: 2Centre for Engineered Quantum Systems, School of Physics, The University of Sydney, Sydney, NSW 2006, Australia
L3431@P60: 3Perimeter Institute for Theoretical Physics, Waterloo, ON N2L 2Y5, Canada
L3432@P60: 4
L3433@P60: Institute for Quantum Computing, University of Waterloo, Waterloo, ON N2L 3G1, Canada
L3434@P60: The promise of high-rate low-density parity check (LDPC) codes to substantially
L3435@P60: reduce the overhead of fault-tolerant quantum computation depends on constructing
L3436@P60: efficient, fault-tolerant implementations of logical gates on such codes. Transversal
L3437@P60: gates are the simplest type of fault-tolerant gate, but the potential of transversal gates
L3438@P60: on LDPC codes has hitherto been largely neglected. We investigate the transversal
L3439@P60: gates that can be implemented in hypergraph product codes, a class of LDPC codes.
L3440@P60: Our analysis is aided by the construction of a symplectic canonical basis for the logical
L3441@P60: operators of hypergraph product codes, a result that may be of independent interest.
L3442@P60: We show that in these codes transversal gates can implement Hadamard (up to logical
L3443@P60: SWAP gates) and control-Z on all logical qubits. Moreover, we show that sequences
L3444@P60: of transversal operations, interleaved with error-correction, allow implementation of
L3445@P60: entangling gates between arbitrary pairs of logical qubits in the same code block. We
L3446@P60: thereby demonstrate that transversal gates can be used as the basis for universal quantum computing on LDPC codes, when supplemented with state injection.
L3447@P60: 1 Introduction
L3448@P60: In recent years, quantum computing has transitioned from a theoretical idea to a real technology that is competitive with state-of-the-art classical computing on carefully selected tasks [1].
L3449@P60: However, realising its potential to solve intractable problems of practical importance requires the
L3450@P60: development of a fault-tolerant quantum computer—a device that can perform long quantum computations to a very high degree of accuracy even in the presence of noise [2]. Fault-tolerant quantum
L3451@P60: computing can be achieved by encoding quantum information into quantum error-correcting codes,
L3452@P60: but only at the cost of substantial overhead. For standard fault-tolerant quantum architectures
L3453@P60: based on the surface code this overhead is prohibitively large for the foreseeable future, with
L3454@P60: approximately 106to 108 qubits being required to realise useful applications [3, 4].
L3455@P60: High-rate quantum LDPC codes offer a promising avenue to significantly reducing this overhead [5]. These codes move beyond topological codes such as the surface code [6] by substituting
L3456@P60: the requirement that stabiliser check operators are geometrically local with a weaker condition
L3457@P60: that they must be sparse. They thereby preserve the essential benefits of the surface code for
L3458@P60: error-correction, while allowing much smaller qubit overheads [5, 7].
L3459@P60: In this work, we focus on hypergraph product codes—a class of high-rate LDPC codes derived
L3460@P60: from the tensor product of classical LDPC codes. Even when restricted to this highly-structured
L3461@P60: class of codes, it is not yet clear what is the best strategy to reliably perform logic. The earliest
L3462@P60: proposal for fault-tolerant logic on LDPC codes relies on state injection protocols that, while
L3463@P60: achieving a constant overhead per logical qubit in the asymptotic regime, would have substantial
L3464@P60: finite-size overheads [8]. In [9, 10], code deformation on hypergraph product codes is used to
L3465@P60: perform Clifford gates. The protocol proposed preserves the LDPC property of the code throughout
L3466@P60: the entire computation; however, even if in principle the whole Clifford group is implementable
L3467@P60: via this framework, there is no promise that this is the case for an arbitrary hypergraph product
L3468@P60: 1
L3469@P60-61: arXiv:2204.10812v2 [quant-ph] 28 Sep 2022code nor is there a promise on the time cost of each gate implementation. Another variation of
L3470@P61: code deformation, via non-destructive measurement of high-weight logical operators, is proposed
L3471@P61: in [11]; the method there proposed enables the implementation of the full Clifford group on all
L3472@P61: hypergraph product codes. Nevertheless, this method requires many ancilla qubits if we want to
L3473@P61: operate in parallel on all of the encoded qubits.
L3474@P61: We take a different approach to fault-tolerance and explore transversal gates [12, 13, 14, 15,
L3475@P61: 16, 17]. Transversal gates on homological codes [18], close cousins of the hypergraph product
L3476@P61: codes, were studied in [19]. Universality is there obtained by combining two quantum codes with
L3477@P61: complementary transversal gates. Hence in [19] the problem of universality is deferred to the one of
L3478@P61: finding complementary classes of good quantum codes that are then combined via the homological
L3479@P61: product; the information is always protected by at least one code whilst leveraging the transversal
L3480@P61: gates of the other. We relax the constraints on the underlying (classical) codes used as seed codes
L3481@P61: in the hypergraph product and investigate how the core symmetries that arise from the product
L3482@P61: structure itself enable transversal gates.
L3483@P61: The transversal gates we find are only a small subset of the Clifford group. Nonetheless, we
L3484@P61: further the knowledge on hypergraph product codes by proving that it is possible to efficiently
L3485@P61: construct a canonical basis for their logical space. The existence of such a canonical basis is nontrivial and may be of independent interest in the development of other computational frameworks
L3486@P61: on hypergraph product codes or related families of codes (e.g. higher dimensional homological
L3487@P61: product codes [18, 20, 21], or the codes introduced in [22]). Furthermore, we illustrate how pieceably
L3488@P61: fault-tolerant circuits [23] and state injection can be used on symmetric hypergraph product codes
L3489@P61: in conjunction with the transversal gates proposed to give a universal gate set. Importantly, our
L3490@P61: scheme is relevant to small, low-overhead quantum error-correcting codes with practical near-term
L3491@P61: potential.
L3492@P61: After a short introduction to hypergraph product codes in Section 2, we detail the existence of
L3493@P61: the canonical basis for their logical space in Section 2.1. In Section 3, we illustrate how the idea
L3494@P61: of unfolding the color code into surface codes relies on more general symmetries of the product
L3495@P61: structure, symmetries that are inherited by square and symmetric codes. Thanks to this observation and the use of our canonical basis, we showcase the transversal implementation of some
L3496@P61: Clifford gates on hypergraph product codes. We comment on how pieceable fault tolerance and
L3497@P61: state injection, in combination with the transversal gates introduced, could be used on ‘small’
L3498@P61: codes to perform arbitrary computations in Section 4.
L3499@P61: 2 Hypergraph Product Codes
L3500@P61: Any binary matrix H in F
L3501@P61: m×n
L3502@P61: 2 defines a classical code whose codewords are vectors in the kernel
L3503@P61: of H, ker H. The classical code so defined uses n bits to protect from errors k = n − rank(H) bits
L3504@P61: and detects all errors of Hamming weight less than d, the minimum weight of a codeword. Briefly,
L3505@P61: we say that H defines an [n, k, d] code [24]. Similarly, any pair of binary matrices Hx in F
L3506@P61: mx×n
L3507@P61: 2
L3508@P61: and Hz in F
L3509@P61: mz×n
L3510@P61: 2
L3511@P61: such that Hx · HT
L3512@P61: z = 0 mod 2 defines a stabiliser CSS code [25, 26, 27]. The
L3513@P61: stabiliser group is S = hSx ∪ Szi, where Sx is the set of X operators whose support vector1is
L3514@P61: equal to a row of Hx, and Sz is the set of Z operators whose support vector is equal to a row of
L3515@P61: Hz. Since an X and a Z operator commute if and only if their supports have even overlap, the
L3516@P61: orthogonality of Hx and Hz in F2 ensures that the stabiliser group S is well defined. The quantum
L3517@P61: code with stabiliser group S uses n physical qubits to encode k = n − rank(Hx) − rank(Hz) logical
L3518@P61: qubits and detects all errors of weight less than d = min(dx, dz), where dx is the minimum weight
L3519@P61: of a vector in ker Hz that is not in the image of HT
L3520@P61: x
L3521@P61: , Im HT
L3522@P61: x
L3523@P61: , and dz is the minimum weight of a
L3524@P61: vector in ker Hx that is not in Im HT
L3525@P61: z
L3526@P61: . Briefly, we say that the quantum code is [[n, k, d]].
L3527@P61: A hypergraph product code [28, 18, 29] is a CSS code produced from the hypergraph product
L3528@P61: of two linear codes. Given Ha in F

L3531@P61: and Hb in F
L3532@P61: mb×nb
L3533@P61: 2 we define:

L3546@P61: 1Given a Pauli operator on n qubits P = P1 ⊗ P2 ⊗ · · · ⊗ Pn ∈ Pn, its support vector is the unique vector v ∈ Fn

L3571@P62: The physical qubits of HGP(Ha, Hb) can be arranged in two rectangular grids of sizes na × nb
L3572@P62: and ma × mb, see fig. 2 and [30]. As such, we enumerate the physical qubits of HGP(Ha, Hb) by
L3573@P62: the triplet (i, h, L) and (j, `, R), where 1 ≤ i ≤ na, 1 ≤ h ≤ nb, 1 ≤ j ≤ ma, 1 ≤ ` ≤ mb. The first
L3574@P62: two coordinates of each triplet refer to the row and column positions of the physical qubit in the
L3575@P62: grid. The third coordinate L or R, short for left and right respectively, distinguishes the two grids
L3576@P62: and is referred to as sector of the physical qubit. Via this spacial mapping of physical qubits, each
L3577@P62: stabiliser generator will have support contained in a row of one sector and a column of the other.
L3578@P62: Specifically, elements of Sx can be indexed by a pair j, h, such that Sx(j, h) has support on qubits
L3579@P62: on row j of the right sector and column h of the left sector. Elements of Sz, indexed by a pair i, `
L3580@P62: are similar, but have support on rows of qubits in the left sector and columns in the right sector.
L3581@P62: We say that a hypergraph product code is a square code if it is derived from one classical matrix
L3582@P62: only. Square codes HGP(H, H) take their name from the shape of the two grids of physical qubits:
L3583@P62: if H ∈ F
L3584@P62: m×n
L3585@P62: 2
L3586@P62: , the left and right sectors are square grids of sizes n × n and m × m respectively. A
L3587@P62: square code HGP(H, H) such that H = HT or, more loosely, such that H ∈ F
L3588@P62: n×n
L3589@P62: 2
L3590@P62: is square and
L3591@P62: Im(H) = Im(HT), is said symmetric and denoted as HGPsy(H).
L3592@P62: 2.1 Logical Pauli Operators
L3593@P62: Logical X and Z operators of HGP(Ha, Hb) can be chosen to have support on a ‘line’ of qubits,
L3594@P62: meaning that each operator acts non-trivially either on the left or right sector, and either on a
L3595@P62: column or on a row of physical qubits [18, 30]. We build on this and show that such a ‘line basis’
L3596@P62: can, in addition, be chosen to be symplectic. The existence of such canonical basis is the key
L3597@P62: ingredient in the identification of transversal gates on hypergraph product codes. Formally:
L3598@P62: Theorem 1. For any hypergraph product code there exist bases B
L3599@P62: x
L3600@P62: line and B
L3601@P62: z
L3602@P62: line of logical X and Z
L3603@P62: operators respectively such that:
L3604@P62: 1. Any operator in B
L3605@P62: x
L3606@P62: line or B
L3607@P62: z
L3608@P62: line has support on a ‘line’ of qubits; by line here we intend that
L3609@P62: the support of the operator is contained in either a column or a row of left or right sector
L3610@P62: qubits, when qubits are arranged on two grids as explained above.
L3611@P62: 2. For any operator in B
L3612@P62: x
L3613@P62: line there exists one and only one operator in B
L3614@P62: z
L3615@P62: line that anticommutes
L3616@P62: with it. More precisely for every pairs of operators, one in B
L3617@P62: x
L3618@P62: line and one in B
L3619@P62: z
L3620@P62: line, either their
L3621@P62: support overlaps on exactly one qubit or does not overlap at all.
L3622@P62: We refer to any such pair of bases as canonical basis for HGP(Ha, Hb).
L3623@P62: The proof of Theorem 1 is constructive and relies on a modified version of the Gaussian elimination algorithm over F2 (Algorithm 1), which yields a special kind of triangular matrices that we
L3624@P62: call strongly lower triangular matrices.
L3625@P62: Definition 1. An m × n matrix A is said to be strongly lower triangular if:
L3626@P62: 1. Any column j has a pivot p such that all the elements below the pivot are zero.
L3627@P62: 2. All the pivots are distinct.
L3628@P62: 3. Reordering if necessary, for any pivot Ap,j = 1, the coefficients to its right are zero i.e.
L3629@P62: Ap,j+1 = Ap,j+2 = . . . Ap,m = 0.
L3630@P62: We indicate by π(A) the set of row pivots of A:
L3631@P62: π(A) = {p s.t. Ap,j is a pivot for some column index j of A}

L3862@P64: Practically, if an arbitrary code has a canonical basis as per Theorem 1, any indexing of the
L3863@P64: physical qubits of the code naturally yields an indexing of the logical qubits. Namely, if the unique
L3864@P64: physical qubit in the overlap of a basis logical X operator and a basis logical Z operator has index
L3865@P64: ι, then the index of the corresponding logical qubit is ι too, and we indicate it as qι. We call the
L3866@P64: physical qubit ι the physical pivot of the logical qubit qι.
L3867@P64: For a hypergraph product code HGP(Ha, Hb), if we display qubits on a grid as explained above
L3868@P64: and we fix a canonical basis as per Theorem 1, we can uniquely index logical qubits as q

L3871@P64: q

L3874@P64: whether the logical qubit has canonical operators supported on the left or right sector qubits and
L3875@P64: the first two coordinates specify where the two canonical operators cross. We refer to physical
L3876@P64: qubits (i, i, σ), σ = L, R, as diagonal qubits and to the others as mirror qubits. The logical qubits
L3877@P64: inherit the same attribute of their pivots. To sum up, we have found a classification of the logical
L3878@P64: qubits of HGP(Ha, Hb) that depends on the physical position of the crossing of the logical X and
L3879@P64: Z operators, X¯ and Z¯, see table 1.

L3898@P65: 3 Transversal Clifford gates
L3899@P65: Transversal logical operators offer the most straightforward approach to realising fault-tolerant
L3900@P65: quantum computation since they naturally limit the spread of errors. A unitary operator U is
L3901@P65: transversal with respect to a partition2 {Qi} of the physical qubits of an [[n, k, d]] code if it can be
L3902@P65: expressed as U =
L3903@P65: N
L3904@P65: i Ui
L3905@P65: , where each Ui acts non-trivially only on qubits in Qi[16]. The usual notion
L3906@P65: of transversal gates [13] is found choosing the singleton partition {{i}}. By construction transversal
L3907@P65: gates do not spread errors between qubits in different subsets and are therefore inherently faulttolerant, provided that all the subsets in the partition are correctable3
L3908@P65: . We say that a partition
L3909@P65: {Qi} is m-local if all its subsets have size at most m: |Qi| ≤ m. The partition-distance δ{Qi} is the
L3910@P65: minimum number of subsets in the partition that supports a logical operator. Equivalently, the
L3911@P65: code can detect all errors that are supported on at most δ{Qi} − 1 subsets. The partition-distance
L3912@P65: is a measure of how many faulty factors Ui the code can tolerate without corrupting the logical
L3913@P65: information. In the same way, we can think of the distance of a code as a measure of how many
L3914@P65: faulty ‘identity factors’ a code can deal with. As an example, for any [[n, k, d]] stabiliser code, the
L3915@P65: singleton partition {{i}} is 1-local, has partition distance δ{{i}} = d and the logical Pauli operators
L3916@P65: are transversal with respect to it.
L3917@P65: In this Section we propose a partition for square codes of partition-distance bd/2c and one for
L3918@P65: symmetric codes of partition-distance d; for both partitions, we report some examples of transversal
L3919@P65: operators. Importantly, as suggested in [16, 31], we expect transversal operators on (2-dimensional)
L3920@P65: hypergraph product codes to be restricted to be either Pauli or Clifford operators, hence we here
L3921@P65: focus on Clifford operators.
L3922@P65: Clifford operators permute the Pauli operators by mapping the Pauli group on n qubits Pn into
L3923@P65: itself. The set of Clifford gates on n qubits, Cn, is a group, that can be generated by:
L3924@P65: (i) The Hadamard gate H, that maps X operators into Z operators and vice-versa, X ↔ Z.
L3925@P65: (ii) The phase gate S, that maps X operators into Y operators and fixes Z operators, X → Y .
L3926@P65: (iii) The CZ gate, a two qubit gate that maps X ⊗ I → X ⊗ Z, I ⊗ X → Z ⊗ X and acts trivially
L3927@P65: on Z operators.
L3928@P65: 3.1 Gates on square codes
L3929@P65: The first partition we propose builds on the unfolding technique for the color code proposed in [32]
L3930@P65: and further studied in [33, 34, 35]. In [32, 33], the equivalence between color codes and folded
L3931@P65: planar codes is leveraged to construct transversal Clifford gates on the planar code. Here we build
L3932@P65: on that same idea to investigate Clifford gates on square hypergraph product codes.
L3933@P65: Hypergraph product codes can be seen as a generalisation of the planar code, which indeed is
L3934@P65: the hypergraph product code HGP(Hrep, Hrep), where Hrep is the full-rank parity check matrix of
L3935@P65: the repetition code, e.g.
L3936@P65: Hrep =
L3937@P65: 
L3938@P65: 1 1 0
L3939@P65: 0 1 1
L3940@P65: , (9)
L3941@P65: 2A partition of a set is a collection of non-empty and disjoint subsets of the set, whose union is the whole set.
L3942@P65: 3A set of qubits is said to be correctable if it cannot contain the support of a non-trivial logical operator.
L3943@P65-66: 6Figure 1: Graphical representation of the diagonal-twin partition for squares hypergraph product codes derived
L3944@P66: from the color code unfolding into the planar code [32, 33]. The two grids of physical qubits are folded separately
L3945@P66: along the principal diagonal and qubits that share the same sites upon folding are paired together. The grey
L3946@P66: circles represent physical diagonal qubits while all the other circles represent mirror qubits. The yellow circles
L3947@P66: (two on the left, and two on the right) are twin qubits.
L3948@P66: for parameters [3, 1, 3]. By exploiting the symmetries of the canonical basis of hypergraph product
L3949@P66: codes (Theorem 1), we are able to generalise the folding of [32] to all square hypergraph product
L3950@P66: codes HGP(H, H). We can fold the left and right grid of qubits along the principal diagonal and
L3951@P66: pair the physical qubits whose sites overlap upon folding, see fig. 1. Upon folding, mirror physical
L3952@P66: qubits are twinned: (i, h, L) twins with (h, i, L) and (j, `, R) with (`, j, R). We call the partition
L3953@P66: given by singletons of diagonal qubits and two-qubit sets of twin qubits, diagonal-twin partition.
L3954@P66: More precisely, if H ∈ F
L3955@P66: m×n
L3956@P66: 2
L3957@P66: and HGP(H, H) is a [[n, k, d]] code, the diagonal-twin partition of its
L3958@P66: physical qubits is given by
L3959@P66: n
L3960@P66: {(i, i, L)}, {(j, j, R)}
L3961@P66: o
L3962@P66: ∪ (10)
L3963@P66: n
L3964@P66: {(i, h, L), (h, i, L)}, {(j, `, R), (`, j, R)}
L3965@P66: o
L3966@P66: where 1 ≤ i, h ≤ n and i 6= h; 1 ≤ j, ` ≤ m and j 6= `.
L3967@P66: The diagonal-twin partition is 2-local and has partition-distance δdt = bd/2c. In fact, as proven
L3968@P66: in [30], a non-trivial logical operator for an [[n, k, d]] hypergraph product code has support on at
L3969@P66: least d rows or columns of qubits in the same sector. Because the diagonal-twin partition is 2-local,
L3970@P66: the union of any choice of µ subsets from it has size at most 2µ. Thus, in order to fill at least d
L3971@P66: rows or d columns of physical qubits, we need to pick at least µ ≥ d/2 subsets in the diagonal-twin
L3972@P66: partition.
L3973@P66: The nomenclature of physical qubits as diagonal and twins is naturally inherited by the logical
L3974@P66: qubits via the correspondence of logical qubits and their physical pivots, see table 1. This labelling
L3975@P66: is key in understanding the logical actions of transversal operations for the diagonal-twin partition
L3976@P66: and therefore we summarize it here: diagonal logical qubits are indexed as q
L3977@P66: L
L3978@P66: i,i and q
L3979@P66: R
L3980@P66: j,j ; twin logical
L3981@P66: qubits as (q
L3982@P66: L
L3983@P66: i,h, qLh,i) and (q
L3984@P66: R
L3985@P66: j,`, qR`,j ) for i 6= h and j 6= `, see fig. 2.
L3986@P66: Similarly to the planar code case, both the Hadamard-SWAP and the CZ-S gates detailed below
L3987@P66: are valid logical operators on HGP(H, H). Whilst it is immediate to verify that the stabilisers group
L3988@P66: is preserved by these two operators, less immediate is the identification of the logical operation
L3989@P66: performed. Crucially, this task becomes trivial when we look at the action induced on a canonical
L3990@P66: basis derived by strongly lower triangular reduction as per Lemma 1.
L3991@P66: On the physical level, Hadamard-SWAP consist of (i) Hadamard on every physical qubit; (ii)
L3992@P66: physical SWAP between twin qubits. Hadamard-SWAP is a valid logical operator as it preserves
L3993@P66: the stabiliser group mapping Sx(j, h) ↔ Sz(h, j) and (i) swaps the logical X and the logical Z
L3994@P66: operators of twin qubits: on the left q
L3995@P66: L
L3996@P66: i,h and q
L3997@P66: L
L3998@P66: h,i, on the right q
L3999@P66: R
L4000@P66: j,` and q
L4001@P66: R
L4002@P66: `,j ; (ii) acts as logical
L4003@P66: Hadamard on the diagonal qubits.
L4004@P66: The operator CZ-S is defined on the physical level as: (i) S gate on left diagonal qubits; (ii)
L4005@P66: S
L4006@P66: †
L4007@P66: on right diagonal qubits; (iii) CZ between twin qubits. CZ-S preserves the Z stabilisers and
L4008@P66: maps the X stabiliser Sx(j, h) into Sx(j, h)Sz(h, j). Importantly, the phase factor in the product
L4009@P66: Sx(j, h)Sz(h, j) is correctly preserved since, by construction, an X-stabiliser Sx(j, h) has a diagonal
L4010@P66: left qubit (h, h) in its support if and only if Ha[j, h] = 1, if an only if it has a diagonal right qubit
L4011@P66-67: 7Figure 2: Graphical representation of the physical qubits of the square code HGP(H, ˜ H˜ ), where H˜ is as in
L4012@P67: eq. (11). The physical qubits (white circles) are arranged on a left and right grid. The black circles highlight
L4013@P67: the position of the physical pivots associated to the logical qubits, see table 1. The canonical basis pictured is
L4014@P67: derived as explained in Lemma 1 from the matrix A of eq. (4), whose columns are a strongly lower triangular
L4015@P67: basis of ker H˜ .
L4016@P67: (j, j) in its support too. As such, if an S gate is applied, an S†gate is applied too and the global
L4017@P67: phase cancels out. Again by looking at the action of the operator CZ-S on a canonical basis, since
L4018@P67: twin logical operators have support on mirror qubits only and each diagonal logical operator has
L4019@P67: support on exactly one diagonal qubit, we find that the logical action of the operator CZ-S is (i) S
L4020@P67: on left diagonal qubits; (ii) S†on right diagonal qubits; (iii) CZ between twin qubits.

L4037@P67: 1 0 1 1T
L4038@P67: generates ker H˜ T. Trivially, {v˜} is a strongly lower triangular
L4039@P67: basis of ker H˜ T, with pivot π(˜v) = 4. In fig. 2 qubits are represented as circles. We note how
L4040@P67: these are divided into a 7 × 7 grid of left physical qubits and a 4 × 4 grid of right physical qubits.
L4041@P67: The characteristic square shape of these two grids makes HGP(H, ˜ H˜ ) a square code. The physical
L4042@P67: diagonal qubits are the ones that lie across the principal diagonals of the squares (the two black
L4043@P67: lines in fig. 2). The black circles correspond to physical pivots, one for each logical qubit of the
L4044@P67: code. As for any other square hypergraph product code derived from a classical [n, k, d] code whose
L4045@P67: transpose is a [m, kT, dT] code, there are k
L4046@P67: 2 = 16 left logical qubits, k = 4 of which diagonal, and
L4047@P67: (k
L4048@P67: T
L4049@P67: )
L4050@P67: 2 = 1 right logical qubits, kT = 1 of which diagonal.
L4051@P67: 3.2 Gates on symmetric codes
L4052@P67: The second partition we propose improves on the diagonal-twin partition, having partition-distance
L4053@P67: d against bd/2c for a [[n, k, d]] code. The price paid is the validity of this partition only on a small
L4054@P67: class of square codes, the symmetric codes: HGPsy(H) = HGP(H, H) for some H such that
L4055@P67: Im H = Im HT.
L4056@P67: Notably, the toric code is a symmetric hypergraph product code HGPsy(Htoric) where e.g. for
L4057@P67: d = 3,
L4058@P67: Htoric =
L4059@P67: 
L4060@P67: 
L4061@P67: 1 1 0
L4062@P67: 0 1 1
L4063@P67: 1 0 1
L4064@P67: 
L4065@P67:  .
L4066@P67: For our purposes, the key feature of symmetric hypergraph product codes is that their physical
L4067@P67: qubits can be arranged in two square grids of the same size. This fact suggests that we can pair
L4068@P67: physical qubits by superimposing the left grid of physical qubits on the right one, and pairing
L4069@P67-68: 8qubits that sit at the same coordinate. In this way, every physical qubit (i, h, L) is paired with its
L4070@P68: sibling qubit (i, h, R). Explicitly, given a symmetric code HGPsy(H), with H ∈ F
L4071@P68: n×n
L4072@P68: 2
L4073@P68: , we define
L4074@P68: its sibling partition as:
L4075@P68: n
L4076@P68: {(i, h, L), (i, h, R)}
L4077@P68: o
L4078@P68: . (12)
L4079@P68: where 1 ≤ i, h, ≤ n. See fig. 3 for a graphical representation of the sibling partition. The sibling
L4080@P68: partition has partition-distance δs = d. In fact, as said above and proven in [30], every non-trivial
L4081@P68: logical operator of a hypergraph product has support on at least d rows or columns in the same
L4082@P68: sector. As such, even if the sibling partition is 2-local, every subsets in it can give a contribution
L4083@P68: of at most 1 towards the covering of an arbitrary logical operator. This observation is more
L4084@P68: general: any partition whose subsets Qi contain at most one qubit in each sector has maximum
L4085@P68: partition-distance d. We call any such partition sector-transversal.
L4086@P68: The Hadamard-SWAP operation defined above for the square codes on the diagonal-twin partition
L4087@P68: is similarly defined on the sibling partition too: physical Hadamard on all qubits and SWAP
L4088@P68: between siblings qubits yields Hadamard on all the logical qubits composed with logical SWAPs
L4089@P68: between sibling logical qubits.
L4090@P68: Via the sibling partition is naturally defined a transversal CZ operator. In fact, applying physical
L4091@P68: CZ gates on all pairs of sibling qubits preserves the Z stabilisers and maps the X stabilisers Sx(j, h)
L4092@P68: into Sx(j, h)Sz(h, j). On the logical level, the CZ operator yields a CZ between pairs of sibling logical
L4093@P68: qubits q
L4094@P68: L
L4095@P68: i,h and q
L4096@P68: R
L4097@P68: i,h.
L4098@P68: 3.2.1 An example of a symmetric code
L4099@P68: Building on our guide example in Section 3.1.1, we illustrate a symmetric hypergraph product code
L4100@P68: derived from the [7, 4, 3] Hamming code with full-rank parity check matrix H,

L4109@P68: We define the symmetric Hamming code as the symmetric hypergraph product code HGPsy(HT H),
L4110@P68: where4

L4124@P68: The symmetric Hamming code HGPsy(HT H) has parameters [[98, 32, 3]] and stabiliser generators
L4125@P68: of weight 8. It has a rate of k/n = 32/98 ≈ 0.33 which substantially outperforms5the surface
L4126@P68: and toric code rates and indeed the optimal rate of k/n = 0.2 for codes with k = 1 and d ≥ 3.
L4127@P68: For this reason, we believe that similarly constructed symmetric codes could be promising for
L4128@P68: low-overhead, near-term quantum computing. In Appendix B we present several more examples of
L4129@P68: small (n < 1000) symmetric hypergraph product codes with stabiliser generators of weight w ≤ 16
L4130@P68: on which all the gates we describe could be implemented.
L4131@P68: 4 Completing a universal gate set
L4132@P68: Transversal gates enable us to implement a subset of logical Clifford gates on hypergraph product
L4133@P68: codes, but this is not sufficient for performing universal fault-tolerant quantum computation. In
L4134@P68: 4Note that, for any matrix A, AT A is symmetric.
L4135@P68: 5For d = 3, the surface and the toric code have rate 1/13 ≈ 0.08 and 1/18 ≈ 0.06 respectively. The optimal rate
L4136@P68: for codes with k = 1 is achieved by the five qubit code [36].
L4137@P68-69: 9(a) (b)
L4138@P69: Figure 3: Graphical representation of the symmetric hamming code HGPsy(HT H). In both figures, the left
L4139@P69: sector qubits sit at the front while the right sector qubits sit at the back. Sibling pair of qubits are superimposed.
L4140@P69: In fig. 3a the orange filled circles represent the support of the X stabiliser Sx(2, 3). In fig. 3b the blue filled
L4141@P69: circles represent the support of the Z stabiliser Sz(4, 6).
L4142@P69: particular, the Clifford gates detailed in Section 3 act equivalently on logical qubits of the same type
L4143@P69: (diagonal, twin or sibling) and so cannot be used to e.g. entangle arbitrary pairs of logical qubits.
L4144@P69: In this Section, we focus on symmetric hypergraph product codes and show how to implement a
L4145@P69: universal gate set using circuits that consist of sequences of sector-transversal gates6. Such circuits
L4146@P69: are pieceably fault-tolerant, meaning that each gate in the sequence is fault-tolerant, and therefore
L4147@P69: the overall circuit is fault-tolerant when supplemented with intermediate error correction [23].
L4148@P69: In Section 4.1, we show how to implement entangling gates between arbitrary logical qubits by
L4149@P69: combining pieceably fault-tolerant gates. Then, in Section 4.2, we show how to complete a universal
L4150@P69: gate via state injection. Lastly, in Section 4.3, we discuss the limitations of our approach.
L4151@P69: 4.1 Pieceably fault-tolerant two-qubit entangling gates
L4152@P69: We begin by constructing a pieceably fault-tolerant circuit for implementing a logical CZ gate

L4258@P70: 4.2 Single-qubit gates via state injection

L4261@P70: factory and then use a pieceably fault-tolerant CZ gate to implement the state injection circuits

L4311@P71: techniques [40, 44, 45, 46]. In addition, multiple magic states could also be injected from a CSS

L4350@P72: 4.3 Limitations of pieceable fault-tolerance
L4351@P72: We conclude this Section by highlighting two important limitations of pieceable fault tolerant
L4352@P72: techniques: intermediate correction and time cost. To guide our analysis, let us consider the
L4353@P72: pieceably fault-tolerant CZ gate of Section 4.1.
L4354@P72: Since CZ is diagonal in the Z basis, Z stabilisers are left unchanged during the protocol and
L4355@P72: hence correction for X errors can be done, at each time step, via standard measurement of Z
L4356@P72: stabilisers. However, the original X stabilizer generators are transformed at each intermediate
L4357@P72: time step, with no guarantee on their weight—which could scale with the code distance. To avoid
L4358@P72: measuring high-weight generators, we can neglect to do error correction for Z errors at intermediate
L4359@P72: time steps, at the cost of allowing Z errors to build up for a constant number of rounds (for codes
L4360@P72: of a fixed distance). This strategy will only be fault-tolerant for codes of a fixed distance and
L4361@P72: therefore will not give a threshold without modification7. Nevertheless, the asymptotic behaviour
L4362@P72: encapsulated by a threshold is less important in the short- to medium-term regime where space
L4363@P72: overhead will likely be the most important constraint.
L4364@P72: As regards to time cost, pieceably fault-tolerant circuits necessarily have a time overhead higher
L4365@P72: than transversal gates because of intermediate error-correction. If the time cost of one cycle of
L4366@P72: error-correction followed by the application of a transversal gate is τ , then each sector-transversal
L4367@P72: gate has time cost τ . By contrast, the pieceably fault-tolerant CZ of eq. (15), has cost at least τ d
L4368@P72: for a distance d code. For instance, in the symmetric Hamming code example given in section 3.2.1,
L4369@P72: CZ or XCX have cost τ4 because 4 is the maximum weight of a canonical logical operator.
L4370@P72: In general, composing m pieceable fault tolerant gates takes time τ d↑m, where d
L4371@P72: ↑
L4372@P72: is the maximum weight of a canonical logical operator. However, this overhead can be reduced via parallelization. For instance,
L4373@P72: CZ q
L4374@P72: L
L4375@P72: i,h, qR
L4376@P72: j,` and CZ q
L4377@P72: L
L4378@P72: i
L4379@P72: 0
L4380@P72: ,h0 , qR
L4381@P72: j
L4382@P72: 0
L4383@P72: ,`0
L4384@P72: can be performed in parallel via eq. (15), provided that the logical Z operators of the qubits
L4385@P72: considered have all disjoint support e.g. whenever h 6= h
L4386@P72: 0 and j 6= j0
L4387@P72: ; see fig. 7 for an example.
L4388@P72: Therefore, if we want to implement m pieceable fault-tolerant gates, we can divide them into
L4389@P72: subsets of parallelizable gates and, if np is the minimum number of gates in any of these subsets,
L4390@P72: we can implement all the gates in time τ d↑m/np, reducing the average time overhead per gate.
L4391@P72: Importantly, performing gates in parallel has no additional space overhead, in contrast to the
L4392@P72: measurement-based scheme of [11].
L4393@P72: 5 Conclusion
L4394@P72: We have investigated the structure of hypergraph product codes to characterize transveral Clifford
L4395@P72: gates on them. In fact, we proved that every hypergraph product code has a canonical basis for
L4396@P72: its logical space whose features suggest qualitative differences between logical qubits (diagonal,
L4397@P72: twin and sibling). We showcased how to leverage such differences to implement some transversal
L4398@P72: Clifford gates on square and symmetric hypergraph product codes. The logical transversal gates
L4399@P72: we have found are limited but we comment on how these could be augmented via pieceable fault
L4400@P72: tolerance and state injection to achieve universality.
L4401@P72: While we have focused on symmetric hypergraph product codes, we believe that the choice of
L4402@P72: structured seed classical codes in the hypergraph product could be exploited to construct other
L4403@P72: 7Another possible solution is to perform weight reduction [11, 47, 48] on the X stabiliser generators at each time
L4404@P72: step, likely at the cost of a high space overhead.

L4417@P73: q
L4418@P73: L
L4419@P73: 5,3q
L4420@P73: R
L4421@P73: 5,7. Pieceably fault tolerant gates that act on the support of these logical operators (e.g. CZ) can be
L4422@P73: implemented in parallel. We note that, for any symmetric hypergraph product code of dimension k
L4423@P73: 2
L4424@P73: , we can
L4425@P73: always find k pairs of logical qubits whose logical operators do not overlap as shown here (one for each left line
L4426@P73: pivot and one for each right line pivot).
L4427@P73: transversal gates. We also conjecture that a partitioning strategy similar to the ones here presented could be used to implement transversal non-Clifford gates on higher dimensional homological codes [34, 21]. More generally, it would be interesting to extend the construction of a canonical
L4428@P73: basis to the more efficient product codes construction such as the ones in [49, 50, 51, 52].

L4539@P76: A Proofs
L4540@P76: In this Section we introduce the strongly lower triangular Gaussian reduction for binary matrices
L4541@P76: (Algorithm 1) and prove its correctness.
L4542@P76: Algorithm 1 takes in input a binary matrix H and outputs a strongly lower triangular basis
L4543@P76: for its kernel, as per Definition 1, and a unit vector basis for its complement 8. It iterates over
L4544@P76: the columns of H (line 4) and iteratively removes from the set of columns indices (line 8) the
L4545@P76: independent columns; the matrix of interest, K, which will contain the column vectors forming
L4546@P76: a basis of the kernel of H is iteratively reduced in triangular form (line 10). We prove that
L4547@P76: Algorithm 1 is correct in Lemma 2 below.

L4555@P76: , see the proof of Lemma 1.

L4590@P77: Lemma 2. Algorithm 1 terminates and is correct. More precisely:
L4591@P77: 1) The set H = {Hj: 1 ≤ j ≤ n, j 6∈ π(K)} is a basis of the column span of H.
L4592@P77: 2) The set K is a basis of the kernel of H.
L4593@P77: 3) The set F is a unit-vector basis of (Im HT)
L4594@P77: •
L4595@P77: .
L4596@P77: Proof. Let v be any vector in the column span of H:
L4597@P77: v =
L4598@P77: X
L4599@P77: j∈V
L4600@P77: Hj.
L4601@P77: If V ∩ π(K) = ∅ then there is nothing to prove. Conversely, suppose that there exists j ∈ V such
L4602@P77: that j ∈ π(K). Then it must be Hˆ j = 0 (see line 8). In other words, the jth column can be written
L4603@P77: as a linear combination of other columns of H, and by substitution if necessary, point 1) is proved.
L4604@P77: To prove point 2), observe that for what said on the zero columns of Hˆ , all the vectors in K are
L4605@P77: in the kernel of H. Moreover, they are linearly independent and lower triangular by construction.
L4606@P77: Now suppose that the matrix is not strongly lower triangular (e.g. 
L4607@P77: 1 1
L4608@P77: 0 1
L4609@P77: ) so that there exists a
L4610@P77: row index i such that Ki,α = Ki,α+τ = 1 for some τ > 0. This means that the αth column Kα
L4611@P77: has been added to the (α + τ )th column Kα+τ at step α. However, if α ∈ π(K) is a pivot index,
L4612@P77: Hˆ α is zero at steps α − 1, . . . , n and therefore the if condition at line 11 is not met.
L4613@P77: Point 3) follows observing that the vectors in F are linearly independent by construction (they
L4614@P77: are unit vectors with different pivots) and none of them belongs to the row span of H. In fact by
L4615@P77: definition, any vector in the kernel of H is orthogonal to vectors in the row span of H, and by
L4616@P77: strong lower triangularity, hk, fi = 1 for any k ∈ K and f ∈ F.

L4696@P78-80: 1970Chapter 5
L4697@P80: Single-shot error correction -
L4698@P80: Confinement
L4699@P80: Context and Results
L4700@P80: In [1] we investigate the theoretical requirements a code must have to be robust against syndrome
L4701@P80: measurement errors. In the literature there are two proposed strategies to address noisy measurements:
L4702@P80: repeated measurement over time [2, 3] and using a single-shot code and decoder [4, 5]. By repeating
L4703@P80: the same stabilizer measurements multiple times we can infer the most likely measurement result via a
L4704@P80: majority-vote strategy. In the single-shot setting instead, measurements are performed only once but
L4705@P80: we allow for some residual error to be present on the code’s data qubits after one round of correction.
L4706@P80: A decoder succeeds if the residual error on the data qubits is kept under control after repeated rounds
L4707@P80: of single-shot error correction, opposite to the standard scenario where a decoder succeeds if no error
L4708@P80: is left on the register after correction.
L4709@P80: We propose the property of confinement of the syndrome function as essential for single-shot error
L4710@P80: correction. Loosely, a code has confinement if, for small enough error, the syndrome weight grows with
L4711@P80: the error weight. Our main result is proving that confinement is indeed sufficient for codes to exhibit
L4712@P80: some form of single-shot behaviour. Further, we illustrate how the original hypergraph product code
L4713@P80: construction [6] can be iterated, and made ‘three-dimensional’ to ensure that the associated quantum
L4714@P80: code has confinement, independently of the classical codes chosen as seeds in the product.
L4715@P80: Limitations
L4716@P80: To date, no code family is known to be single-shot without also having the confinement property
L4717@P80: as defined in [1]. Indeed we shown that confinement is a sufficient condition for single-shot error
L4718@P80: correction. Nonetheless, we were after a necessary condition for it and we conjecture that in fact
L4719@P80: confinement and single-shot properties are equivalent.
L4720@P80: Another, minor, limitation of this work is found in the numerical results obtained. We have
L4721@P80: simulated single-shot decoding on three-dimensional hypergraph product codes using what we call a
L4722@P80: two-stage decoder. This choice is justified by the proof structure of our theoretical result. However,
L4723@P80: better numerical performances are possible when the first stage of our decoder is dropped [7].

L4726@P81: Appendices A, B, C and D. All the authors contributed to Sections I, II and VI.

L4741@P81: homological product codes”. In: Bulletin of the American Physical Society (2022).

L4743@P82: Editors’ Suggestion
L4744@P82: Single-Shot Error Correction of Three-Dimensional Homological Product Codes
L4745@P82: Armanda O. Quintavalle ,
L4746@P82: 1,* Michael Vasmer ,2,3,† Joschka Roffe ,1,4,‡ and Earl T. Campbell1,5,§
L4747@P82: 1
L4748@P82: Department of Physics and Astronomy, University of Sheffield, Sheffield S3 7RH, United Kingdom 2
L4749@P82: Perimeter Institute for Theoretical Physics, Waterloo, Ontario N2L 2Y5, Canada 3
L4750@P82: Institute for Quantum Computing, University of Waterloo, Waterloo, Ontario N2L 3G1, Canada
L4751@P82: 4
L4752@P82: Dahlem Center for Complex Quantum Systems, Freie Universität Berlin, 14195 Berlin, Germany 5
L4753@P82: AWS Center for Quantum Computing, Cambridge, CB1 2GA, United Kingdom
L4754@P82: (Received 17 December 2020; accepted 20 April 2021; published 14 June 2021)
L4755@P82: Single-shot error correction corrects data noise using only a single round of noisy measurements on
L4756@P82: the data qubits, removing the need for intensive measurement repetition. We introduce a general concept
L4757@P82: of confinement for quantum codes, which roughly stipulates qubit errors cannot grow without triggering
L4758@P82: more measurement syndromes. We prove confinement is sufficient for single-shot decoding of adversarial errors and linear confinement is sufficient for single-shot decoding of local stochastic errors. Further
L4759@P82: to this, we prove that all three-dimensional homological product codes exhibit confinement in their X
L4760@P82: components and are therefore single shot for adversarial phase-flip noise. For local stochastic phase-flip
L4761@P82: noise, we numerically explore these codes and again find evidence of single-shot protection. Our Monte
L4762@P82: Carlo simulations indicate sustainable thresholds of 3.08(4)% and 2.90(2)% for three-dimensional (3D)
L4763@P82: surface and toric codes, respectively, the highest observed single-shot thresholds to date. To demonstrate
L4764@P82: single-shot error correction beyond the class of topological codes, we also run simulations on a randomly
L4765@P82: constructed family of 3D homological product codes.
L4766@P82: DOI: 10.1103/PRXQuantum.2.020340
L4767@P82: I. INTRODUCTION
L4768@P82: Quantum error correction encodes logical quantum
L4769@P82: information into a codespace [1]. Given perfect measurement of the codespace stabilizers we obtain the syndrome
L4770@P82: of any error present. A suitable decoding algorithm can
L4771@P82: determine a recovery operation that returns the system to
L4772@P82: the codespace. Either this recovery is a perfect success, or a
L4773@P82: failure resulting in a high weight logical error. However, in
L4774@P82: real quantum systems the measurements are not perfect and
L4775@P82: this simple story becomes more involved. The three main
L4776@P82: strategies for tackling noisy measurements are as follows:
L4777@P82: repeated measurements on the code [2,3]; performing measurement driven error correction on a cluster state [4–9]; or
L4778@P82: using a single-shot code and decoder [10]. Focusing on the
L4779@P82: last strategy, the single-shot approach has the advantage of
L4780@P82: *armandaoq@gmail.com
L4781@P82: †mvasmer@perimeterinstitute.ca
L4782@P82: ‡joschka@roffe.eu
L4783@P82: §earltcampbell@gmail.com
L4784@P82: Published by the American Physical Society under the terms of
L4785@P82: the Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International license. Further distribution of this work must maintain attribution to the
L4786@P82: author(s) and the published article’s title, journal citation, and
L4787@P82: DOI.
L4788@P82: no additional time cost or cluster-state generation cost and
L4789@P82: provides a resilience against time-correlated noise [11]. In
L4790@P82: single-shot error correction, some residual error persists
L4791@P82: after each round of error correction, but this residual error
L4792@P82: is kept small and does not rapidly accumulate. However,
L4793@P82: only a special class of codes support single-shot error correction, but exactly which codes and why is not yet fully
L4794@P82: understood.
L4795@P82: Bombín coined the phrase single-shot error correction
L4796@P82: and remarked that it “is related to self-correction and
L4797@P82: confinement phenomena in the corresponding quantum
L4798@P82: Hamiltonian model.”[10]. He defined confinement for
L4799@P82: subsystem codes, and showed that it is sufficient for singleshot error correction with a limited class of subsystem
L4800@P82: codes. In particular, he proved that the three-dimensional
L4801@P82: (3D) gauge color code supports single-shot error correction, though it is unknown whether the corresponding Hamiltonian exhibits self-correction. Later single-shot
L4802@P82: error correction was numerically observed in a variety of
L4803@P82: higher-dimensional topological codes, including the following: the 3D gauge color code [12], four-dimensional
L4804@P82: (4D) surface codes [13] and their hyperbolic cousins [14],
L4805@P82: and 3D surface codes with phase noise [15–17]. Campbell
L4806@P82: established a general set of sufficient conditions, encapsulated by a code property called good soundness, that
L4807@P82: ensured adversarial noise could be suppressed using a
L4808@P82-83: 2691-3399/21/2(2)/020340(35) 020340-1 Published by the American Physical SocietyQUINTAVALLE, VASMER, ROFFE, and CAMPBELL PRX QUANTUM 2, 020340 (2021)
L4809@P83: single-shot decoder [18]. While Campbell’s sufficiency
L4810@P83: conditions explained single-shot error correction in a wide
L4811@P83: range of codes, around the same time it was shown that
L4812@P83: quantum expander codes [19–21] supported single-shot
L4813@P83: error correction [22]. However, quantum expander codes
L4814@P83: lack the soundness property so neither Bombín’s notion
L4815@P83: of confinement or Campbell’s notion of soundness is sufficient to encompass all known examples of single-shot
L4816@P83: error correction. Our work provides the first framework
L4817@P83: that captures all known forms of single-shot error correction, encompassing both previous approaches within a
L4818@P83: single theory.
L4819@P83: We can use different classical algorithms to decode a
L4820@P83: given quantum code, and this choice will affect the utility
L4821@P83: of the code. Different decoders have various time complexities and error tolerances, which affects the resources
L4822@P83: required by a quantum computer based on the code
L4823@P83: [23–25]. Thus far, single-shot decoders come in two flavors. The first are two-stage decoders [12,13], where stage
L4824@P83: 1 decoding repairs the noisy syndrome using redundancy
L4825@P83: in the parity check measurements and stage 2 decoding
L4826@P83: solves the corrected syndrome problem. The second flavor of decoders computes a correction from the noisy
L4827@P83: syndrome without attempting to repair it. Most examples of such decoders are local decoders, meaning that
L4828@P83: the whole correction is made up of corrections computed
L4829@P83: in small local regions of the code using syndrome information in the immediate neighborhood [14–17,21,26,27].
L4830@P83: However, there are some examples of nonlocal decoders
L4831@P83: such as belief propagation (BP) being used for single-shot
L4832@P83: error correction without syndrome repair [14,27]. A natural
L4833@P83: question to ask is the following: what is the optimal decoding strategy for single-shot codes? Even in the simple case
L4834@P83: of the 3D toric code this is not well understood.
L4835@P83: The remainder of this paper is structured as follows.
L4836@P83: In Sec. II, we give a summary of our results. In Sec. III,
L4837@P83: we formally state our results on confinement and singleshot decoding. In Sec. IV, we detail the construction of 3D
L4838@P83: product codes. In Sec. V, we present our numerical simulations and analyse their results. Finally, in Sec. VI, we
L4839@P83: discuss future research directions that flow from this work.
L4840@P83: II. SUMMARY OF RESULTS
L4841@P83: This paper is in two parts: on the one hand, we propose
L4842@P83: the concept of confinement as an essential characteristic for a code family to display single-shot properties; on
L4843@P83: the other, we investigate the single-shot performances of
L4844@P83: the class of 3D homological product codes [19,28,29],
L4845@P83: which we call 3D product codes. First, we introduce confinement in Sec. III. Loosely, confinement stipulates that
L4846@P83: syndrome weight must increase with qubit weight, under
L4847@P83: some caveats. We formalize the notion of a code family
L4848@P83: having good confinement, which we prove is a sufficient
L4849@P83: condition for single-shot decoding in the adversarial noise
L4850@P83: setting. In addition to that, we prove that good linear confinement is a sufficient condition for a family of codes
L4851@P83: to exhibit a sustainable single-shot threshold for local
L4852@P83: stochastic noise (Appendix A). Second, we review the construction of the 3D product codes in Sec. IV, and show that
L4853@P83: the 3D surface and toric codes are particular instances of
L4854@P83: this more general class of codes in Appendix B. We prove
L4855@P83: that all 3D product codes have (cubic) confinement for
L4856@P83: phase-flip errors (Appendix C), and therefore have singleshot error correction for adversarial phase-flip noise. We
L4857@P83: expect these codes to have single-shot error correction
L4858@P83: for local stochastic phase-flip noise as well. In fact, our
L4859@P83: definition of confinement generalizes the definition proposed by Bombín [10] for the gauge color code and the
L4860@P83: notion of robustness for expander codes [20]; since both
L4861@P83: class of codes are proven to have a single-shot threshold for local stochastic noise [10,22] we conjecture that
L4862@P83: low-density parity-check (LDPC) codes with good (superlinear) confinement have a threshold too. We investigate
L4863@P83: this case numerically.
L4864@P83: In the single-shot setting, the code always has some
L4865@P83: residual error present and the error-correction procedure
L4866@P83: introduces noise correlations in subsequent rounds of
L4867@P83: single-shot error correction. How then do we assess success or failure of a decoding algorithm? The concept of
L4868@P83: sustainable threshold was proposed by Brown et al.[12]
L4869@P83: as a metric for single-shot codes and decoders. We use
L4870@P83: pth(N) to denote the threshold of a code-decoder family
L4871@P83: given N cycles of qubit noise, noisy syndrome extraction,
L4872@P83: and single-shot decoding, with the Nth cycle followed by a
L4873@P83: single round of noiseless syndrome extraction and decoding. The final round ensures that we return the system
L4874@P83: to the codespace and assess success by the absence of a
L4875@P83: logical error. We define the sustainable threshold of the
L4876@P83: code-decoder family to be
L4877@P83: psus = lim
L4878@P83: N→∞ pth(N). (1)
L4879@P83: Numerically, this is estimated by plotting pth(N) against N
L4880@P83: and fitting to the following ansatz,
L4881@P83: pth(N) = psus{1 − [1 − pth(0)/psus]e−γN }. (2)
L4882@P83: We numerically estimate the sustainable error thresholds
L4883@P83: of 3D toric and surface codes for two different two-stage
L4884@P83: decoders. We surpass all previous single-shot error thresholds for these code families, and we also obtain the highest
L4885@P83: phase-flip noise threshold; see Table I. For our singleshot simulations, we use an independent and identically
L4886@P83: distributed noise model where each qubit experiences a
L4887@P83: phase-flip error with probability p, and each stabilizer
L4888@P83: measurement outcome is flipped with probability q = p.
L4889@P83: We investigate two decoding strategies: one where we use
L4890@P83: minimum-weight perfect matching (MWPM) for stage-1
L4891@P83: decoding and belief propagation with ordered statistics
L4892@P83-84: 020340-2SINGLE-SHOT ERROR CORRECTION OF THREE-DIMENSIONAL. . . PRX QUANTUM 2, 020340 (2021)

L4894@P84: decoders (results from this work are highlighted in bold).
L4895@P84: For phase-flip noise, BP+OSD outperforms all prior art, and
L4896@P84: approaches the theoretical upper bound given by mapping to a
L4897@P84: statistical mechanical model. In the single-shot regime, MWPM
L4898@P84: and BP+OSD outperforms the Sweep decoder (the theoretical
L4899@P84: maximum is unknown in this case).

L4956@P84: There is an important difference in single-shot decoding
L4957@P84: for the 3D toric code when compared with the 3D surface
L4958@P84: code. Specifically, in the 3D toric code, the syndromerepair stage of single-shot decoding can fail, producing a
L4959@P84: “syndrome” for which there is no corresponding error. We
L4960@P84: find that this failure mode substantially increases the logical error rate of the 3D toric code when compared with the
L4961@P84: 3D surface code (although the thresholds are very similar).
L4962@P84: We provide a novel decoding subroutine for dealing with
L4963@P84: these errors, which dramatically improves the performance
L4964@P84: of the 3D toric code. Furthermore, our subroutine is applicable to any single-shot LDPC code whose parity-check
L4965@P84: matrix is not full rank.
L4966@P84: The advantage of using BP+OSD for stage-1 decoding
L4967@P84: is that, unlike MWPM, this decoder does not rely on the
L4968@P84: special structure of the looplike syndrome present in 3D
L4969@P84: toric and surface codes. We use BP+OSD for single-shot
L4970@P84: decoding of a family of nontopological 3D product codes,
L4971@P84: achieving sustainable thresholds that are comparable to
L4972@P84: those of the 3D surface code. This is the second example
L4973@P84: of single-shot decoding of nontopological codes, the first
L4974@P84: being the quantum expander codes considered in Ref. [27].
L4975@P84: Whilst our single-shot threshold (2.7%) is slightly below
L4976@P84: the corresponding value observed in quantum expander
L4977@P84: codes (3%), our code family has other advantages over
L4978@P84: expander codes. Most importantly, the expansion properties of our code family are less severe, which implies
L4979@P84: that our code family would be easier to implement in
L4980@P84: architectures with geometrically constrained connectivity.
L4981@P84: III. DEFINITIONS AND THEOREMS
L4982@P84: In this section, we introduce the definition of confinement for a stabilizer code and exhibit a theoretical twostage decoder, the shadow decoder, which we prove is
L4983@P84: single shot on confined codes against adversarial noise. We
L4984@P84: refer the reader to Appendix A to see how a variant of the
L4985@P84: shadow decoder can be used to prove that good families of
L4986@P84: codes with linear confinement have a single-shot threshold
L4987@P84: for local stochastic noise.
L4988@P84: A stabilizer code encoding k logical qubits into n physical qubits can be described by its stabilizer group S and a
L4989@P84: syndrome map σ(·). The stabilizer group S is an Abelian
L4990@P84: subgroup of the Pauli group Pn on n qubits, which does not
L4991@P84: contain −1 and has dimension n − k. The syndrome map
L4992@P84: is not unique: any generating set of the group S defines a
L4993@P84-85: 020340-3QUINTAVALLE, VASMER, ROFFE, and CAMPBELL PRX QUANTUM 2, 020340 (2021)
L4994@P85: valid syndrome map for the code. If {s1, ... ,sm} is one such
L4995@P85: generating set, the associated function σ(·) maps a qubit
L4996@P85: operator p ∈ Pn into the binary vector (s¯1, ... ,s¯m)T ∈ Fm
L4997@P85: 2 ,
L4998@P85: where s¯i = 1 if si anticommutes with p and 0 otherwise. Importantly, σ(·) is linear, meaning that σ(p · q) =
L4999@P85: σ(p) + σ(q) over Fm
L5000@P85: 2 . Because any Pauli operator p ∈ Pn
L5001@P85: can be factorized as the product of an X and a Z operator pX and pZ, we can identify it with a binary vector
L5002@P85: p¯ = (p¯X , p¯Z)T ∈ F2n
L5003@P85: 2 , where the ith entry of p¯X /p¯Z is 1 if
L5004@P85: and only if pX /pZ acts nontrivially on the ith qubit. Given
L5005@P85: a Pauli operator p, its weight |p| is the number of qubits
L5006@P85: on which its action is not the identity. Consider a stabilizer
L5007@P85: code with syndrome function σ(·), then the reduced weight
L5008@P85: of a Pauli operator p ∈ Pn on the physical qubits is
L5009@P85: |p|
L5010@P85: red := min{|q| : σ(q) = σ(p), q ∈ Pn}.
L5011@P85: A stabilizer code is said to be distance d if d is the minimum weight of a Pauli operator not in S that has trivial
L5012@P85: syndrome. We refer to a code of length n, dimension k,
L5013@P85: and distance d as a [[n, k, d]] code.
L5014@P85: For a stabilizer code, we then have the following.
L5015@P85: Definition 1 (Confinement). Let t be an integer and f :
L5016@P85: Z → R some increasing function with f (0) = 0. We say
L5017@P85: that a stabilizer code has (t, f ) confinement if, for all errors
L5018@P85: e with |e|
L5019@P85: red ≤ t, it holds
L5020@P85: f (|σ(e)|) ≥|e|
L5021@P85: red.
L5022@P85: The reduced weight of operators is crucial in this
L5023@P85: definition to avoid making confinement fail by adding stabilizer operators to arbitrarily increase the weight of an
L5024@P85: otherwise low weight error.
L5025@P85: Let us contrast this with Bombín’s notion of confinement (Definition 16 of Ref. [10]) that has some similarities but allows only for linear functions of the form
L5026@P85: f (x) = κx for some constant κ. Many codes, including
L5027@P85: 3D product codes, have superlinear confinement functions,
L5028@P85: as such Bombín’s definition does not encompass them.
L5029@P85: Moreover, the concept of confinement is closely related
L5030@P85: to soundness [18] but it is weaker and so able to encompass more families of codes, such as the expander codes
L5031@P85: [19–21], which are confined but not sound. Roughly speaking, a code has good confinement if small qubit errors
L5032@P85: produce small measurement syndromes; this differs from
L5033@P85: good soundness, which entails that small syndromes can
L5034@P85: be produced by small errors.
L5035@P85: Formally, we define the following notion of good confinement for a family of stabilizer codes.
L5036@P85: Definition 2 (Good confinement). Consider an infinite
L5037@P85: family of stabilizer codes. We say that the family has good
L5038@P85: confinement if each code in it has (t, f ) confinement, where
L5039@P85: the following holds:
L5040@P85: 1. t grows with the length n of the code: t ∈ (nb) with
L5041@P85: b > 0;
L5042@P85: 2. and f (·) is monotonically increasing and independent of n.
L5043@P85: We say the code family has good X confinement if the
L5044@P85: above holds only for Pauli-Z errors.
L5045@P85: Our main analytic result is that codes with good confinement are single shot.
L5046@P85: Theorem 1. Consider a family of [[n, k, d]] quantumLDPC codes with good confinement and growing distance
L5047@P85: d ≥ anb with a > 0 and b > 0. This code family is single
L5048@P85: shot for the adversarial noise model. If the code family only
L5049@P85: has good X confinement then it is single shot with respect
L5050@P85: to Pauli-Z noise.
L5051@P85: We conjecture that the result of Theorem 1 can be
L5052@P85: extended to deal with local stochastic noise and used to
L5053@P85: show that LDPC codes with good confinement have a
L5054@P85: single-shot threshold. In this direction, we are able to prove
L5055@P85: that linear confinement is sufficient for codes to exhibit a
L5056@P85: single-shot threshold in the local stochastic noise setting.
L5057@P85: Theorem 2. Consider a family of [[n, k, d]] quantumLDPC codes with qubit degree at most ω − 1 and good
L5058@P85: linear confinement such that d ≥ anb with a > 0 and b >
L5059@P85: 0. This code family has a sustainable single-shot threshold for any local stochastic noise model. If the code family
L5060@P85: only has good X confinement then it has a sustainable
L5061@P85: single-shot threshold with respect to Pauli-Z noise.
L5062@P85: We further prove that 3D product codes have X confinement.
L5063@P85: Theorem 3. All 3D product codes have (t, f ) X confinement, where t is equal to the Z distance of the code and
L5064@P85: f (x) = x3/4 or better.
L5065@P85: Theorems 1 and 3 together motivate our numerical
L5066@P85: experiments reported in Sec. V.
L5067@P85: We now proceed to prove Theorem 1. To this end, we
L5068@P85: use the shadow decoder that we introduce in Definition 3.
L5069@P85: The shadow decoder differs from previous single-shot two
L5070@P85: stage decoders (e.g., the MW single-shot decoder introduced in Definition 6 of [18]) in that it does not rely on
L5071@P85: metachecks on syndromes. If syndromes are protected by
L5072@P85: a classical code, as is the case for X syndromes of 3D
L5073@P85: product codes introduced in Sec. IV, then a single-shot
L5074@P85: decoding strategy could work as follows: (1) correct the
L5075@P85: measured syndrome whenever it does not satisfy all the
L5076@P85: constraints defined by the metacode; (2) find a recovery
L5077@P85: operator on qubits that has syndrome equal to the one
L5078@P85: found at point (1). The shadow decoder, instead, corrects
L5079@P85-86: 020340-4SINGLE-SHOT ERROR CORRECTION OF THREE-DIMENSIONAL. . . PRX QUANTUM 2, 020340 (2021)
L5080@P86: the syndrome both anytime it fails to satisfy all the constraints of the metacode and when it is generated by high
L5081@P86: weight errors. We do not describe how to implement it or
L5082@P86: make statements concerning the complexity of decoding.
L5083@P86: Our proof makes similar assumptions as the KovalevPryadko quantum-LDPC threshold theorem [37] where
L5084@P86: they assumed a minimum-weight decoder without addressing implementation issues. Indeed, decoding for arbitrary
L5085@P86: LDPC codes is an nondeterministic polynomial-time complete (NP complete) problem that we do not expect to be
L5086@P86: efficiently solvable in full generality.
L5087@P86: The building blocks of the shadow decoder are the
L5088@P86: tshadows of the code. A t shadow is a set in the syndrome
L5089@P86: space, which contains all the images of Pauli errors e on the
L5090@P86: physical qubits that have weight at most t. In other words,
L5091@P86: if we identify Pauli errors e on n qubits with 2n-bit strings
L5092@P86: and we consider the metric space M = F2n
L5093@P86: 2 endowed with
L5094@P86: the Hamming distance [i.e., the distance d(e¯1, e¯2) between
L5095@P86: the vectors e¯1 and e¯2 corresponding to the Pauli errors
L5096@P86: e1 and e2, respectively, is defined as d(e¯1, e¯2) =|e1 + e2|]
L5097@P86: then the t shadow of the code is the image, via the syndrome function σ(·), of the ball of radius t centered at 0
L5098@P86: inM. Note that, because balls onMare not vector spaces,
L5099@P86: the shadows are not vector spaces either.
L5100@P86: We are now ready to introduce the shadow decoder.
L5101@P86: Definition 3 (Shadow decoder). The shadow decoder has
L5102@P86: variable parameter t > 0. Given an observed syndrome
L5103@P86: s¯ = σ(e) +¯se where s¯e ∈ Fm
L5104@P86: 2 is the syndrome error, the
L5105@P86: shadow decoder of parameter t performs the following two
L5106@P86: steps:
L5107@P86: 1. Syndrome repair: find a binary vector s¯r of minimum
L5108@P86: weight |¯sr| such that s¯ +¯sr belongs to the t shadow
L5109@P86: of the code, where
L5110@P86: t shadow ={σ(e) : |e|≤ t}.
L5111@P86: 2. Qubit decode: find er of minimum weight |er| such
L5112@P86: that σ(er) =¯s +¯sr.
L5113@P86: We call r = er · e the residual error.
L5114@P86: A key result in proving Theorem 1 is the following
L5115@P86: promise on the performance of the shadow decoder: when
L5116@P86: a code has confinement, the weight of the residual error
L5117@P86: after one decoding cycle is bounded by a function of the
L5118@P86: weight of the syndrome error.

L5198@P87: Theorem 2 is proven in Appendix A. There, we introduce a novel notion of weight to describe local stochastic
L5199@P87: errors: the closeness weight. We then present the stochastic shadow decoder, a variant of the (adversarial) shadow
L5200@P87: decoder of Definition 3. Importantly, on confined codes, it
L5201@P87: keeps the closeness weight of the residual error under control over repeated correction cycles. Finally, the proof of
L5202@P87: Theorem 2 follows by combining these results with some
L5203@P87: classic percolation theory bounds.
L5204@P87: The proof of Theorem 3 is very technical and is deferred
L5205@P87: to Appendix C. It is an adaption of the one of soundness
L5206@P87: for 4D codes given in Ref. [18], and it is reported in this
L5207@P87: paper for completeness. We remind the reader that, for our
L5208@P87: numerical studies on 3D product codes, we do not use the
L5209@P87: shadow decoder, but rather heuristics that perform well
L5210@P87: in practice. In particular, we use a two-stage decoder that
L5211@P87: exploits a metacheck structure on syndromes and attempts
L5212@P87: to repair the syndrome if and only if it does not pass all
L5213@P87: metachecks (see Sec. VC).
L5214@P87: Our main motivation to introduce the concept of confinement and the shadow decoder was to find a feature of
L5215@P87: codes able to encompass all known examples of singleshot codes. Campbell [18] introduced the notion of soundness and showed that this property is a sufficient condition
L5216@P87: for codes to show single-shot properties in the adversarial setting. Nonetheless, Fawzi et al. [22] showed that
L5217@P87: expander codes have a single-shot threshold for local
L5218@P87: stochastic noise, even though they do not have the soundness property. As already said though, expander codes do
L5219@P87: have confinement. In Corollary 9 of Ref. [20] the authors
L5220@P87: prove that their confinement function is linear and call
L5221@P87: this property robustness. Confinement, in other words, fills
L5222@P87: the gap leaved by the concept of soundness. Furthermore,
L5223@P87: as Lemma 2 states, it is a requirement strictly weaker
L5224@P87: than soundness: any LDPC family of codes with good
L5225@P87: soundness has good confinement.
L5226@P87: Definition 4 (Soundness [18]). Let t be an integer and
L5227@P87: f : Z → R be a function with f (0) = 0. Given a stabilizer code with syndrome map σ(·) we say it is (t, f )
L5228@P87: sound if for all error sets e with |σ(e)|≤ t it follows that
L5229@P87: f (|σ(e)|) ≥|e|
L5230@P87: red.
L5231@P87: Definition 5 (Good soundness [18]). Consider an infinite
L5232@P87: family of codes with syndrome maps σn(·). We say that the
L5233@P87: family has good soundness if each code in it is (t, f ) sound
L5234@P87: where the following holds:
L5235@P87: 1. t grows with n such that t ∈ (nb) with b > 0;
L5236@P87: 2. and f (·) is monotonically increasing and independent of n.
L5237@P87: It follows easily from Campbell’s definition of soundness and our definition of confinement that the former
L5238@P87: entails the latter.
L5239@P87: Lemma 2. Consider a LDPC code that is (t, f ) sound with
L5240@P87: f increasing. If its qubit degree is at most ω, then it has
L5241@P87: ((t/ω), f ) confinement.
L5242@P87: If e is an error set with |e|
L5243@P87: red ≤ t/ω, for its syndrome the
L5244@P87: following holds:
L5245@P87: |σ(e)|≤
L5246@P87: t
L5247@P87: ω
L5248@P87: × ω = t. (11)
L5249@P87: By soundness of the code,
L5250@P87: f (|σ(e)|) ≥|e|
L5251@P87: red. (12)
L5252@P87: In conclusion, confinement successfully describes general and inclusive properties related to single-shot error
L5253@P87: correction. More than that, good confinement is a requirement strictly weaker than good soundness as the following
L5254@P87: example illustrates. We consider the quantum expander
L5255@P87: code family of Ref. [20], which has the following four
L5256@P87: properties: (i) they have full-rank parity-check matrices;
L5257@P87: (ii) they have (t,3x) confinement, with t ∈ (d); (iii) for
L5258@P87: every small error e with |e|≤ 3, we have |σ(e)| > 1 (see
L5259@P87: Appendix D for details).
L5260@P87: By property (i), every syndrome is a valid syndrome and
L5261@P87: we can consider some weight-1 syndrome s. Assume to the
L5262@P87: contrary that there exists an |e|≤ t giving the syndrome
L5263@P87: s = σ(e), then by (t,3x) confinement
L5264@P87: 3|σ(e)|≥|e|
L5265@P87: red,
L5266@P87: and plugging in |σ(e)|= 1 gives
L5267@P87: 3 ≥|e|
L5268@P87: red.
L5269@P87: We know by property (iii) that this would entail |σ(e)| > 1,
L5270@P87: which leads to a contraction. Therefore, the assumption
L5271@P87: |e|≤ t must be false and so |e| > t.
L5272@P87: Therefore, if the code family had (τ , ϕ) soundness, then
L5273@P87: ϕ(1)> t. (13)
L5274@P87: Because ϕ is the same across the whole family, and t ∈
L5275@P87: (d) increases proportionally to the code distance, by
L5276@P87: considering bigger codes if necessary, this leads to a contradiction. In other words, we show that this family of
L5277@P87: expander codes with good linear confinement cannot have
L5278@P87: good soundness.
L5279@P87: The remainder of this paper is devoted to the study of the
L5280@P87: 3D product codes. We recall their construction in Sec. IV
L5281@P87: and we numerically assess their single-shot performance
L5282@P87: under local stochastic noise in Sec. V.

L5287@P88: 2 is a group homomorphism

L5307@P88: on the physical qubits. Following the nomenclature from
L5308@P88: classical coding theory, we refer to the syndrome matrix
L5309@P88: H as parity-check matrix and we say that a code is LDPC
L5310@P88: when its parity check is low density.
L5311@P88: A stabilizer code is a Calderbank-Shor Steane (CSS)
L5312@P88: code if its stabilizer group can be generated by the disjoint

L5322@P88: for the Z component. In fact, if a Pauli operator has vector
L5323@P88: representation (p¯X , p¯Z)T = (p¯X ,0)T + (0, p¯Z)T ∈ F2n

L5367@P88: as a [[n, k, dx, dz]] code.
L5368@P88: For our purposes, it is useful to describe CSS codes in
L5369@P88: terms of chain complexes. We first explain how a CSS code
L5370@P88: yields a chain complex and then how to define valid CSS
L5371@P88: codes starting from chain complexes. This last step ultimately allows us to use a standard method to iteratively
L5372@P88: build chain complexes, namely the product of complexes,
L5373@P88: to build interesting CSS codes (see, for instance, Ref. [38]
L5374@P88: for a comprehensive discussion on the subject).
L5375@P88: Consider a CSS code C defined by the syndrome maps
L5376@P88: HX and HZ of size mx × n and mz × n respectively. The
L5377@P88: sequence of maps and vector spaces,
L5378@P88: Fmz

L5386@P88: the dimension of the vector space in the middle, n, is the
L5387@P88: length of the code, and the dimensions mx and mz of the
L5388@P88: external spaces are, respectively, the number of X and Z
L5389@P88: stabilizer generators. The logical dimension k of the code

L5395@P88: We use ker H for the kernel of H, which is the set of all v
L5396@P88: such that Hv = 0. We use Im H for the image of H, which
L5397@P88: is the set of all vectors w that can be written as w = Hv for
L5398@P88: some v. The distances dx and dz are given by
L5399@P88: dx = min{|v| such that v ∈ ker HZ, v ∈ Im(HT

L5403@P88: Lastly, because rows of HX and HZ represent the support
L5404@P88: of X and Z stabilizer generators, respectively, we can also
L5405@P88: verify that X and Z stabilizers commute by assuring that
L5406@P88: the scalar product of each row of HX and any row of HZ
L5407@P88: (or equivalently each row of HZ and any row of HX ) is 0
L5408@P88: in F2. In fact, this is equivalent to verifying that the supports of any two X and Z stabilizer generators have even
L5409@P88: overlap and therefore that they represent commuting Pauli
L5410@P88: operators. In other words, for HX and HZ it holds that
L5411@P88: HX HT

L5418@P88: of length 2 or greater determines a CSS code.

L5438@P89: Importantly, the parameters [[n, k, dx, dz]] of C(Ci) all have

L5483@P89: (a1 + a2)⊗b1 = a1⊗b1 + a2⊗b1,
L5484@P89: a1⊗(b1 + b2) = a1⊗b1 + a1⊗b2.

L5571@P90: It is easy to verify that the chain complex (C

L5582@P90: We refer to the matrix M = δ2 as the metacheck matrix for
L5583@P90: the X stabilizers. Condition (18) entails MHX = 0 and as
L5584@P90: a consequence we can think of the matrix M as a paritycheck matrix on the X syndromes: any valid X syndrome
L5585@P90: satisfies the constraints defined by M.
L5586@P90: Remarkably, the parameters of the code C(δA, δB, δC) can
L5587@P90: be derived in terms of the properties of the seed matrices δA, δB, δC. In fact, let [n	, k	, δ	]/[nT

L5592@P90: complex (C
