L5604@P90: By convention, the distance of a code with dimension 0 is
L5605@P90: ∞. We define the single-shot distance dSS [18] of the chain
L5606@P90: complex (C
L5607@P90: 
L5608@P90: 
L5609@P90: ) as the minimum weight of a vector in C2
L5610@P90: that satisfies all the constraints given by δ2 (i.e., it belongs
L5611@P90: to the kernel of δ2) but is not a valid X syndrome (i.e., it
L5612@P90: does not belong to the image of δ1). In other words, dSS is
L5613@P90: the minimum weight of a vector in the second homology
L5614@P90: group H2 = ker δ2/ Im δ1 of the chain complex C. Following Ref. [40] it is easy to verify that dSS = min{da, db, dc}
L5615@P90: if H2 = 0 and ∞ otherwise.
L5616@P90: It is important to note that, if the matrices δ	 are LDPC,
L5617@P90: then their 3D product code is quantum LDPC. In fact, if δ
L5618@P90: has column (row) of weight bounded by c	 (r	), then δi has
L5619@P90: column and row weight bounded by ci and ri, respectively,
L5620@P90: where
L5621@P90: i. c0 ≤ ca + cb + cc and r0 ≤ max{ra,rb,rc};
L5622@P90: ii. c1 ≤ max{ca + cb, ca + cc, cb + cc}
L5623@P90: and
L5624@P90: r1 ≤ max{ra + rb,ra + rc,rb + rc};
L5625@P90: iii. c2 ≤ max{ca, cb, cc} and r2 ≤ ra + rb + rc.
L5626@P90: A. On geometric locality
L5627@P90: In addition to preserving the LDPC properties of the
L5628@P90: seed matrices, the 3D product yields local codes when
L5629@P90: qubits are placed on edges of a 3D cubic lattice. We defer
L5630@P90: the reader to Appendix B for a thorough discussion on the
L5631@P90: embedding of 3D product codes on a cubic lattice and we
L5632@P90: here present a loose summary.
L5633@P90: Qubits of a 3D product code associated to the chain complex (C
L5634@P90: 
L5635@P90: 
L5636@P90: ) are in bijection with basis elements of the space
L5637@P90: C1; since C1 is the direct sum of the three vector spaces
L5638@P90: C1
L5639@P90: A⊗C0
L5640@P90: B⊗C0
L5641@P90: C, C0A⊗C1
L5642@P90: B⊗C0
L5643@P90: C and C0A⊗C0
L5644@P90: B⊗C1
L5645@P90: C we introduce
L5646@P90: three different types of qubits: transverse, vertical, and
L5647@P90: horizontal. Qubit types naturally correspond to the three
L5648@P90: different orientations of edges on a cubic lattice, namely
L5649@P90: edges parallel to each of the three crystal planes. Referring to this particular display of qubits, the stabilizers of
L5650@P90: the code defined by Eq. (C
L5651@P90: 
L5652@P90: 
L5653@P90: ) have support as follows:
L5654@P90: 1. X stabilizers have support on a two-dimensional
L5655@P90: (2D) cross of qubits of two types out of three, contained in one of the three crystal planes; the crossing
L5656@P90: is defined by a square face of a cube (see Fig. 7);
L5657@P90: 2. Z stabilizers have support on a 3D cross of qubits,
L5658@P90: with crossing defined by a vertex of a cube (see
L5659@P90: Fig. 8).
L5660@P90: The cubic lattice considered can present some irregularities: in general it is a cubic lattice with some missing
L5661@P90: edges. Nonetheless, square faces and vertices are uniquely
L5662@P90: defined and they correspond to a stabilizer every time they
L5663@P90: contain at least one edge. More specifically, a square face
L5664@P90: identifies two perpendicular lines of edges i.e., qubits on
L5665@P90: a plane, which are the edges parallel to the boundary of
L5666@P90: the square face itself. The corresponding X stabilizer has
L5667@P90: support contained on those lines of edges i.e., qubits. Similarly, a vertex identifies three perpendicular lines of qubits
L5668@P90: and the corresponding Z stabilizer has support there contained. When combined with some locality properties of
L5669@P90: the seed matrices, this characteristic “cross shape” of the
L5670@P90: stabilizers support entails that 3D product codes are local
L5671@P90: on a cubic lattice (Proposition 1 in Appendix B). Here,
L5672@P90: by locality, we mean that for some positive integer ρ, the
L5673@P90: following hold:
L5674@P90: 1. any X -stabilizer generator has weight at most 2ρ
L5675@P90: with support contained in a 2D box of size ρ × ρ;
L5676@P90: 2. any Z-stabilizer generator has weight at most 3ρ
L5677@P90: with support contained in a 3D box of size ρ × ρ ×
L5678@P90: ρ.
L5679@P90: Interestingly, it follows easily as a corollary of our locality
L5680@P90: proof that the 3D toric and surface codes are in fact 3D
L5681@P90: product codes. We now detail an explicit construction of
L5682@P90: the 3D toric and surface codes as 3D product codes and we
L5683@P90: refer the reader to Appendix B for further details.
L5684@P90-91: 020340-9QUINTAVALLE, VASMER, ROFFE, and CAMPBELL PRX QUANTUM 2, 020340 (2021)
L5685@P91: The 3D toric code is the 3D product code obtained by
L5686@P91: choosing δA = δB = δC = δ as seed matrices, where δ is
L5687@P91: the parity-check matrix of the repetition code. For instance,
L5688@P91: the 3D toric code with linear lattice size L = 3 is given by
L5689@P91: δ =
L5690@P91: ⎛
L5691@P91: ⎝
L5692@P91: 110
L5693@P91: 011
L5694@P91: 101
L5695@P91: ⎞
L5696@P91: ⎠ .
L5697@P91: In general, the 3D toric code of lattice size L, has
L5698@P91: parameters
L5699@P91: [[3L3
L5700@P91: , 3, L, L2]]
L5701@P91: and single-shot distance dSS = L.
L5702@P91: The 3D surface code is obtained from this construction
L5703@P91: by choosing, for linear lattice size L = 3,
L5704@P91: δA = δB =
L5705@P91: 
L5706@P91: 110

L5709@P91: δC =

L5718@P91: and single-shot distance dSS =∞. Further details can be
L5719@P91: found in Appendix B.
L5720@P91: It should be noted that the surface and toric codes are
L5721@P91: special instances of 3D product codes that have geometrically local stabilizers. This is beneficial, as it means they
L5722@P91: could be implemented on a quantum computer using only
L5723@P91: nearest-neighbor (in 3D) interactions between qubits. The
L5724@P91: disadvantage of the 3D surface and toric codes is that
L5725@P91: they have fixed dimension, encoding only one and three
L5726@P91: qubits, respectively. The 3D product construction can be
L5727@P91: used to obtain codes that are not geometrically local, but
L5728@P91: have improved encoding rates over the surface and toric
L5729@P91: codes. We refer to these codes as “nontopological” codes
L5730@P91: and investigate their decoding in Sec. VE.
L5731@P91: V. NUMERICS
L5732@P91: To assess the single-shot performance of the 3D product
L5733@P91: codes, we simulate the decoding of phase-flip (Z) errors.
L5734@P91: As 3D product codes are CSS codes, the relevant stabilizers are the X stabilizers. Let e¯Z ∈ Fn
L5735@P91: 2 describe the
L5736@P91: support of a phase-flip error, i.e., (e¯Z)i = 1 if qubit i has
L5737@P91: a phase-flip error. The syndrome, s¯X , of this error is then
L5738@P91: s¯X = HX e¯Z, (19)
L5739@P91: where HX ∈ Fm×n
L5740@P91: 2 is the parity-check matrix of the X
L5741@P91: stabilizers of the code [see Eq. (14)].
L5742@P91: Owing to the chain-complex structure of 3D product
L5743@P91: codes (outlined in Sec. IV) the syndromes s¯X are themselves the codewords of a classical linear code with paritycheck matrix M such that Ms¯X = 0 for all s¯X ∈ Im(HX ).
L5744@P91: We refer to such a code on the syndromes as a metacode.
L5745@P91: The metacheck matrix can be used to detect and correct
L5746@P91: syndrome noise.
L5747@P91: In a two-stage single-shot decoder, stage-1 decoding
L5748@P91: corrects the syndrome noise using M before stage-2 decoding corrects the data qubits. In general, decoding is an
L5749@P91: NP-complete problem that cannot be solved exactly in
L5750@P91: polynomial time. However, good heuristic techniques exist
L5751@P91: that allow approximate solutions to be efficiently computed. In this work, we use two such decoding methods:
L5752@P91: minimum-weight perfect matching and belief propagation plus ordered statistics decoding. Both MWPM and
L5753@P91: BP+OSD are algorithms that run over graphical models
L5754@P91: that encapsulate the structure of the code. We now briefly
L5755@P91: describe each decoder.
L5756@P91: A. Minimum-weight perfect matching
L5757@P91: The minimum-weight perfect-matching decoder is useful for codes in which chains of errors produce weight-2
L5758@P91: syndromes. The method works by mapping the decoding
L5759@P91: problem to a graph where nodes represent the code’s stabilizer generators and weighted edges represent error chains
L5760@P91: of different lengths. For a given pair of unsatisfied stabilizers, MWPM deduces the shortest error chain that could
L5761@P91: have caused it [41].
L5762@P91: MWPM finds use for a variety of topological codes,
L5763@P91: most famously for the 2D surface and toric codes [2,42–
L5764@P91: 45]. For 3D codes, MWPM is a suitable candidate for
L5765@P91: the syndrome-repair step referred to as stage-1 decoding.
L5766@P91: Specifically, the syndrome of a phase-flip error can be
L5767@P91: viewed as a collection of closed loops of edges in a simple cubic lattice [46] (with boundary conditions depending
L5768@P91: on the code). Measurement errors cause loops of syndrome to be broken, and the job of stage-1 decoding is to
L5769@P91: repair these broken syndromes. To obtain the corresponding matching problem, we create a complete graph whose
L5770@P91: vertices correspond to the break-points of the broken syndrome loops, with edge weights that are equal to the path
L5771@P91: lengths between the break points. We use the Blossom V
L5772@P91: [47] implementation of Edmonds’s algorithm to solve this
L5773@P91: matching problem. The edges in the matching correspond
L5774@P91: to the syndrome-recovery operators.
L5775@P91: B. Belief propagation + ordered statistics decoding
L5776@P91: Belief propagation is an algorithm for performing inference on sparse graphs that finds widespread use in highperformance classical coding. Classical LDPC codes, for
L5777@P91: example, achieve performance close to the Shannon limit
L5778@P91: when decoded with BP [48]. In the context of quantum
L5779@P91-92: 020340-10SINGLE-SHOT ERROR CORRECTION OF THREE-DIMENSIONAL. . . PRX QUANTUM 2, 020340 (2021)
L5780@P92: coding, BP is useful for codes that do not produce pairs of
L5781@P92: syndromes and therefore cannot be decoded with MWPM.
L5782@P92: The BP algorithm maps the decoding problem to a
L5783@P92: bipartite factor graph where the two node species represent data qubits and syndromes, respectively. Graph edges
L5784@P92: are drawn between the data and syndrome nodes according to the code’s parity-check matrix. The factor graph is
L5785@P92: designed to provide a factorization of the probability distribution that describes the relationship between syndromes
L5786@P92: and errors. The BP algorithm proceeds by iteratively passing “beliefs” between data and syndrome nodes, at each
L5787@P92: step updating the probability that a data node is errored.
L5788@P92: The algorithm terminates once the probability distribution
L5789@P92: implies an error pattern that satisfies the inputted syndrome. For a full description of the BP algorithm we direct
L5790@P92: the reader to Ref. [49].
L5791@P92: For quantum codes, the standard BP algorithm alone
L5792@P92: does not achieve good decoding performance due to the
L5793@P92: presence of degenerate errors. These cause “split beliefs”
L5794@P92: and prevent the algorithm from terminating. Various methods have been proposed for adapting BP decoding to
L5795@P92: quantum codes [27,50–54]. A particularly effective recent
L5796@P92: proposal involves combining BP with a postprocessing
L5797@P92: technique known as ordered statistics decoding [55]. The
L5798@P92: OSD step uses the probability distribution outputted by BP
L5799@P92: to select a low-weight recovery operator that satisfies the
L5800@P92: syndrome equation.
L5801@P92: The BP+OSD algorithm was first applied to quantum
L5802@P92: expander codes by Panteleev and Kalachev [55]. Following this, Roffe et al. [56] demonstrated that the BP+OSD
L5803@P92: decoder applies more widely across a broad range of
L5804@P92: quantum-LDPC codes, including the 2D surface and toric
L5805@P92: codes. For this work, we use the software implementation of BP+OSD from Ref. [56], which can be downloaded
L5806@P92: from Ref. [57].
L5807@P92: C. The two-stage single-shot decoding algorithm
L5808@P92: Our simulations of the two-stage single-shot decoder
L5809@P92: employ two strategies. (1) MWPM and BP+OSD: stage-1
L5810@P92: decoding is performed using MWPM and stage-2 decoding uses BP+OSD. (2) BP+OSD × 2: both stages are
L5811@P92: BP+OSD.
L5812@P92: Algorithm 1 describes our methodology for the simulations of the two-stage single-shot decoder.
L5813@P92: The 3D toric code has a failure mode that is not present
L5814@P92: in the 3D surface code. In such codes, syndromes s¯X exist
L5815@P92: that satisfy all of the metachecks, Ms¯X = 0, but are invalid
L5816@P92: syndromes, meaning that s¯X does not belong to the image
L5817@P92: of HX . In other words, s¯X is invalid if there is no error
L5818@P92: vector e¯Z ∈ C1 with syndrome s¯X but s¯X is a codeword of
L5819@P92: the metacode.
L5820@P92: More generally, referring to the chain complex (C
L5821@P92: 
L5822@P92: 
L5823@P92: ):
L5824@P92: C0
L5825@P92: δ0 −→
L5826@P92: HT
L5827@P92: Z
L5828@P92: C1
L5829@P92: δ1 −→
L5830@P92: HX
L5831@P92: C2
L5832@P92: δ2
L5833@P92: −→
L5834@P92: M
L5835@P92: C3
L5836@P92: we see that nonvalid syndromes do exist whenever Im δ1 
L5837@P92: ker δ2. In the homology language, we say that invalid syndromes are nontrivial elements of the second homology
L5838@P92: group:
L5839@P92: H2 = ker δ2/ Im δ1 = ker M/ Im HX .
L5840@P92: If km is the dimension of H2, the set of invalid syndromes
L5841@P92: is a vector subspace of C2 of dimension km whose vectors
L5842@P92: can be written as u¯ + HX e¯Z where u¯ is a representative of
L5843@P92: the equivalence class [u¯] ∈ H2 and e¯Z ∈ C1. Thus, if FM is
L5844@P92: a matrix whose columns are km vectors in C2 that generate
L5845@P92: H2 (meaning that they belong to km different equivalence
L5846@P92: classes in H2), we can write any invalid syndrome s¯X as
L5847@P92: s¯X = FM v¯ + HX e¯Z, (20)
L5848@P92-93: 020340-11QUINTAVALLE, VASMER, ROFFE, and CAMPBELL PRX QUANTUM 2, 020340 (2021)
L5849@P93: where v¯ ∈ Fkm
L5850@P93: 2 is nonzero if and only if s¯X is invalid and
L5851@P93: e¯Z is any error vector in C1.
L5852@P93: By duality on (C
L5853@P93:

L5855@P93: ), the second cohomolgy group:
L5856@P93: H∗

L5858@P93: has order km too. If LM is a matrix whose km rows generates
L5859@P93: H∗
L5860@P93: 2, then the product  = LM FM has full rank km because
L5861@P93: both LM and FM have full rank. Moreover, since the rows
L5862@P93: of LM in particular belongs to ker HT
L5863@P93: X , it holds LM HX = 0.
L5864@P93: Combining these two observations with Eq. (20) yields
L5865@P93: LM s¯X = LM FM v¯ + LM HX e¯Z
L5866@P93: = v¯,
L5867@P93: where v¯ = 0 if and only if v¯ = 0 because  is full rank.
L5868@P93: In conclusion, we find that
L5869@P93: LM s¯X = 0
L5870@P93: if and only if s¯X is an invalid syndrome. As a consequence,
L5871@P93: we can assess whether a syndrome is invalid or not by calculating this product. The meaning of matrices LM and FM
L5872@P93: can be understood by looking at elements in H2 and H∗
L5873@P93: 2
L5874@P93: as logical operators of a CSS code defined on (C

L5878@P93: qubits in C2 (see Sec. IV). In this setting, the full-rank condition rank(LM FM ) = km translates in the anticommuting
L5879@P93: relation between logical X and logical Z operators of the
L5880@P93: code.
L5881@P93: In the 3D toric code, these invalid syndromes are loops
L5882@P93: of edges around one of the handles of the torus, and are
L5883@P93: equivalent to the logical operators of the metacode. It
L5884@P93: is therefore possible to check whether stage-1 decoding
L5885@P93: results in such a failure by checking whether the repaired
L5886@P93: syndrome anticommutes with a matrix LM whose rows
L5887@P93: generate the relevant group of the logical operators of
L5888@P93: the metacode. When a metacode failure is encountered,
L5889@P93: a failure-mode subroutine (line 13 of Algorithm 1) is
L5890@P93: called that forces the repaired syndrome into the correct
L5891@P93: form. This subroutine involves using BP+OSD to decode
L5892@P93: a modified version of the metacheck matrix M
L5893@P93:  defined as
L5894@P93: follows:
L5895@P93: M
L5896@P93:  =
L5897@P93:  M
L5898@P93: LM
L5899@P93: 
L5900@P93: . (21)
L5901@P93: The additional constraints in the modified metacheck
L5902@P93: matrix ensure that the repaired syndrome is never an
L5903@P93: invalid syndrome. We use this subroutine only when we
L5904@P93: have an invalid syndrome (rather than all the time) as the
L5905@P93: LM component causes M
L5906@P93:  to have higher maximum row
L5907@P93: and column weights than M, resulting in a reduction in BP
L5908@P93: decoding performance. Indeed, the rows of LM must have
L5909@P93: weight lower bounded by the transpose distances of the
L5910@P93: seed codes [58]. Since the transpose distances of the seed
L5911@P93: 0.0 0.5 1.0 1.5 2.0 2.5 3.0
L5912@P93: ×10−2

L5940@P93: D. 3D toric and surface codes
L5941@P93: We estimate the sustainable threshold of the 3D toric and
L5942@P93: surface codes using our two decoding strategies. For codecapacity noise (i.e., perfect syndrome measurements), the
L5943@P93: syndrome-repair step is not required, so both decoding
L5944@P93: strategies are the same. For each code family, we observe
L5945@P93: a code capacity threshold of pth ≈ 21.6%, as illustrated in
L5946@P93: Fig. 3. To obtain our threshold estimates, we use the standard critical exponent method [59] (see Appendix E for
L5947@P93: details). In the single-shot setting, we find similar performance for both our decoding strategies, as summarized in
L5948@P93: Table II. Our results compare favorably with the performance of other decoders, which we list in Table I. We

L5978@P94: We remark that the sustainable threshold that we observe
L5979@P94: for the 3D toric code is very close to the threshold of
L5980@P94: MWPM for stringlike errors in the 3D toric code [60]. This
L5981@P94: implies that the performance of decoder 1 (the syndromerepair step) is limiting the performance of the entire decoding procedure, as was suggested in Ref. [13]. Although the
L5982@P94: sustainable thresholds we observe for 3D surface codes
L5983@P94: are slightly higher than for 3D toric codes, the codes we
L5984@P94: consider are relatively small, which means that boundary effects may be having an impact on our sustainable
L5985@P94: threshold estimates.
L5986@P94: We also investigate the suppression of the logical error
L5987@P94: rate below threshold in the 3D toric code, using MWPM
L5988@P94: and BP+OSD. We use the following ansatz for the logical
L5989@P94: error rate for values of p < pth,
L5990@P94: pfail(L) ∝ (p/pth)
L5991@P94: αLβ
L5992@P94: , (22)
L5993@P94: where α and β are parameters to be determined. The code
L5994@P94: distance of the 3D toric code for Z errors is L2, so if
L5995@P94: the decoder is correcting errors up to this size, we would
L5996@P94: expect β ≈ 2. Using the fitting procedure described in
L5997@P94: Appendix E, we estimate β = 1.91(3) for N = 0 (code
L5998@P94: capacity) and β = 1.15(3) for N = 8 (eight rounds of
L5999@P94: single-shot error correction). Therefore, for the (relatively
L6000@P94: small) codes that we consider, we find evidence that
L6001@P94: BP+OSD is correcting errors of weight up to the code
L6002@P94: distance. Viewed as an error-correction problem, the distance of the syndrome-repair step of decoding (i.e., the
L6003@P94: single-shot distance dSS) is L, which is consistent with our
L6004@P94: observed value of β in the single-shot case. This provides
L6005@P94: further evidence that the bottleneck of our single-shot
L6006@P94: decoding procedure is the syndrome-repair step.
L6007@P94: E. Nontopological codes
L6008@P94: Up to this point, we explore the single-shot decoding performance of the 3D surface and toric codes. As
L6009@P94: explained in Sec. IV A, the 3D surface and toric codes
L6010@P94: are topological codes obtained by taking the 3D product
L6011@P94: of classical repetition codes. In this section, we extend our
L6012@P94: numerical analysis to nontopological codes constructed via
L6013@P94: the 3D product of random classical codes. Our motivation
L6014@P94: for investigating nontopological codes is twofold. First, by
L6015@P94: demonstrating that a random 3D product code has a sustainable threshold, we provide evidence for our conjecture
L6016@P94: that the results of Theorems 1 and 3 extend to the stochastic
L6017@P94: noise setting. Second, we provide evidence that BP+OSD
L6018@P94: is a general decoding method that applies beyond the class
L6019@P94: of well-studied topological 3D product codes.
L6020@P94: Table III shows a family of nontopological codes
L6021@P94: C(δA, δB, δC) obtained by taking the 3D product of a random code, δA, with two codes obtained from full-rank
L6022@P94: repetition codes, δB and δC. For this example, we choose
L6023@P94: δA to be a classical LDPC code constructed by randomly generating parity checks under the constraint that
L6024@P94: the parity-check matrix δA has column and row weights
L6025@P94: upper bounded by three and four, respectively. The specific
L6026@P94: advantage of nontopological codes is that they can have
L6027@P94: nonfixed dimension. For example, the d = 6 instance of
L6028@P94: C(δA, δB, δC) encodes four logical qubits, whilst the d = 10
L6029@P94: instance encodes ten logical qubits. In contrast, the dimension of the surface and toric codes are fixed at one and three
L6030@P94: for all code distances. The trade-off is that nontopological codes have nonlocal stabilizer checks, meaning they
L6031@P94: would have to be implemented on hardware with the ability
L6032@P94: to perform beyond-nearest-neighbor interactions between

L6069@P95: completely local topological codes and random quantum
L6070@P95: LDPC codes, as explored for the 2D setting in Ref. [56].
L6071@P95: To numerically benchmark the single-shot performance
L6072@P95: of the nontopological code family listed in Table III, we
L6073@P95: simulate error correction under the two-stage decoder. The
L6074@P95: strategy we employ is BP+OSD × 2, for which both stage1 and stage-2 decoding use the BP+OSD decoder. The
L6075@P95: MWPM and BP+OSD strategy used for the surface and
L6076@P95: toric codes would not work in this setting, as nontopological codes do not have the looplike metacheck syndromes
L6077@P95: required for MWPM decoding. The simulation results are
L6078@P95: summarized by Fig. 4, which shows a sustained threshold
L6079@P95: for the C(δA, δB, δC) family of nontopological codes in the
L6080@P95: region of 2.7%. This result demonstrates that the BP+OSD
L6081@P95: decoding strategy can be used to decode new 3D product
L6082@P95: codes and achieve performance close to that of established
L6083@P95: codes such as the 3D surface and toric codes.
L6084@P95: VI. CONCLUSION
L6085@P95: In this paper, we investigate single-shot decoding of 3D
L6086@P95: product codes. We gave a formal definition of confinement in quantum codes and proved that all 3D product
L6087@P95: codes have confinement for Z errors. We also prove that
L6088@P95: confinement is sufficient for single-shot error correction
L6089@P95: against adversarial noise. This is a strengthening of the
L6090@P95: result of Campbell [18], who showed that a property called
L6091@P95: soundness is sufficient for single-shot error correction, in
L6092@P95: that soundness implies confinement but the converse is
L6093@P95: not true. Remarkably, there are important classes of codes,
L6094@P95: such as quantum expander codes, which have confinement but not soundness. Further to that, we prove that
L6095@P95: codes with linear confinement, and so expander codes, do
L6096@P95: have a single-shot threshold for local stochastic noise. The
L6097@P95: obvious open problem arising from our work is how to
L6098@P95: extend our findings for linear confinement to the superlinear case. Is confinement, in general, a sufficient condition
L6099@P95: for quantum-LDPC codes to exhibit a single-shot threshold? If not, what other requirements should a code satisfy
L6100@P95: to ensure the existence of a single-shot threshold?
L6101@P95: We simulate single-shot error correction for a variety
L6102@P95: of 3D product codes, concentrating on 3D toric and surface codes. Using MWPM and BP+OSD, we achieve the
L6103@P95: best known code-capacity error threshold and sustainable
L6104@P95: single-shot error threshold for this code family (for phaseflip noise). Our results strongly suggest that the bottleneck
L6105@P95: of two-stage decoders is the first stage where the noisy
L6106@P95: syndrome is repaired. For the 3D toric code, the optimal threshold of the syndrome-repair step is 3.3% [33],
L6107@P95: whereas the optimal threshold of the entire decoding problem is 11.0% [35]. This implies that two-stage decoders
L6108@P95: can never achieve optimal performance in these codes, so
L6109@P95: perhaps other single-shot decoding methods ought to be
L6110@P95: investigated in future.
L6111@P95: We also simulate single-shot error correction for a family of nontopological 3D product codes, using BP+OSD for
L6112@P95: both decoding steps. We achieve performance very close to
L6113@P95: that of the 3D toric and surface codes, which indicates that
L6114@P95: BP+OSD is a high-performance single-shot decoder. Furthermore, the versatility of BP+OSD means that we expect
L6115@P95: it to work as a single-shot decoder for general LDPC 3D
L6116@P95: product codes. We leave confirmation of this to future
L6117@P95: work, and we conjecture that BP+OSD will achieve good
L6118@P95: performance for other classes of quantum-LDPC codes
L6119@P95: such as topological fracton codes [61,62].

L6137@P96: APPENDIX A: LINEAR CONFINEMENT AND
L6138@P96: SINGLE-SHOT THRESHOLD
L6139@P96: We present the stochastic shadow decoder, a variant of
L6140@P96: the (adversarial) shadow decoder described in Definition
L6141@P96: 3, and prove that it succeeds in correcting errors that
L6142@P96: have connected components that are sufficiently sparse and
L6143@P96: of bounded size, both on the syndrome and the qubits
L6144@P96: (Lemma 6). Theorem 2 will then follow from Lemma 6 on
L6145@P96: the performance of the stochastic shadow decoder: a family of codes with good linear confinement has a single-shot
L6146@P96: threshold under the local stochastic noise model.
L6147@P96: This appendix is organized as follows. After fixing
L6148@P96: some graph-theory notation in Appendix A1, we introduce a novel weight function for node sets in a graph,
L6149@P96: the closeness function, Appendix A2. We prove that
L6150@P96: the closeness weight function preserves confinement and
L6151@P96: that the stochastic shadow decoder can be used on confined codes to keep the closeness of error under control
L6152@P96: (Appendix A3). Crucially, the closeness weight function
L6153@P96: characterizes the structure of local stochastic errors better
L6154@P96: that the Hamming weight does, as some classic results in
L6155@P96: percolation theory show. We conclude, in Appendix A4,
L6156@P96: by showing that a family of codes with good linear confinement has a sustainable single-shot threshold (Theorem
L6157@P96: 1). Our proof is built on the results in Refs. [22,65], where
L6158@P96: the authors prove that expander codes (which have linear
L6159@P96: confinement) have a single-shot threshold when decoded
L6160@P96: via the small-set flip decoder.
L6161@P96: 1. Notation and preliminaries
L6162@P96: Given a stabilizer code on n qubits with stabilizer group
L6163@P96: S ⊆ Pn, we associate to it two graphs: (Gq, ∼q), the qubit
L6164@P96: graph, and (Gs, ∼s), the syndrome graph. The set of nodes
L6165@P96: are Gq, the n qubits, and Gs, a generating set of the stabilizer group S [66]. The adjacency relations ∼q and ∼s are
L6166@P96: defined as
L6167@P96: q1 ∼q q2 ⇔∃s ∈ Gs such that {q1, q2}⊆ supp(s),
L6168@P96: s1 ∼s s2 ⇔∃q ∈ Gq such that q ∈ supp(s1) ∩ supp(s2);
L6169@P96: where the support supp(s) of a Pauli operator s in Pn is the
L6170@P96: set of all the qubits on which its action is nontrivial. We
L6171@P96: use lowercase symbols for Pauli operators in Pn and the
L6172@P96: corresponding uppercase symbol to indicate its support,
L6173@P96: e.g., E := supp(e). We use the term error to refer interchangeably to a Pauli operator or its support, in particular
L6174@P96: given two Pauli operators e1 and e2 we use the symbol +
L6175@P96: to indicate the support of the product operator e1 · e2 [67],
L6176@P96: so that
L6177@P96: E1 + E2 = supp(e1 · e2).
L6178@P96: In this picture, the syndrome σ(·) maps the set of Pauli
L6179@P96: operators on n qubits Pn into the power set of Gs,
L6180@P96: σ : Pn −→ P(Gs)
L6181@P96: e −→ {s ∈ Gs : se =−es}.
L6182@P96: We define the neighbor map  as
L6183@P96:  : Pn −→ P(Gs)
L6184@P96: e −→ {s ∈ Gs : supp(s) ∩ E = ∅}.
L6185@P96: With slight abuse of terminology, we call syndrome any
L6186@P96: element of P(Gs), even when such a set does not belong
L6187@P96: to the image of σ. When referring to an error as a set E,
L6188@P96: it is always to be intended as corresponding to a fixed
L6189@P96: Pauli operator e ∈ Pn such that E := supp(e). We write
L6190@P96: interchangeably σ(e)/σ (E) and (e)/ (E) to indicate
L6191@P96: the image, via the syndrome map and the neighbor map,
L6192@P96: respectively, of the Pauli error e.
L6193@P96: Given two syndromes sets in Gs we use the symbol + to
L6194@P96: indicate their symmetric difference. It is easy to verify that
L6195@P96: the map σ(·) preserves the + operation (i.e., it is linear):
L6196@P96: σ(e1 · e2) = σ(E1 + E2) = σ(E1) + σ(E2).
L6197@P96: Moreover, the image via σ of disjoint nonconnected sets
L6198@P96: is disjoint. In fact, if E1, E2 are two disjoint nonconnected
L6199@P96: sets in Gq and we suppose that their syndrome sets are not
L6200@P96: disjoint we find a contradiction. Let sˆ be a stabilizer in
L6201@P96: σ(E1) ∩ σ(E2). By definition of σ, this entails that e1 and
L6202@P96: e2 both anticommute with sˆ, which is equivalent to saying that their supports have odd overlap with supp(sˆ). In
L6203@P96: particular, there exists qi ∈ Ei such that qi ∈ supp(sˆ) and,
L6204@P96: by the definition of the adjacency relation ∼q, q1 ∈ E1 and
L6205@P96: q2 ∈ E2 would be connected via sˆ, against the assumption.
L6206@P96: Note that, in general the image via the syndrome map σ(·)
L6207@P96: of a connected set needs not to be connected. However, the
L6208@P96: neighbor function (·) maps connected sets into connected
L6209@P96: sets. We make use of these properties in Appendix A3.
L6210@P96: 2. The closeness weight function
L6211@P96: When errors are local stochastic it can be handy to use
L6212@P96: definitions of weight other than the cardinality and Hamming weight. For instance, the authors in Ref. [22] define
L6213@P96: the quantities of Definition 6 and study a related notion of
L6214@P96: percolation to understand the tolerance to errors of a given
L6215@P96: connected graph.
L6216@P96: Definition 6 (α subsets, MaxConnα(E) [22]). An α subset
L6217@P96: of a set E ⊆ Gq is a set K such that |K ∩ E|≥ α|K|. The
L6218@P96: maximum size of a connected α subset of E is denoted by
L6219@P96: MaxConnα(E).
L6220@P96-97: 020340-15QUINTAVALLE, VASMER, ROFFE, and CAMPBELL PRX QUANTUM 2, 020340 (2021)
L6221@P97: We here introduce a conceptual cousin to MaxConnα(E),
L6222@P97: the β closeness of an error set E, and prove that it is a welldefined weight function (see Lemma 3). We do not explicitly detail the relations between α subsets and closeness
L6223@P97: here. However, we implicitly use them, as our percolation
L6224@P97: results and ultimately the proof of Theorem 1 heavily rely
L6225@P97: on those relations and the proofs in Refs. [22,65].
L6226@P97: Definition 7 (β closeness). Let G be a connected graph,
L6227@P97: i.e., a graph in which there exist a path between any two of
L6228@P97: its nodes. Given a subset E of nodes and a positive integer
L6229@P97: β, we define its β closeness as the quantity:
L6230@P97: Eβ := max{|K ∩ E| : K is connected, |K|= β}.
L6231@P97: We call any connected subset of β nodes a β patch and any
L6232@P97: β patch K such that |K ∩ E|=Eβ maximal patch for E.
L6233@P97: Since we are interested in the β closeness of error sets
L6234@P97: on a qubit graph Gq, it is natural to introduce the notion of
L6235@P97: reduced β closeness.
L6236@P97: Definition 8. Given a qubit error set E ⊆ Gq, its reduced
L6237@P97: β closeness Ered
L6238@P97: β is defined as
L6239@P97: Ered
L6240@P97: β := min{E + Tβ : σ(E + T) = σ(E),
L6241@P97: T = supp(t) for some t ∈ Pn}.
L6242@P97: Crucially, we see in Lemma 5 that the closeness function
L6243@P97: preserves confinement. As a consequence, we can build a
L6244@P97: variant of the shadow decoder (Definition 9) that succeeds
L6245@P97: in correcting errors of small reduced closeness.
L6246@P97: We now prove some basic properties of the β-closeness
L6247@P97: weight function ·β on a connected graph G.
L6248@P97: Lemma 3. Let G be a connected graph and denote by |G|
L6249@P97: the number of its nodes. For any positive integer β< |G|,
L6250@P97: the following hold:
L6251@P97: (i) ·β ≤|·|;
L6252@P97: (ii) ·β ≤ β; the equality holds if and only if the considered set of nodes has a connected component of
L6253@P97: size at least β; conversely, if ·β <β then the
L6254@P97: connected components of the set all have size less
L6255@P97: than β;
L6256@P97: (iii) it is positive:Eβ ≥ 0 and equality holds if and
L6257@P97: only if E =∅;
L6258@P97: (iv) it satisfies the triangle inequality: for any E1, E2,
L6259@P97: E1 ∪ E2β ≤E1β +E2β.
L6260@P97: (v) it is monotonic: if E1 ⊆ E2 then E1β ≤E2β;
L6261@P97: In the following, let K ⊆ G be a maximal β patch for E,
L6262@P97: i.e., Eβ =|K ∩ E|.
L6263@P97: (i) Eβ =|K ∩ E|≤|E|.
L6264@P97: (ii) |K ∩ E|≤|K|= β. Equality holds if and only if
L6265@P97: K ∩ E = K ⊆ E, which entails that E has a connected component of size at least β, since K is
L6266@P97: connected.
L6267@P97: (iii) If E is nonempty then there exists at least one node
L6268@P97: g ∈ E. Since G is connected, for any integer 1 ≤
L6269@P97: β ≤|G| there exists a β patch that contains g so that
L6270@P97: Eβ ≥ 1.
L6271@P97: (iv) Let J be any β patch in G. The following hold:
L6272@P97: |J ∩ (E1 ∪ E2)|=|(J ∩ E1)|+|(J ∩ E2)|+
L6273@P97: −|J ∩ (E1 ∩ E2)|
L6274@P97: ≤|J ∩ E1|+|J ∩ E2|
L6275@P97: ≤E1β +E2β.
L6276@P97: Since this holds for any β patch, we obtain
L6277@P97: E1 ∪ E2β ≤E1β +E2β.
L6278@P97: (v) Let K1, K2 be maximal β patches for E1 and E2,
L6279@P97: respectively. Then
L6280@P97: |K1 ∩ E1|≤|K1 ∩ E2| because E1 ⊆ E2,
L6281@P97: ≤|K2 ∩ E2| by maximality of K2,
L6282@P97: which yields E1β ≤E2β.
L6283@P97: Lemma 2 below states that there exists a canonical form
L6284@P97: for maximal β patches of an error set E. Roughly speaking, a canonical β patch K will be made up of some entire
L6285@P97: connected components of E, plus at most one connected
L6286@P97: proper subset of a connected component of E, and some
L6287@P97: other nodes not in E (see Fig. 5). The existence of a canonical β patch is key in proving that the closeness function
L6288@P97: preserves confinement in the sense explained by Lemma 5.
L6289@P97: Lemma 4 (Canonical β patch). For any error E on a qubit
L6290@P97: graph G there exists a maximal β patch T such that, for all
L6291@P97: but one connected component Ei of E, the following holds:
L6292@P97: either Ei ⊆ T or Ei ∩ T =∅.
L6293@P97: In other words, if E1, ... , Em are the connected components of E, reordering if necessary, there exists an index
L6294@P97: ν such that
L6295@P97: |T ∩ Ei|=|Ei| if i <ν,
L6296@P97: |T ∩ Ei|≤|Ei| if i = ν,
L6297@P97: |T ∩ Ei|= 0 if i >ν. (A1)
L6298@P97: We call any such T a canonical β patch for the set E.
L6299@P97: Let J be any maximal β patch for E, i.e., J is connected,
L6300@P97: has size β and |J ∩ E|=Eβ. Starting from J we build a

L6317@P98: qubit patch. In this way we have the certainty to include in the green and syndrome patch a sufficient number of crosses and syndrome

L6379@P99: that are incident to J	. The updated metatree T derived
L6380@P99: from the updated set T has ν − 1 metanodes. By the induction hypothesis, it can be modified to obtain the desired
L6381@P99: form.
L6382@P99: In other words, we pick a metaleaf of T and we either
L6383@P99: remove part of its nodes [case (a)] or all of them [case
L6384@P99: (b)]. We preserve the quantity |T ∩ E| by adding new error
L6385@P99: nodes to some different error-patch component that overlaps with an incomplete component of the error E. By
L6386@P99: choosing a leaf, we are able to preserve the connectivity
L6387@P99: of T and thus the connectivity of the corresponding node
L6388@P99: sets T.
L6389@P99: We iterate this procedure over metaleaves of T until the
L6390@P99: overlap of the corresponding set T in G and the error set E
L6391@P99: has the desired form.
L6392@P99: b. ENLARGEMENT
L6393@P99: By contradiction, we prove that it is possible to add
L6394@P99: nodes to the set T corresponding to the balanced metatree
L6395@P99: T so that it is connected, it has size exactly β and |T ∩
L6396@P99: E|=Eβ. First note that during the balancing procedure,
L6397@P99: the number |T ∩ E| remains constant and the following
L6398@P99: holds:
L6399@P99: |T ∩ E|= ν
L6400@P99: i=1
L6401@P99: |Ji|=|J ∩ E|=Eβ.
L6402@P99: Moreover, the initial tree is connected and the balancing
L6403@P99: procedure preserves connectivity. However, we only have
L6404@P99: an upper bound on the size of T. In fact, if T is the initial metatree and T is its corresponding subgraph in G, it
L6405@P99: holds T ⊆ J and therefore |T|≤ β. During the balancing
L6406@P99: step the size of T could decrease when we remove nodes
L6407@P99: of eij , belonging to a metaedge εij . Thus, in general, after
L6408@P99: the balancing step for the weight of T holds:
L6409@P99: |T|≤ β.
L6410@P99: If |T|= β, then T is a β patch with maximum overlap
L6411@P99: with E and, by balancing, it is canonical. If |T| <β, then
L6412@P99: there must exist at least β −Eβ nodes in G \ (E ∪ T)
L6413@P99: that are connected to T. In fact, a connected proper subset can always be enlarged in a connected graph. If the
L6414@P99: only way to enlarge T to a β patch were by adding nodes
L6415@P99: in E, then we would have found a β patch whose overlap
L6416@P99: with E has size greater than its β closeness, which contradicts the definition of Eβ. In conclusion, any of such
L6417@P99: enlargements of the tree T is a canonical β patch for E.
L6418@P99: 3. Confinement and stochastic shadow decoder
L6419@P99: Here, we first prove that the closeness function preserves confinement, as Lemma 5 states. Then, we present
L6420@P99: the stochastic shadow decoder (Definition 9) and prove,
L6421@P99: in Lemma 6, that it succeeds in correcting errors of small
L6422@P99: enough closeness. These findings, together with the percolation results of Appendix A4, will yield the proof of the
L6423@P99: existence of a single-shot threshold for codes with linear
L6424@P99: confinement.

L6578@P101: Lemma 5 in particular entails that the closeness weight
L6579@P101: is in fact a sensible quantity to look at when dealing with
L6580@P101: errors on confined codes.
L6581@P101: We now introduce the stochastic shadow decoder. The
L6582@P101: difference between this variant and the one previously
L6583@P101: presented (Definition 3) is on the weight functions used.
L6584@P101: While the standard and adversarial shadow decoder tries
L6585@P101: to minimize the Hamming weight of the residual error, the
L6586@P101: stochastic shadow decoder attempts to keep under control
L6587@P101: its closeness.
L6588@P101: Definition 9 (Stochastic shadow decoder). The stochastic
L6589@P101: shadow decoder has variable parameters 0 <α ≤ 1, and
L6590@P101: 0 <β, γ ∈ Z. Given an observed syndrome S = σ(E) +
L6591@P101: Se where Se ⊆ Gs is the syndrome error, the stochastic shadow decoder of parameters (α, β, γ) performs the
L6592@P101: following two steps:
L6593@P101: 1. Syndrome repair: find Sr of minimum γ closeness
L6594@P101: Srγ such that S + Sr belongs to the (α, β) shadow
L6595@P101: of the code, where
L6596@P101: (α, β) shadow ={σ(E) such that Eβ ≤ αβ}.
L6597@P101: 2. Qubit decode: find Er of minimum β closeness
L6598@P101: Erβ such that σ(Er) = S + Sr.
L6599@P101: We call R = E + Er the residual error.
L6600@P101: We have the following promise on the stochastic shadow
L6601@P101: decoder, which mirrors the results of Lemma 1 for the
L6602@P101: adversarial shadow decoder.
L6603@P101: Lemma 6. Consider a stabilizer code that has (t, f ) confinement and qubit degree ≤ ω − 1. Provided that the
L6604@P101: original error pattern E has Ered
L6605@P101: t ≤ t/2, on input of
L6606@P101: the observed syndrome S = σ(E) + Se, the residual error
L6607@P101: R left by the stochastic shadow decoder of parameter
L6608@P101: [(1/2), t, ωt] satisfies
L6609@P101: Rred
L6610@P101: t ≤ f (2Seωt). (A9)
L6611@P101: Thanks to Lemma 5, we know that the closeness function preserves confinement. The proof is then a straightforward adaption of the proof of Lemma 1, where the
L6612@P101: Hamming weight has to be substituted with ·t on error
L6613@P101: sets and ·ωt on syndrome sets, respectively. We here
L6614@P101: briefly report the proof for completeness.
L6615@P101: Assume Ered
L6616@P101: t ≤ t/2, and let Er be the output of the
L6617@P101: qubit decode step. By construction, it has minimum t
L6618@P101: closeness among the errors with syndrome S + Sr, which
L6619@P101: belongs to the [(1/2), t] shadow of the code. In particular,
L6620@P101: Ert ≤ (t/2). We recall that the + operation between two
L6621@P101: error sets in Gq denotes the support of the product of the
L6622@P101: two corresponding Pauli operators and, as such, it holds
L6623@P101: that (see Appendix A1)
L6624@P101: E + Er ⊆ E ∪ Er.
L6625@P101: By the property of the closeness weight function, this
L6626@P101: entails
L6627@P101: E + Ert ≤E ∪ Ert ≤E+Ert.
L6628@P101: The linearity of the syndrome function σ(·) yields
L6629@P101: σ(E + Er) = σ(E) + σ(Er) = Se + Sr.
L6630@P101: Since Se is a possible solution of the syndrome-repair step
L6631@P101: Srωt ≤Seωt and so,
L6632@P101: Se + Srωt ≤Seωt +Srωt
L6633@P101: ≤ 2Seωt.
L6634@P101: Combining this and the monotonicity of f gives
L6635@P101: E + Erred
L6636@P101: t ≤ f (2Seωt).
L6637@P101: Lemma 5 tells us that the stochastic shadow decoder succeeds whenever the t closeness of the error is small enough.
L6638@P101: Importantly then, if we are able to bound the probability
L6639@P101: of the complement of this event, we could infer an upper
L6640@P101: bound on the failure probability of our decoder. This is the
L6641@P101: subject of the next section.
L6642@P101: 4. Percolation results and proof of Theorem 2
L6643@P101: We consider error sets E on the qubit graph Gq and error
L6644@P101: sets Se on the syndrome graph Gs and we assume that
L6645@P101: the probability of observing a particular error is at most
L6646@P101: exponential in its size. Formally, we use this error model.
L6647@P101: Definition 10 (Local stochastic error). An error set E on a
L6648@P101: graph G is local stochastic of parameter p if, for all set of
L6649@P101: nodes G ⊆ G, the following holds:
L6650@P101: P(G ⊆ E) ≤ p|G|.
L6651@P101: We then use some results in percolation theory, Lemmas
L6652@P101: 7 and 8 below, to understand the probability that errors of
L6653@P101: closeness linear in the patch size (i.e., Eβ = αβ for some
L6654@P101: 0 <α ≤ 1) occur when the noise is local stochastic.
L6655@P101-102: 020340-20SINGLE-SHOT ERROR CORRECTION OF THREE-DIMENSIONAL. . . PRX QUANTUM 2, 020340 (2021)

L6659@P102: Nβ ≤|G|β,

L6833@P103: In other words, for local stochastic noise of intensity
L6834@P103: p ≤ pth on the qubits and q ≤ qth on the syndrome, the
L6835@P103: stochastic shadow decoder has a sustainable single-shot
L6836@P103: threshold.
L6837@P103: We conclude by noting that the assumption of linear
L6838@P103: confinement is key in the proof of Theorem 2. However,
L6839@P103: we speculate that the limitations of Theorem 2 are an artefact of our proof and superlinear confinement is a sufficient
L6840@P103: condition for a family of codes to exhibit a single-shot
L6841@P103: threshold. In fact, the existence of a threshold pth and qth
L6842@P103: relies on the bounds given in Lemma 8. There, it is fundamental that the relation between the chosen size of the
L6843@P103: patch and the size of the overlap with the error is linear [see Eqs. (A11) and (A12)]. In other words, Lemma
L6844@P103: 8 states that, if we take β patches on the error graph and γ
L6845@P103: patches on the syndrome graph, we are able to estimate the
L6846@P103: probability that errors have closeness less than αβ and αγ˜ ,
L6847@P103: respectively. By Eq. (A13), in order to bound the closeness
L6848@P103: of the residual error left by the stochastic shadow decoder,
L6849@P103: we need
L6850@P103: Seγ ≤
L6851@P103: 1
L6852@P103: 2
L6853@P103: f −1
L6854@P103: (αβ).
L6855@P103: As a consequence, combining this with the requirements
L6856@P103: of Lemma 8, entails
L6857@P103: γ = κ
L6858@P103: 1
L6859@P103: 2
L6860@P103: f −1
L6861@P103: (αβ),
L6862@P103: for some positive constant κ. In conclusion, building up
L6863@P103: on the results of Lemma 8, we either need to prove that
L6864@P103: confinement is preserved if we take on the syndrome graph
L6865@P103: patches of size linear in f −1(αβ) or, using our Lemma 5
L6866@P103: without modification, that the function is itself linear.
L6867@P103: APPENDIX B: QUBIT PLACEMENT ON A 3D

L7162@P105: (possibly incomplete) square face. These points correspond to X stabilizers, which we therefore identify with
L7163@P105: faces. Given a triplet corresponding to one of such a
L7164@P105: point, the associated X stabilizer has support contained
L7165@P105: in the set of edges, which are parallel to the edges of
L7166@P105: the square, forming a cross in a plane. X stabilizers,
L7167@P105: like qubits, are of three different types, being in one-toone correspondence with basis elements of C2. Namely,
L7168@P105: each X stabilizer in C2 has support in two out of three
L7169@P105: types of qubits: transverse-vertical, transverse-horizontal,
L7170@P105: or vertical-horizontal (see Table VI and Fig. 7).
L7171@P105: Points with integer coordinates are associated to Z stabilizers; these are points where endpoints of edges intersect.
L7172@P105: FIG. 8. Z stabilizers on the lattice described in Fig. 6. (a) Z stabilizer corresponding to the vertex indexed by (i, j , k) = (2, 4, 2);
L7173@P105: its support is contained in the cross of qubits highlighted as red
L7174@P105: edges in the picture. The crossing has coordinates (z, y, x) =
L7175@P105: (2, 4, 2) (red circle). (b) Z stabilizer corresponding to the vertex indexed by (i, j , k) = (3, 6, 2); its support is contained in
L7176@P105: the cross of qubits highlighted as red edges in the picture. The
L7177@P105: crossing has coordinates (z, y, x) = (3, 6, 2) (red circle).
L7178@P105: The Z stabilizer corresponding to (i, j , k) has support on a
L7179@P105: 3D cross of edges and qubits centered in (z, y, x) = (i, j , k)
L7180@P105: (see Table VI and Fig. 8).
L7181@P105: Points with half-integer coordinates sit in the center of
L7182@P105: a cube. To any such cube is associated a metacheck in C3.
L7183@P105: Metachecks have support on a 3D cross of faces and X stabilizers parallel to the faces of the cube they are associated
L7184@P105: to (see Table VI).
L7185@P105: 1. On geometric locality
L7186@P105: One interesting feature of the embedding of 3D product
L7187@P105: codes on a cubic lattice is that it preserves some locality
L7188@P105: properties of the seed matrices δA, δB, and δC. Thus, if we
L7189@P105: were able to place qubits on a 3D cubic lattice we could
L7190@P105: use the 3D homological product to build LDPC codes with
L7191@P105: nearest-neighbor interactions.
L7192@P105: Let δ be an m × n matrix with row and column indices
L7193@P105: α ∈{1, ... , m} and i ∈{1, ... , n}, respectively, and let ν =
L7194@P105: max{m, n}. We say that δ is geometrically ρ local on a
L7195@P105: TABLE VI. Correspondence between operators of the chain complex (C
L7196@P105: 
L7197@P105: 
L7198@P105: ), their type as geometric objects on the lattice, and their
L7199@P105: support. Note that the support of X and Z stabilizers is a set of qubits and edges while the support of metachecks is a set of X stabilizers
L7200@P105: and faces.

L7205@P105: (α, j , γ ) Horizontal qubits: (α∗, j , γ )
L7206@P105: Vertical-horizontal square Vertical qubits: (i, β, γ ∗)

L7209@P105: ∗, j , k)
L7210@P105: Vertical qubits: (i, j ∗, k)

L7217@P106: α∗ ⊆ Uρ, ν (α) and i
L7218@P106: ∗ ⊆ Uρ, ν (i), (B3)
L7219@P106: where Uρ, ν (ζ ) is any set of ρ consecutive integers modulo ν, which contains ζ . In particular, we require the αth
L7220@P106: rows to have support on columns with index that is close to
L7221@P106: the integer α, and similar for columns. The reason for this
L7222@P106: choice will be clear when we prove Proposition 1. Briefly,
L7223@P106: conditions (B3) say that δ is geometrically ρ local on a
L7224@P106: torus if the following hold: (1) any of its rows has support
L7225@P106: on a bounded box of ρ columns, and the box for row α + 1
L7226@P106: is a right shift of the box for row α; (2) any of its columns
L7227@P106: has support on a bounded box of ρ rows, and the box for
L7228@P106: column i + 1 is a downward shift of the box for column i.
L7229@P106: In particular, if we associate row and column indices with
L7230@P106: integer points on a circle of ν points:
L7231@P106: 1
L7232@P106: 2
L7233@P106: ν − 1 3
L7234@P106: ν
L7235@P106: locality means that any set α∗/i

L7237@P106: interval on the circle such that (i) it has length at most ρ and
L7238@P106: (ii) it contains the point α/i. For instance, the degenerate

L7256@P106: A closely related notion of locality on a torus is geometric locality in Euclidean space. We say that an m × n
L7257@P106: matrix is geometrically ρ local in Euclidean space if for
L7258@P106: any row and column index

L7312@P106: In general, geometric locality is a relaxation of the locality property of the repetition code, which only allows for
L7313@P106: interactions between pairs of nearest bits. Importantly, as
L7314@P106: Proposition 1 states, it is preserved by the 3D product
L7315@P106: construction. For this reason, geometrically local classical
L7316@P106: codes, combined with the 3D product construction, could
L7317@P106: be good candidates in the quest to quantum local codes
L7318@P106: beyond the toric and the surface codes.
L7319@P106: The remainder of this appendix is organized as follows. We first state Proposition 1 and prove that geometric
L7320@P106: locality is preserved by the 3D product construction. We
L7321@P106: conclude by observing how this proof provides an explicit
L7322@P106: identification of the 3D toric and surface codes as 3D
L7323@P106: product codes.
L7324@P106: To ease the notation, in the following we shortly refer
L7325@P106: to codes as geometrically local, dropping the specification
L7326@P106: on a torus or in Euclidean space. When considering qubits
L7327@P106: on a cubic lattice, the lattice would be on a torus or in
L7328@P106: Euclidean space depending on the definition of locality that
L7329@P106: applies to the seed matrices.
L7330@P106: Proposition 1. Consider the 3D product code obtained
L7331@P106: from three seed matrices geometrically ρ local. If its qubits
L7332@P106: are displayed on the edges of a cubic lattice as detailed
L7333@P106: in Appendix B, then it is geometrically ρ local in the
L7334@P106: following sense:
L7335@P106: 1. any X -stabilizer generator has weight at most 2ρ
L7336@P106: with support contained in a 2D box of size ρ × ρ;
L7337@P106: 2. any Z-stabilizer generator has weight at most
L7338@P106: 3ρ with support contained in a 3D box of size
L7339@P106: ρ × ρ × ρ.
L7340@P106-107: 020340-25QUINTAVALLE, VASMER, ROFFE, and CAMPBELL PRX QUANTUM 2, 020340 (2021)
L7341@P107: We prove the condition on the Z stabilizers, the proof
L7342@P107: for the X stabilizer being similar.

L7402@P107: t ={(¯i, j , k) : ¯i ∈ Uρ(i)},

L7406@P107: sets of coordinates intersect on the point (z, y, x) = (i, j , k).
L7407@P107: Moreover, all three intervals t, v, h have length at
L7408@P107: most ρ. Combining these, we find that the support of Sz
L7409@P107: indexed by (i, j , k) is contained in in a ρ × ρ × ρ neighborhood of the point (z, y, x) = (i, j , k) and has cardinality
L7410@P107: at most 3ρ. In other words, we show that the support of Sz
L7411@P107: is contained on a 3D cross of qubits with arms of length at
L7412@P107: most ρ.
L7413@P107: As previously said, the 3D toric and planar codes are
L7414@P107: particular instances of the 3D product construction. Furthermore, it is well known that they are local on a torus
L7415@P107: and in the Euclidean space, respectively. To see how this
L7416@P107: is the case, we remind the reader that the 3D toric code
L7417@P107: is obtained by choosing as seed matrices the degenerate
L7418@P107: parity-check matrix of the repetition code in the standard
L7419@P107: basis. For matrix size L × L, it holds that
L7420@P107: {1, ... , L} ←→ {1, ... , L}
L7421@P107: i −→ {i, i + 1 mod L}
L7422@P107: {α, α + 1 mod L} ←− α.
L7423@P107: Therefore, stabilizers have support on pairs of consecutive
L7424@P107: edges, and it is straightforward to see that they have the
L7425@P107: usual shape:
L7426@P107: 1. Z stabilizers have support on edges incident to a
L7427@P107: vertex;
L7428@P107: 2. X stabilizers have support on edges on the boundary
L7429@P107: of a square face;
L7430@P107: 3. metachecks have support on the faces of a cube.
L7431@P107: A similar argument holds for the 3D surface code, which
L7432@P107: is local in Euclidean space.

L7583@P108: Loosely, Corollary 1 states that the result of Lemma 9

L7926@P111: follows a surface area-perimeter law.
L7927@P111: APPENDIX D: ON SOME PROPERTIES OF
L7928@P111: EXAPANDER CODES
L7929@P111: Here we prove that the family of expander codes considered in Ref. [20] has the three properties stated in the main
L7930@P111: text, namely,
L7931@P111: (i) they have full-rank parity-check matrices;
L7932@P111: (ii) they have (t,3x) confinement with t ∈ (d);
L7933@P111: (iii) for every small error |e|≤ 3, σ(e)> 1.
L7934@P111: Property (i) is true by assumption made by the authors in
L7935@P111: Ref. [20]. In order to prove property (ii) we use Corollary 9
L7936@P111: of Ref. [20], which states that the code family considered
L7937@P111: has robustness. Robustness for a code is very similar to
L7938@P111: confinement but uses a slightly different notion of reduced
L7939@P111: weight, which, for an operator e, is defined as
L7940@P111: |e|
L7941@P111: red
L7942@P111: S := min{|e + s| : s is a stabilizer}.
L7943@P111: Our definition of reduced weight instead minimizes over
L7944@P111: all Pauli operators with the same syndrome and therefore
L7945@P111: it considers both stabilizers and logical operators. Because
L7946@P111: for the reduced weight we minimize over a bigger set, the
L7947@P111: following holds:
L7948@P111: |e|
L7949@P111: red
L7950@P111: S ≥|e|
L7951@P111: red. (D1)
L7952@P111: Confinement follows combining the statement of Corollary
L7953@P111: 9 in Ref. [20] for errors such that |e|
L7954@P111: red
L7955@P111: S < d and Eq. (D1):
L7956@P111: 3|σ(e)|≥|e|
L7957@P111: red.
L7958@P111: In order to prove property (iii) we need to make use of
L7959@P111: the hypergraph product structure of the expander codes
L7960@P111: in Ref. [20]. Briefly, the code family is defined by taking
L7961@P111: the two-product of the length-1 chain complex δ : C0 −→
L7962@P111: C1 with itself, where δ is an expander matrix (see also
L7963@P111: Ref. [70]). More precisely, the expander codes in Ref. [20]
L7964@P111: are CSS codes defined on the chain complex:
L7965@P111: C0 ⊗ C1
L7966@P111: C0 ⊗ C0 C1 ⊗ C1
L7967@P111: C1 ⊗ C0
L7968@P111: E2
L7969@P111: E1
L7970@P111: E0
L7971@P111: HX
L7972@P111: HZ
L7973@P111: where
L7974@P111: HZ = 
L7975@P111: δ⊗11⊗δT
L7976@P111: ,
L7977@P111: HX = 
L7978@P111: 1⊗δδT⊗1
L7979@P111: 
L7980@P111: .
L7981@P111: The matrix δ is chosen in a family of LDPC expander
L7982@P111: matrices with full rank and constant column and row
L7983@P111: weight wc and wr bigger than two.
L7984@P111: We prove property (iii) for X errors e and the syndrome
L7985@P111: map σ(e) = HZe¯, where e¯ is the binary vector representation of the Pauli operator e; the proof for Z errors and
L7986@P111: syndrome map HX follows by duality with minor changes.
L7987@P111: Let e be a weight-1 X operator and e¯ its representation as a unit vector. Then σ(e) = HZe¯ is a column of the
L7988@P111: matrix HZ, namely column j if e¯ has j th coordinate equal
L7989@P111: to 1. Since the seed matrix δ has constant column and row
L7990@P111: degree bigger than 2, so has the matrix HZ and therefore
L7991@P111: |σ(e)|≥ 2.
L7992@P111: Consider now a weight-2 error operator e. By reshaping
L7993@P111: of vectors into matrices we can write e as
L7994@P111: (L, R)
L7995@P111: for some binary matrices L of size n × n, R of size m ×
L7996@P111: m, and such that |L|+|R|= 2, where δ has size m × n.
L7997@P111: Following this notation, the syndrome S of E can be
L7998@P111: written as
L7999@P111: S = δL + Rδ.
L8000@P111: We have three cases to be distinguished.
L8001@P111: (a) |L|= 2. If the support of L is not contained in one
L8002@P111: column, i.e., Li1,j1 = Li2,j2 = 1 and j1 = j2, then for
L8003@P111: the syndrome S = δL the following holds:
L8004@P111: Sj1 = δi1 and Sj2 = δi2 ,
L8005@P111: i.e., the syndrome matrix S has at least two nonzero
L8006@P111: columns and therefore weight at least 2. If instead
L8007@P111: the support of L is contained in one column, Li1,j =
L8008@P111-112: 020340-30SINGLE-SHOT ERROR CORRECTION OF THREE-DIMENSIONAL. . . PRX QUANTUM 2, 020340 (2021)
L8009@P112: Li2,j = 0 then the syndrome S is zero but for the j th
L8010@P112: column:
L8011@P112: Sj = δi1 + δi2 .
L8012@P112: In this case, whenever δ has distance at least 3,
L8013@P112: because it has constant column weight wc, the
L8014@P112: following holds:
L8015@P112: |δi1 + δi2 |=|δi1 |+|δi2 |− 2|δi1 ∧ δi2 |
L8016@P112: ≥ 2wc − 2(wc − 1)
L8017@P112: ≥ 2.
L8018@P112: Where the second to last inequality holds because
L8019@P112: if δ defines code of distance bigger than 3, then it
L8020@P112: must have all distinct columns and different vectors of constant weight wc overlap in at most wc − 1
L8021@P112: positions.
L8022@P112: (b) |L|=|R|= 1. Suppose Li1,j1 = Ri2,j2 = 1. The syndrome S has support contained in one column and
L8023@P112: one row, in the shape of a cross as follows:
L8024@P112: Sk,j1 = δk,i1, k = i2
L8025@P112: Si2,k = δj2,k, k = j1
L8026@P112: Si2,j1 = δi2,i1 + δj2,j1 ,
L8027@P112: Sij = 0, otherwise.
L8028@P112: It then follows for the weight of the syndrome S that
L8029@P112: |S|≥ wc + wr − 1,
L8030@P112: which is bigger that 1 by assumption on the column
L8031@P112: and row weight of δ.
L8032@P112: (c) The case |R|= 2 can be proven as done in (a) for
L8033@P112: |L|= 2 by exchanging the role of columns and rows.
L8034@P112: To sum up, whenever |e|= 2, |σ(e)| > 2.
L8035@P112: Last, consider a weight-3 error e. As done for weight-2
L8036@P112: errors, we can write e as (L, R) for some binary matrices
L8037@P112: L and R. Again, we need to distinguish between the possible weight combinations of |L|+|R|= 3. We now prove

L8122@P113: the case for |L|= 3 and support of L contained in one column. The other cases are either a dual argument of this one
L8123@P113: (i.e., for |R|= 3 supported on one row) or follows easily
L8124@P113: adapting the proof for |e|= 2.
L8125@P113: Let e be a weight-3 error operator with reshaping (L, R)
L8126@P113: such that |L|= 3 and Li1,j = Li2,j = Li3,j = 1, for some
L8127@P113: column index j . In this case, the syndrome matrix S has
L8128@P113: support contained in its j th column:
L8129@P113: Sj = δi1 + δi2 + δi3 ,
L8130@P113: and therefore,
L8131@P113: |σ(e)|=|S|=|Sj
L8132@P113: |=|δi1 + δi2 + δi3 |.
L8133@P113: In order to prove |σ(e)|=|Sj | > 2, we need to use the
L8134@P113: expansion properties of δ and more specifically Lemma 3
L8135@P113: of Ref. [20], (see also Ref. [70]). We first introduce some
L8136@P113: notation. We refer to the rows of δ as checks and to
L8137@P113: its columns as bits; we say that a bit j is in the support of the check i if and only if δij = 1. Given a set of
L8138@P113: bits B ⊆{1, ... , n} we say that the check i ∈{1, ... , m}

L8193@P113: odd-even effect so we include only the data for odd L. We extract the gradients g(L) from the corresponding straight line fits in (a),(b)
L8194@P113: (gray lines), and plot the logarithms of these values against log L [(c),(d)]. The data fit well to the linear ansatz given in Eq. (E5), which
L8195@P113: allows us to estimate the parameters α and β, which control the suppression of the logical error rate as per Eq. (E2). For code-capacity
L8196@P113: noise, we estimate α = 0.546(33) and β = 1.91(3), and for eight rounds of single-shot error correction, we estimate α = 0.610(37) and

L8205@P114: is a unique neighbor of B if and only if one and only
L8206@P114: one bit in B belongs to the support of the check i. We
L8207@P114: indicate with u(B) the set of unique neighbors of B.
L8208@P114: Lemma 3 in Ref. [20] states that, for the considered class
L8209@P114: of matrices δ:
L8210@P114: |u(B)|≥
L8211@P114: 2
L8212@P114: 3
L8213@P114: wc|B|.
L8214@P114: Combining this with the observation that |u({i1, i2, i3})| is
L8215@P114: a lower bound on |Sj| and plugging in |B|= 3, we find
L8216@P114: |Sj
L8217@P114: |≥|u({i1, i2, i3})|≥ 2wc.
L8218@P114: To sum up, whenever an error (L, R) of weight 3 has
L8219@P114: support on either one column of L or one row of R, by
L8220@P114: expansion its syndrome has weight strictly bigger than 1.
L8221@P114: When instead a weight-3 error has support spread among
L8222@P114: more than one column and row it is enough to use the
L8223@P114: hypergraph product structure of the code family, as done
L8224@P114: for weight-2 errors, to find that its syndrome need to have
L8225@P114: weight at least 2.
L8226@P114: APPENDIX E: FITTING DETAILS
L8227@P114: To obtain our threshold estimates, we use the standard
L8228@P114: critical exponent method [59]. Specifically, in the vicinity
L8229@P114: of the threshold, we fit our data to the following ansatz:
L8230@P114: a0 + a1x + a2x2
L8231@P114: , (E1)
L8232@P114: where the rescaled variable x = (p − pth)L1/μ. Examples
L8233@P114: of this fit are shown in Fig. 9.
L8234@P114: We use the fitting method described in Ref. [12] to
L8235@P114: understand the behavior of the 3D toric code logical error
L8236@P114: rate for error rates p significantly below threshold. Recall
L8237@P114: from Sec. V that we use the following ansatz:
L8238@P114: pfail(L) ∝ (p/pth)
L8239@P114: αLβ
L8240@P114: , (E2)
L8241@P114: we take the logarithm of both sides to obtain
L8242@P114: log pfail = log f (L) + αLβ log(p/pth). (E3)
L8243@P114: For different values of L, we plot log pfail as a function of
L8244@P114: log(p/pth) and fit to a straight line to obtain gradients
L8245@P114: g(L) = ∂ log pfail
L8246@P114: ∂u = αLβ, (E4)
L8247@P114: where u = log(p/pth). Finally, take the logarithm of both
L8248@P114: sides of the above to give
L8249@P114: log g = log α + β log L. (E5)
L8250@P114: We then plot log g as a function of log L and fit to a straight
L8251@P114: line to get α and β. Figure 10 illustrates the above fitting
L8252@P114: procedure for code-capacity noise (no measurement errors)
L8253@P114: and for eight rounds of single-shot error correction.

L8441@P116-118: 020340-35108Chapter 6
L8442@P118: Outlook and Open problems
L8443@P118: In this work, we have addressed some of the most fundamental issues in the design of a full-stack
L8444@P118: hypergraph-product-codes quantum computer. We believe that the study of hypergraph product codes
L8445@P118: could provide promising new approaches to the quantum error correction paradigm, particularly informing novel techniques that use to good advantage constant rate codes. We discussed the limitations
L8446@P118: – and henceforth possible directions – of our work in the relevant Sections but beyond those, there are
L8447@P118: a few open problems that are of particular interest to us.
L8448@P118: First and foremost, we lack a comprehensive framework for encoded operations that fully exploits
L8449@P118: the logical dimension of the code. Current proposals [1, 2] rely on auxiliary patches of planar code and
L8450@P118: we believe this is sub-optimal. Transversal gates seem to lose much of their appeal when the underlying
L8451@P118: code has more than one logical qubit. In fact, they naturally tend to act on the entire logical group of
L8452@P118: the code and it is still unknown how to address single logical qubits without supplementary gadgets.
L8453@P118: Possibly completely new paradigms are needed.
L8454@P118: A related, unexplored, problem is logical circuit compilation. The logical action of fault-tolerant
L8455@P118: gates on the encoded space will likely be tied to a generating set for the logical Pauli group. Once
L8456@P118: a logical basis is chosen, we expect different logical qubits to have inherently different properties in
L8457@P118: respect of minimum weight but also ease of implementation of some logical operations over others.
L8458@P118: Presumably, some but not all logical operations could be implemented in parallel. For all these reasons, ad hoc logical circuit compilation could be needed to make the most of the specific structure of
L8459@P118: the encoded space and the chosen universal set of fault-tolerant operations.
L8460@P118: Decoding: decoding is not a solved problem. Efficient decoding algorithms with linear running time are
L8461@P118: known in theory, but the hidden constants are often too big in practice. Bringing the computational
L8462@P118: cost of decoding down could be the turning point for usable quantum computers [3, 4, 5, 6]. The
L8463@P118: current approach is to use a cheap ‘pre-decoder’ to correct the most common and trivial errors before
L8464@P118: the syndrome information is sent to a general-purpose decoder. Such kind of approach has not been
L8465@P118: studied for codes with k > 1 and hypergraph product codes more specifically.
L8466@P118: Strictly related to the decoding problem is the design of fault-tolerant syndrome extraction circuits
L8467@P118: that are compatible with the current hardware [7, 8]. This will not only affect the overall performance
L8468@P118: of the computation but also shape and constrain the decoding problem that needs to be solved.
L8469@P118-119: 109On a more speculative level, we would like to further investigate the relationship between confinement, as defined in Chapter 5, and energy barrier. Stabilizer codes can be mapped into the degenerate
L8470@P119: ground state of a Hamiltonian where Pauli errors correspond to excited states. The energy barrier of
L8471@P119: a code is then the minimum energy cost to implement a non-trivial logical operator, namely moving
L8472@P119: from one ground state to an orthogonal one, via a sequence of single-qubit Pauli errors [9, 10, 11].
L8473@P119: Since confinement is a relation between the syndrome weight – roughly the energy cost – and the error
L8474@P119: weight, the two concepts are coupled. We know that a code with (macroscopic) confinement has a
L8475@P119: macroscopic energy barrier and we conjecture that the converse is also true. Our community agrees
L8476@P119: on the close link between confinement, energy barrier, single-shot error correction and self-correction.
L8477@P119: The exact nature of this link is still not fully understood.
L8478@P119: Can we build this bridge?
L8479@P119: A scalable quantum computer is one whose computational power can be increased on demand, but at
L8480@P119: the same time the resources used – manufacturing cost, physical space and energy usage – do not grow
L8481@P119: exponentially. To actually increase the computational power of a machine, qubits’ quality and the
L8482@P119: ability to perform gates between them have to be preserved when increasing the number of available
L8483@P119: qubits [12, 13, 14, 15]. A figure of merit for the quality of the qubits is their relaxation times expressed
L8484@P119: as exponential decay constants. The longitudinal relaxation time measures the exponential decay of the
L8485@P119: probability that a qubit initialised in the |1i state is found in the |0i state. The transverse relaxation
L8486@P119: time measures the decay of a qubit state in some superposition α |0i+β |1i. Equally important are the
L8487@P119: gate and measurement times, both in absolute terms and relative to the qubits’ relaxation time. Long
L8488@P119: qubit relaxation times as well as fast gates and measurements naturally allow for longer computations.
L8489@P119: In addition, we need gates and measurement error rates to be as low as possible. Last, as the number of
L8490@P119: qubits grows, the number of possible multiple-qubit entangling gates – e.g. CNOTs – between different
L8491@P119: qubits has to substantially grow too. In other words, we say that the number of connected qubits has
L8492@P119: to grow.
L8493@P119: A key issues in code design is indeed that the physical arrangement of qubits and consequent ability
L8494@P119: to perform gates has to be compatible with the chosen code family and unfortunately good qLDPC
L8495@P119: codes are not local in two-dimensions [9, 16]. Here by D-dimensional local quantum codes we mean a
L8496@P119: code family in which data qubits and auxilia qubits for stabilizer measurements have a layout in the
L8497@P119: D-dimensional real space such that each stabiliser’s support only contains neighbouring qubits in the
L8498@P119: layout. Bravyi, Poulin and Terhal [16] proved that a [[n, k, d]] code in D dimensions obeys kd
L8499@P119: 2
L8500@P119: D−1 ∼ n
L8501@P119: and Baspin and Krishna [17] further proved that long-range connectivity is indeed essential to surpass
L8502@P119: this trade-off between parameters. Delfosse, Beverland and Tremblay [18] have shown that, when we
L8503@P119: are limited to local circuits for syndrome measurement, the number of auxilia needed grows with the
L8504@P119: number of physical qubits – as n or n
L8505@P119: 2
L8506@P119: , depending on the depth of the circuits. In particular in 2-
L8507@P119: dimensions, when d ∼
L8508@P119: √
L8509@P119: n, the number of encoded logical qubits has to be constant – as for the planar
L8510@P119: code [16]. Hypergraph product codes circumnavigate the no-go result of Ref. [16] by dropping locality
L8511@P119: constraints; as per Refs. [18, 19], a scalable implementation on hardware would require going past a
L8512@P119: two-dimensional physical layout of qubits with nearest-neighbour interactions.
L8513@P119-120: To summarise, good codes need long-range connectivity but many current hardware implemen110tations – e.g. superconducting qubits – do not accommodate for this. Superconducting qubits are
L8514@P120: constrained in terms of connectivity and do not have long relaxation times, in the order of microseconds, but have fast gates, in the order of nanoseconds [13]. On the other hand, trapped ion and neutral
L8515@P120: atom qubits, for example, are quite versatile in terms of their connectivity, and have longer relaxation
L8516@P120: times, in the seconds range, but slow gates, in the order of microseconds [14, 15]. The interplay
L8517@P120: between the different requirements for the construction of a useful quantum computer is a complex
L8518@P120: technological challenge. As of today, we do not know which qubit architecture will be favoured but
L8519@P120: we are optimistic it will overcome the constraints of a planar qubit layout to allow diverse quantum
L8520@P120: codes – hypergraph product codes and beyond.
